This is an AI modernization of Elementary Lessons in Logic into contemporary English. The original is available from Internet Archive. Also available as an epub.

Editor's Introduction

William Stanley Jevons wrote this book in 1870 for Macmillan's series of elementary class-books, and for decades afterwards it was the logic that British and colonial schoolchildren, and a good many university students, learned first. It was reprinted again and again. Jevons revised it once, in 1876, the year he moved from Owens College in Manchester to University College London, and that revised text is the one retold here.

He was an unusual person to write a schoolbook on logic. He had left university at eighteen to spend five years as an assayer at the new mint in Sydney, came home to finish his degree, and made his name as an economist: The Coal Question (1865) asked what Britain would do when its coal ran out, and The Theory of Political Economy (1871) helped found modern economics. Logic was a second career pursued with the same energy. He had read George Boole's algebra of thought and decided it could be done without the algebra, and in 1869 he built a machine to do it, a wooden box with a keyboard of twenty-one keys that he showed to the Royal Society in January 1870. It sorted the possible combinations of four terms and struck out the ones the premises forbade, which is why it is sometimes counted among the ancestors of the computer. Lesson 23 teaches the same method by hand.

The book itself is the old logic of the schools, set out with unusual clarity: terms, propositions, the syllogism with its moods and figures and the Latin rhyme for remembering them, and the fallacies. Then, in its last third, it turns to induction, how scientists get from particular observations to general laws, and here Jevons is drawing on Mill, on Herschel and on his own work. One section has dated in an interesting way. The "recent logical views" of Lessons 22 and 23 set Sir William Hamilton beside Boole as rival reformers; Hamilton's system went nowhere, and Boole's became the foundation of the symbolic logic that eventually displaced most of this book. Jevons is left to say what he thought in 1876.

This edition changes his sentences and nothing else in the logic. His technical vocabulary is kept exactly, since it is the vocabulary every other book on traditional logic uses, and no modern notation has been added. The questions he printed at the back of the book now close the lessons they belong to, his index of terms has become a short glossary, and his references to his own pages now name lessons. Three examples have been reworded: a people he called by a name now taken as a slur is called by its own name, an exercise on the dignity of a Black man uses today's words for him, and a sentence offered only for its grammar, which happened to rank the races, has been replaced by another. Jevons gave no answers to his exercises, and none have been added.

On the cover. Drawn for this edition, in the manner of the Dover science paperbacks of the 1950s and 60s. It is the book's first argument, from Lesson 1, drawn as Euler's circles, the figures Jevons uses from Lesson 9 on: iron inside the class of metals, the metals inside the class of elements, and so iron inside the elements. Part of a part is part of the whole.

Preface

In writing these Lessons I have tried to show that logic, even in its traditional form, can be made a really useful subject of study and a powerful way of exercising the mind. With that aim I have avoided unnecessary technical terms, and I have kept out of questions that are purely speculative or metaphysical. Books on logic too often use childish illustrations. In their place I have generally put examples drawn from the clear-cut objects and ideas of the natural and experimental sciences. In this and in other ways I have tried to make these Lessons a fitting companion to a series of science schoolbooks.

Logic is not only an exact science; it is the simplest and most elementary of all the sciences, and so it certainly ought to have some place in every course of education. The relations between propositions and the forms of argument make as precise a subject to teach, and as vigorous an exercise for the mind, as the properties of geometrical figures or the rules of algebra. Yet every schoolboy is made to learn mathematical problems he will never use in later life, while he is left completely ignorant of the simple principles and forms of reasoning that will come into his thoughts every hour of the day. Logic should no longer be thought of as an elegant, learned accomplishment. It should take its place as an essential study for every well-informed person. I hope these Lessons will bring to the science many people who have neither the time nor the inclination to read longer and more detailed treatises, and many who would not be drawn in by the numerous, but rather dry and brief, summaries published in past years.

Lessons in logic ought to be the basis of plenty of exercises, and for that purpose I have supplied a large number of questions and examples at the end of the book, some of them taken from the examination papers of the universities of Oxford, London and Edinburgh. In my own classes I have always found that working out logical questions, examining arguments and spotting fallacies is just as practical and useful an exercise of the mind as doing calculations and solving problems in a mathematics class.

Except in a few places, where I say so, I have not put forward any views that teachers of logic do not commonly accept. Throughout, I have spent more effort on describing clearly and simply the doctrines that logicians generally agree on than on discussing the points where opinions differ. The recent logical discoveries of Sir William Hamilton, Mr. George Bentham, Professor De Morgan and, above all, the late Professor Boole cannot yet be fully adopted in an elementary book, but I have tried to give a clear idea of the results they are bound to lead to.

In the later Lessons, which deal with induction, I have generally followed Sir John Herschel, Dr. Whewell and Mr. J. S. Mill, as the recognized authorities on the subject. In fact, these Lessons can be treated as an easy introduction to some of the most important parts of Mr. Mill's treatise on logic.

At the end of almost every Lesson the student will find references to the books in which he can most profitably go on reading about the subject of that Lesson, so that this little volume can serve as a guide to a wider course of study.

Hampstead, November, 1876.

Introduction

Lesson 1: Definition and Sphere of the Science

The shortest definition of logic is that it is the Science of Reasoning. More often it is called the Science of the Laws of Thought, and some logicians like to be more exact still and call it the Science of the Formal, or Necessary, Laws of Thought. Before definitions like these can do us any good, we need to be clear about what the words in them mean. Once we are, we will probably find that there is not much difference between them.

By a Law of Thought we mean a certain uniformity: a way in which everyone thinks and reasons alike, and must think alike, as long as they don't make what we call mistakes, or contradict themselves, or fall into fallacies. The laws of thought are natural laws. We have no power to interfere with them, and they must never be confused with the man-made laws of a country, which people invent and can change. Every science is busy finding and describing the natural laws that the things it studies obey without exception. Astronomy, for example, studies the uniform way in which the heavenly bodies, and in fact all material things, tend to fall toward one another, the way a stone falls toward the earth, or to move around one another under the pull of that tendency. The universal law of gravitation is the natural law, or uniformity, that physical astronomy deals with.

In chemistry, the law of equivalent proportions describes the well-established fact that each chemical substance combines with every other only in certain fixed proportions. Exactly eight parts by weight of oxygen combine with one part of hydrogen to make water, for instance, and sixteen parts of oxygen combine with six parts of carbon to make carbonic acid gas (carbon dioxide) whenever an ordinary flame or fire burns. Wherever we can find uniformities or similarities, we are to that extent creating science and arriving at natural laws. But there may be, and there are, many things so changeable, so complicated and so uncertain that we can never be sure we have found laws they will always obey, and in those cases no science, in the proper sense of the word, is possible. There is no real science of human character, for example, because the human mind is too variable and too complicated to study that way. No two people are so alike that you can be sure one will act in every situation exactly as the other would. So it is impossible to sort people into classes in such a way that everyone in the same class will act the same way in any given situation.

But there is a science of human reason, or thought, separate from all the other workings of the mind that make up a person's character. That is because there are ways in which everyone does think and reason alike, and must. For example: if two things are identical with a third thing, they are identical with each other. This is a law of thought, and a very simple and obvious one, and we can notice two things about it:

1. Everyone thinks in agreement with it, and agrees that they do as soon as they understand what it means.

2. They think in agreement with it no matter what subject they are thinking about.

Suppose the things we are considering are these:

	London,
	The capital,
	The most populous city in Great Britain.

Since "the capital is identical with London," and "London is identical with the most populous city in Great Britain," it follows necessarily, in every mind, that "the capital is identical with the most populous city in Great Britain."

Or compare these three things:

	Iron,
	The most useful metal,
	The cheapest metal.

If we grant that "the most useful metal is iron" and "iron is the cheapest metal," it follows necessarily, in every mind, that "the most useful metal is the cheapest." These are two examples of the general truth that things identical with the same thing are identical with each other. We can call this a general, or necessary, form of thought and reasoning.

Now compare these three:

	The earth,
	Planets,
	Bodies revolving in elliptical orbits.

This time we can't say, as before, that "the earth is identical with the planets." It is identical with only one of the planets, so we say that "it is a planet." In the same way we can say that "the planets are bodies revolving in elliptical orbits," but they are only some of all the bodies that revolve that way. Even so, it follows that if the earth is among the planets, and the planets are among the bodies revolving in elliptical orbits, then the earth is among those bodies too.

The most elementary chemistry lets us argue the same way about these:

	Iron,
	Metals,
	Elementary substances.

Iron is one of the metals, and the metals are elements, that is, simple substances that cannot be broken down any further. The metals are among the elements, part of them, not the whole of them. It follows necessarily that "iron is one of the elementary substances." So we now have two examples of a fixed and necessary form of thought, one that holds true whatever things we apply it to. This form of argument can be put in several ways, and we will look at it closely in the lessons on the syllogism. One way to put it is: "part of a part is part of the whole." Iron is part of the class of metals, which is part of the class of elements, so iron is part of the class of elements.

So if I now offer another definition of logic, and say it is "the science of the necessary forms of thought," I hope the reader will see clearly what "necessary forms of thought" means. A form is something that can stay the same while the matter poured into it changes. Medals struck from the same dies have exactly the same form, but they can be made of different matter: bronze, copper, gold or silver. A building of exactly the same form could be built of stone or of brick. Furniture of exactly the same shape can be made of oak, mahogany, walnut and so on. We recognize the difference between form and substance in everyday objects without thinking about it, and in logic we can see in just the same way that the form of an argument is one thing, and the subject or matter that the argument happens to be about is quite another. We can almost show the form of our last two arguments to the eye, like this:

If we put three names inside the three pairs of brackets, marked X, Y and Z, so that the name in X comes under the name in Y, and the name in Y comes under the name in Z, then it follows necessarily that the first (X) comes under the last (Z).

Logic, then, is the science that works out and describes all the general forms of thought we must use as long as we reason validly. These forms are very many, though the principles they are built on are few and simple. This makes logic the most general of all the sciences. We need its help more often than the help of any other science, because each particular science deals with only some of the things that exist, and the sciences grow into very different and often unconnected branches of knowledge. Logic, though, deals with the principles and forms of thought that must be used in every branch of knowledge. It deals with the very origin and foundations of knowledge itself. It is true that the logical method used in one science may differ a little from the method used in another. But whatever its particular form, the method must be logical, and it must obey the laws of thought. In short, there is something all the sciences must have in common, something they must all obey as long as they hold to what is true and consistent, and the work of logic is to explain this common basis of all science.

One name that has been given to logic, the Science of Sciences, describes very well how far the reach of logical principles extends. People who work in the particular branches of knowledge seem to have known perfectly well that they owe allegiance to the highest of the sciences, because the names they have usually chosen say so. The word "logic" itself turns up in almost all the names recently adopted for the sciences. These are often casually called the "ologies," but they are really the "logics," since the "o" is only a connecting vowel or the end of the word before it. So geology is logic applied to explaining how the earth's crust was formed; biology is logic applied to the facts of life; psychology is logic applied to the nature of the mind; and the same goes for physiology, entomology, zoology, teratology (the study of abnormal growths), morphology, anthropology, theology, ecclesiology (the study of churches), thalattology (the study of the sea) and the rest. Each of these sciences openly admits, in its own name, that it is a special kind of logic. The name "logic" comes from the common Greek word λόγος (logos), which usually means a word, the sign and outward expression of a thought inside. But the same word was also used for the inward thought or reasoning that words express, and that is probably how later Greek writers on reasoning came to call their science ἐπιστήμη λογική, "logical science," and also τέχνη λογική, "logical art." The adjective λογική, used on its own, soon became the name of the science, in the same way that mathematics, rhetoric and other names ending in "-ic" started out as adjectives and were turned into nouns.

Footnote: Except philology, which is formed differently and means the love or study of words. If its name had been formed on the same plan, the science would be called "logology."

A good deal of rather trivial argument has gone into the question of whether logic should be thought of as a science only, an art only, or both at once. Sir William Hamilton even took the trouble to sort nearly every writer on logic according to which opinion they held. But it seems correct, and enough, to say this: logic is a science insofar as it simply investigates the necessary principles and forms of thought, and so teaches us what correct thinking consists of. It becomes an art when it sets about making rules to help people catch false reasoning. A science teaches us to know, and an art teaches us to do, and all the more advanced sciences lead to useful arts that go with them. Astronomy is the foundation of the art of navigating the ocean, and of arranging the calendar and keeping track of dates. Physiology is the basis of the art of medicine, and chemistry is the basis of many useful arts. In the same way, logic has been thought of as the basis of an art of correct reasoning, or investigation, which ought to teach the true method to be followed in every science. The famous British logician Duns Scotus, who lived in the thirteenth century, called logic the Science of Sciences, and he also called it the Art of Arts, which says everything about its preeminence. Others have defined it like this: "Logic is the art of directing the reason aright in acquiring the knowledge of things, for the instruction both of ourselves and others." Dr. Isaac Watts took this view of logic and called his well-known book "the Art of Thinking."

Still, it is fair to say that logic is more a science than an art, and here is the reason. Everyone picks up the ability and the habit of reasoning long before they have even heard the word "logic." They pick it up by the natural use of their minds, or by constantly and unconsciously copying other people. So in all very simple cases they follow the principles of the science correctly, but without knowing it. Yet the contradictory opinions and absurd fallacies put forward by people with no training show that this unaided use of the mind can't be trusted once the subject under discussion gets at all difficult or complicated. So studying logic cannot be useless. It explains the principles on which everyone has often reasoned correctly already, and it also points out the dangers of arguing wrongly. The reasoner becomes a correct reasoner on purpose, and learns to avoid the traps of fallacy on purpose. To say that people can reason well without the science of logic is about as true as saying they can stay healthy without medicine. So they can, as long as they are healthy; and reasoners can do without the science of reasoning, as long as they do reason correctly. But how many people can? A man might as well claim his body is immortal as claim his mind is infallible.

And if it is worth saying a few words in defense of logic as an art, because of misunderstandings that have arisen in the history of the subject, does anything need to be said in praise of it as a science? Everything great in science, in art or in literature is the work of the intellect. In the form of our bodies we are related to the animals, and in the part of us that perishes we are nothing but matter. What raises human beings above everything else on earth is a conscious intellect, the power of reasoning with general ideas. Who can say, then, that the nature and workings of this intellect are not very nearly the highest and most interesting subject we could ever study? No one could seriously deny the truth of Sir William Hamilton's favorite saying:

	In the world there is nothing great but man.
	In man there is nothing great but mind.

Questions and Exercises

1. What do we mean by a Law of Nature, and by a Law of Thought?

2. Explain the difference between the Form of Thought and the Matter of Thought.

3. In what sense can logic be called the Science of Sciences?

4. Where does the name "logic" come from?

5. How does a science differ from an art, and why is logic more a science than an art?

6. Can we say that logic is a necessary aid to correct reasoning, when people who have never studied logic reason correctly?

Lesson 2: The Three Parts of Logical Doctrine

We saw in the last lesson that logic is the Science of Reasoning, or the Science of the Necessary Laws of Thought, the laws we must follow if we are to argue consistently with ourselves and avoid contradicting ourselves. So argument, or reasoning, is strictly speaking the proper subject in front of us. But the most convenient and usual way to study logic is to look first at the parts that any argument must be made of. An architect has to know the materials of a building, and a mechanic the materials of a machine, before either can claim to understand how the thing is built. In the same way, it makes sense to describe the materials and instruments we work with in reasoning before we go on to the actual forms of argument.

Take a simple argument like the one in the last lesson:

	Iron is a metal,
	Every metal is an element,
	Therefore Iron is an element,—

We see that it is made up of three statements, or assertions. Each of them contains, besides some minor words, two nouns, or names of things, and the verb "is." In short, two names, or terms, joined by a verb make up an assertion, or proposition; and three such propositions make up an argument, which in this case is called a syllogism. So it is natural and convenient to describe terms first, since they are the simplest parts. Next we go on to the nature and the different kinds of propositions that are built out of terms. Then we will be ready to deal with the syllogism as a whole. These, then, are the three parts of logical doctrine.

We can say that the three parts of logic deal with terms, propositions and syllogisms. But it is just as true, or truer, to say that our real subject is the acts of mind that these forms of language point to. Logicians' opinions on this point have varied, or perhaps it is only the way they express themselves that has varied. Archbishop Whately says plainly that logic is entirely about language. Sir William Hamilton, Mr. Mansel and most other logicians treat it as being about the acts or states of mind that the words point to. Mr. J. S. Mill goes back to the things themselves that we are arguing about. So is the subject of logic language, thought, or objects? The simplest and truest answer is that in a certain sense it deals with all three. No process of reasoning can be explained or passed on to another person without words, so in practice we are limited to reasoning that has been put into the form of language. That means we will always be dealing with words, but only insofar as they are the instruments for recording and referring to our thoughts. The grammarian deals with language too, but he treats it simply as language, and his science stops once it has described and explained the forms, kinds and relations of words. Logic also deals with language, but only as the necessary sign of what the mind is doing.

Again, as long as we think correctly, we must think of things as they are. The state of mind inside us must match the state of things outside us whenever we get a chance to compare them. It is impossible, and we cannot even conceive, that iron should turn out not to be an elementary substance, if it is a metal and every metal is an element. We cannot suppose, and there is no reason to suppose, that the way our minds are built forces us to think of things differently from the way they are. If, then, we can assume that things really agree or differ just as correct logical thought leads us to believe they will, the views of the logicians I named do not seem impossible to reconcile. We deal with things insofar as they are the objects of thought, and we deal with language insofar as it is the embodiment of thought. If the reader keeps this explanation in mind, he will be spared some confusion later, when he reads different books on logic and finds that they vary enormously in their way of treating the subject, or at least in their way of putting it.

If logic, once it is put into language, has three parts—terms, propositions and syllogisms—then there must be just as many different kinds of thought, or operations of the mind. They are usually called:

	1. Simple apprehension.
	2. Judgment.
	3. Reasoning or discourse.

The first of these, Simple Apprehension, is the act of mind by which we simply become aware of something, or have a notion, idea or impression of it brought into the mind. The adjective "simple" means apart from other things, and "apprehension" means the mind's taking hold of something. So the name, or term, Iron instantly makes the mind think of a strong and very useful metal, but it does not tell us anything about it or compare it with anything else. The words sun, Jupiter, Sirius and St. Paul's Cathedral are terms too. They call up in the mind certain well-known objects, which stay in our memory even when they are not in front of our senses. In fact, a term like these is used simply as a stand-in for showing the actual things it names.

Judgment is a different action of the mind. It consists in comparing two notions or ideas of objects, which we got by simple apprehension, to find out whether they agree or differ. Clearly, then, we cannot judge or compare unless we are aware of two things, or have the notions of two things in the mind, at the same time. Suppose I compare Jupiter and Sirius. First I simply apprehend each of them. But when I bring them into comparison, I notice that they agree in being small, bright, shining bodies that rise and set and move around the sky at what looks like the same speed. On close examination, though, I notice that Sirius gives a twinkling, flickering light, while Jupiter shines steadily. Watching for longer shows that Jupiter and Sirius do not really move at the same steady speed: Jupiter shifts its position in the sky from night to night in a rather complicated way. If I widen the comparison to other heavenly bodies that I can apprehend, or see, at the same time, I will find a multitude of stars that agree with Sirius in giving a twinkling light and in staying perfectly fixed in position relative to one another. Meanwhile two or three other bodies can be seen that resemble Jupiter in giving a steady light and also in changing their place from night to night among the fixed stars. Through the action of judgment I have now formed in my mind the general notion of fixed stars, by mentally grouping together a number of objects that agree; and out of several other objects I have formed the general notion of planets. When I compare these two general notions, I find that they do not have the same qualities or appearances, and I state this in the proposition, "Planets are not fixed stars."

I have used the expression "General Notion" as if the reader already knew it well. But although philosophers have been using the expressions general notion, idea, conception, concept and so on for more than two thousand years, they have never managed to agree exactly on what the terms mean. One group of philosophers, called Nominalists, say it is all a matter of names. When we put together Jupiter, Mars, Saturn, Venus and the rest and call them planets, the common name is what binds them together in our minds. Others, called Realists, have claimed that besides these particular planets there really is something that combines the properties they all share, without any of the differences of size, color or motion that set them apart. Today, however, everyone agrees that nothing can physically exist that corresponds to a general notion. Anything that exists must be here or there, of this size or that size, so it would be one particular planet and not any planet whatever. The Nominalists seem just as wrong, though, because language, if it is to be of any use, must stand for something, and must correspond, as we have seen, to acts of mind. So if proper names raise in our minds the images of particular things, like the sun or Jupiter, general names should raise general notions.

The true view seems to be that of the philosophers called Conceptualists. They say that the general notion is the mind's knowledge of the common properties, or resemblances, of the things the notion takes in. So the notion planet really means the awareness, in anyone's mind, that there are certain heavenly bodies which agree in giving a steady light and in moving around the sky differently from the fixed stars. I should add, however, that many people, including Sir William Hamilton, would be counted as Nominalists and yet hold that the general name carries with it an awareness of the resemblance between the things it denotes. It is not easy to draw a precise line between this form of the doctrine and conceptualism, and the subject is too much a matter of debate to follow any further in this book.

It will become clear over these lessons that the whole of logic, and the whole of any science, consists in arranging the individual things we meet into general notions, or classes, and giving them suitable general names, or terms, so that our knowledge of them can be made as simple and as general as possible. Every properly formed general notion allows us to state general laws, or truths, about it. Of the planets, for example, we can affirm that they move in elliptical orbits around the sun from west to east, that they shine with light reflected from the sun, and so on. Of the fixed stars we can affirm that they shine with their own light, that they are incomparably farther away than the planets, and so on. All reasoning, we will find, comes from this faculty of judgment, which lets us discover and affirm that a large number of objects have similar properties, so that whatever is known of some of them can be inferred and asserted of the others.

It is in applying knowledge like this that we use the third act of mind, called discourse or reasoning. By it, starting from certain judgments, we are able to form a new judgment without going back to the real objects at all. If we know that iron comes under the general notion of metal, and that this notion comes under the still wider notion of element, then without examining iron any further we know that it is a simple substance that cannot be broken down, the kind chemists call an element. Or suppose we learn from one source that Neptune is a planet, and from another that planets move in elliptical orbits. We can join these two pieces of knowledge together in the mind and draw out the truth that Neptune moves in an elliptical orbit.

Reasoning, or Discourse, can therefore be defined as the progress of the mind from one or more given propositions to a proposition different from those given. The propositions we argue from are called Premises, and the one we draw from them is called the Conclusion. The conclusion is said to follow from the premises, or to be concluded, inferred or collected from them. The premises get their name because they are put forward, or put at the beginning (Latin præ, before, and mitto, I send or put). The heart of the process is gathering up the truth that the premises contain when they are joined together, and carrying it with us into the conclusion, where it takes shape in a new proposition, or assertion. We extract from the premises all the information that is useful for the purpose in hand—and that is all that reasoning does.

I have now pointed out the three parts of logical doctrine—Terms, Propositions, and Reasoning or Syllogism—into which the subject is conveniently divided, and we will go on to look at each of them. It is worth mentioning, though, that a fourth part has often been added, called Method, which deals with the arrangement of the parts of any composition.

It is sometimes said that method is to the syllogism what the syllogism is to the proposition, and what the proposition is to the term, and that a fourth division is needed to complete the doctrine of logic. In any case, this fourth part is certainly much less important and less clearly marked off than the other three, and everything that will be said about it is in Lesson 24.

Questions and Exercises

1. Name the parts that a syllogism is made of.

2. How far is it correct to say that logic is concerned with language?

3. What are the three acts of mind considered in logic? Which of them is more especially the subject of the science?

4. Can you state exactly what is meant by a general notion, idea, or conception?

5. How do the Nominalists, Realists, and Conceptualists differ in their opinions about the nature of a general notion?

6. What is the supposed fourth part of logic?

Terms

Lesson 3: Terms, and Their Various Kinds

In the last lesson we saw that every assertion or statement expresses the agreement or difference of two things, or of two general notions. So when we put an assertion into words, we need two kinds of words. We need words that draw the mind's attention to the things being compared, and we need words that give the result of the comparison, that is, whether the things agree or differ. The words that point out the things, or classes of things, are called Terms. The words that express the comparison are said to form the Copula. A complete assertion or statement therefore consists of two terms and a copula, and when it is put this way it forms a Proposition. Take the proposition "Dictionaries are useful books." The two terms are dictionaries and useful books. The copula is the verb are, and it expresses a certain agreement between the class of dictionaries and the class of useful books: the class of dictionaries forms part of the class of useful books. Here each term is only one or two words long, but a term can need any number of words to describe the notions or classes being compared. In the proposition "the angles at the base of an isosceles triangle are equal to each other," the first term takes nine words, and the second term takes four (equal to each other). There is no limit to the number of words that can go into a term.

A term gets its name because it forms one end (Latin terminus, an end or boundary) of a proposition, and strictly speaking it is a term only as long as it stands in a proposition. But we commonly use "term" or "name" to mean any noun, substantive or adjective, or any group of words that denotes an object of thought. As we will soon see, that object may be a single thing, a group of things, a quality of things, or a group of qualities. No one has defined a name or term better than Hobbes: "A name is a word taken at pleasure to serve for a mark, which may raise in our mind a thought like to some thought which we had before, and which, being pronounced to others, may be to them a sign of what thought the speaker had before in his mind."

Every term or name is made of words, but not every word can make a name by itself. We can't properly say "Not is agreeable" or "Probably is not true." Nothing can be asserted about a preposition, an adverb or certain other parts of speech, except that they are prepositions, adverbs and so on. Only a noun substantive, or a group of words used as a noun substantive, can form the subject, or first term, of a proposition. Only a noun substantive, an adjective, something equivalent to an adjective, or a verb can form the second term, or predicate. It is even open to question whether an adjective can ever form a term on its own. In "Dictionaries are useful," it may be said that the substantive things or books is understood in the predicate, so that the complete sentence is "Dictionaries are useful books." But since this is a disputed point, we will assume that words divide into two kinds, as follows.

Words that stand, or seem to stand, alone as complete terms, namely the substantive, the adjective and certain parts of a verb, are called categorematic words, from the Greek word κατηγορέω (katēgoreō), to assert or predicate.

The other parts of speech, such as prepositions, adverbs and conjunctions, can only form parts of names or terms. These are called syncategorematic words, because they have to be used with other words to make up terms (Greek σύν, syn, with, and κατηγορέω). We need not pay any more attention to syncategorematic words, except where they form part of categorematic terms.

Next we need to look at the different kinds of terms and their peculiarities, so that we get a clear idea of what they mean. The first division of terms is into singular or individual terms, and general or common terms. It is a very obvious division, but an important one. A Singular term is one that can denote only a single object, at least as long as it is used in exactly the same meaning. The Emperor of the French, the Atlantic Ocean, St Paul's, William Shakespeare and the most precious of the metals are all singular terms. All proper names belong to this class. John Jones is the name of many men, it is true, but it is not used to mean any one of those men you like. It is used to mean some single man. In short, it has a different meaning in each case, just as London, the name of our capital, has no connection in meaning with London in Canada.

General terms, by contrast, apply in the same sense, and equally well, to any one of an indefinite number of objects that resemble each other in certain qualities. Metal is a general name, for example, because it can be applied without preference to gold, silver, copper, tin, aluminum, or any of about fifty known substances. It is no more the name of one of these than of any other. In fact it is applied to any substance that has a metallic luster, cannot be decomposed, and has certain other qualities that chemists easily recognize. Nor is the number of substances in the class fixed: as new kinds of metal are discovered from time to time, they are added to it. Again, Mars, Jupiter, Saturn and the rest are singular terms, since each can denote only one planet. But the term planet is a general one, because it applies to as many bodies as may be found to revolve around the sun as the earth does.

We must be careful not to confuse general terms with collective terms. A collective term is the name of a number of things all joined together as one whole, like the soldiers of a regiment, the men of a jury, or the crew of a ship. A collective term is the name of all of them, but not of each. A general term, on the other hand, is the name of a number of things, but of each of them separately, or, to use the technical expression, distributively. Soldier, juryman and sailor are general names that may belong to John Jones, Thomas Brown and so on. But we can't say that John Jones is a regiment, or Thomas Brown a jury. Pointed out like this, the distinction is extremely obvious. But it can turn up in less obvious forms, and then it is likely to lead to faulty reasoning, as we will see in Lesson 20. Notice that we cannot divide terms into those that are general and those that are collective, because the same term will often be both, depending on how we look at it. Library is collective with respect to the books in it, but general with respect to the great number of different libraries, private or public, that exist. Regiment is a collective term with respect to the soldiers who make it up, but general with respect to the hundred different regiments, the Coldstream Guards, the Highland regiment, the Welsh Fusiliers and the rest, that make up the British standing army. Army, in its turn, is a collective whole, because it is made of a number of regiments organized together. Year is collective with respect to the months, weeks or days it consists of, but general because it is the name of 1869, or of 1870, or of any period marked by one revolution of the earth around the sun.

English does not always give us a convenient way to tell the general use of a term from its collective use. Latin expressed the difference exactly: omnes meant all distributively, and cuncti meant all taken together (it is a shortened form of conjuncti, joined together). In English, all men may mean any man, or all men together. Even the more exact word every is sometimes misused, as in the old proverb, "Every little makes a mickle" (a mickle is a large amount). Plainly, no little portion can make much all by itself. It can only do so when joined to other little portions.

A second important distinction is between concrete terms and abstract terms. It can't be described better than in the words of Mr. Mill: a concrete name is the name of a thing, and an abstract name is the name of a quality, attribute or circumstance of a thing. So red house is the name of a thing that physically exists, and it is concrete. Redness is the name of one quality of the house, and it is abstract. The word abstract means drawn from (Latin abstractus, from abstrahere, to draw away from). It tells us that the quality redness is thought of in the mind apart from all the other qualities that belong to the red house, or to any other red object. But although we can think of a quality by itself, we cannot suppose that the quality can exist physically apart from the matter in which it shows itself to us. Redness means either a notion in the mind, or whatever it is in red objects that excites that notion.

The reader should notice carefully that adjectives are concrete, not abstract. If we say that a book is useful, it is the book we apply the adjective useful to, and usefulness is the abstract noun that denotes the quality. In the same way, the adjectives equal, grateful, reverent and rational are names of things, and the abstract nouns that go with them are equality, gratitude, reverence and rationality. This distinction will become clearer when you read Lesson 5.

It is good practice to try to find pairs of matching concrete and abstract names. Animal has animality; miser, miserliness; old, agedness or old age; substance, substantiality; soap, soapiness; shrub, shrubbiness; and so on. But it does not follow at all that there is an abstract word for every concrete one. Table hardly has an abstract tabularity, and although ink has inkiness, we would not find an abstract for pen. Whether we have an abstract name or not depends on the accidents of the history of language, and as time and science move on, people keep inventing new abstract words.

Unfortunately, concrete and abstract names are often confused, and it is far from easy to keep their meanings apart. Relation, properly speaking, is the abstract name for the position two people or things stand in toward each other, and the people themselves are properly called relatives (Latin relativus, one who is related). But nowadays we constantly speak of relations, meaning the people themselves, and when we want to point to the abstract relation between them we have to invent a new abstract name, relationship. Nation has long been a concrete term, though judging by its form it was probably abstract at first. Yet the misuse of language has gone so far, especially in newspaper writing, that we hear of a nationality meaning a nation. Of course, if nation is the concrete, then nationality ought to be the abstract, meaning the quality of being a nation. In the same way, action, intention, extension, conception and a great many other names that are properly abstract are used loosely for the matching concrete words, namely act, intent, extent, concept and so on. Production is properly the condition or state of someone who is producing, or drawing something forth. But it has become confused with the thing produced, so that we constantly talk about the productions of a country, meaning its products. The logical terms Proposition, Deduction, Induction and Syllogism are all properly abstract words, yet they are used concretely for a Proposition, a Deduction, an Induction, a Syllogism. And it has to be admitted that logicians are nearly as bad as everyone else at confusing abstract and concrete terms. This misuse does a lot of harm to the language.

Another very obvious division of terms is between positive terms and negative terms. The usual way of describing the difference is to say that positive terms signify that a quality exists or is possessed, as in grateful, metallic and organic, while the matching negatives signify that the same qualities are absent, as in ungrateful, non-metallic and inorganic. Negative terms may be adjectives, like these, or substantives, either concrete or abstract. Ingratitude, inequality and inconvenience are abstract negative terms; individuals, unequals and so on are concrete negatives. We usually treat as negative any term with a negative prefix such as not, non, un or in. But a great many terms work as negatives without carrying any mark of it. Darkness is the negative of light or lightness, since it means the absence of light. Compound is the negative of element, since we give the name compound to whatever can be decomposed, and an element is what cannot be decomposed. In theory, every term has a matching negative, but it does not follow at all that the language supplies that term ready-made. Table has the matching adjective tabular, but there is no negative untabular to go with it. One person may be called a bookworm, but there is no negative for people who are not bookworms, because no one has felt the need for the expression. New negative terms are being invented all the time, perhaps faster than is good for us. When an idea is not mentioned often, it is better to express it with a phrase than to lengthen the dictionary with a newly made word.

In many cases, it seems, a negative term implies that some definite quality or fact is present. Inconvenience no doubt implies the absence of convenience, but it also implies the presence of real trouble or pain caused by that absence. Unhappiness is a negative term, but exactly the same notion is expressed by the positive term misery. The negative of healthy is unhealthy, but the positive term sickly does just as well. So whether a positive or a negative term is used for a particular notion seems to be more a matter of accident than anything else. All we can really say is that every positive term necessarily implies the possibility of a matching negative term, which is the name of all the things the positive name cannot be applied to. Whether that negative term has actually been invented is an accident of language; in logic we may assume it exists.

The reader should be warned not to assume that every term that looks negative because it has a negative prefix really is negative. The participle unloosed certainly looks like the negative of loosed, but the two words mean exactly the same thing: the prefix un is not really a negative here. Invaluable, again, does not mean having no value. It means so valuable that the value cannot be measured. And a shameless action can just as well be called by the positive term, a shameful action. No doubt other examples could be found.

Take great care not to confuse terms that express the presence or absence of a quality with terms that describe its degree. Less is not the negative of greater, because there is a third possibility, equal. The true negative of greater is not-greater, and that amounts to either equal or less. In the same way, we may say that disagreeable is not the simple negative of agreeable, because some things may be neither one nor the other, but simply indifferent to us. It would not be easy to say offhand whether every action that is not honest is dishonest, or whether there may be actions of an in-between character. The rule is this. Wherever the question is one of degree or quantity, a middle ground is possible, and the subject belongs to the science of quantity rather than to simple logic. Where the question is whether a quality is present or absent, there cannot be more than two alternatives, according to one of the Primary Laws of Thought, which we will look at in Lesson 14. In the case of quantity, we may call the extreme terms opposites: less is the opposite of greater, and disagreeable of agreeable. In the case of simple negation, we may call the terms negatives or contradictories. From a logical point of view, it makes no difference which of a pair of contradictory terms we treat as the positive and which as the negative. Each is the negative of the other.

Logicians have set apart from simple negative terms a class of terms called privative, such as blind, dead and so on. Such terms express that a thing has been deprived of a quality that it had before, or could have had, or usually does have. A man may be born blind, so that he never did see; but he has the organs that would have let him see if not for some accident. A stone or a tree could not have had the faculty of sight under any circumstances. No mineral substance can properly be said to die or to be dead, because it was never capable of life. But it may be called uncrystallized, because it could have been in the form of a crystal. So we apply a privative term to anything that lacks a quality it was capable of having, and a negative term to anything that lacks a quality and could not have had it. It is doubtful, though, whether this distinction can be properly carried through, and it does not matter very much.

It is also usual to divide terms into relative and absolute, that is, non-relative. The adjective absolute means whatever is "loosed from connection with anything else" (Latin ab, from, and solutus, loosed). Relative means whatever is carried, in thought at least, into connection with something else. So a relative term denotes an object that cannot be thought of without reference to some other object, or as part of a larger whole. A father cannot be thought of except in relation to a child, a monarch except in relation to a subject, a shepherd except in relation to a flock. So father, monarch and shepherd are relative terms, while child, subject and flock are their correlatives (Latin con, with, and relativus): the objects that are necessarily joined in thought with the first ones. The very meaning of father, in fact, is that he has a child; of monarch, that he has subjects; and of shepherd, that he has a flock. As examples of terms with no obvious relation to anything else, I might mention water, gas and tree. Nothing seems to me so habitually connected with water that we must think of it as part of the same idea. Gas, tree and a great many other terms also denote objects that have no notable or permanent relations of the kind that would earn the terms the name of relatives. So they may be considered absolute, or non-relative, terms.

The truth, though, is that everything really must have relations to something else: water to the elements it is made of, gas to the coal it is manufactured from, the tree to the soil it is rooted in. And by the very laws of thought, we cannot think of any thing, or class of things, except by separating it from the other existing things it differs from. I cannot use the term mortal without immediately splitting all existing or conceivable things into two groups, mortal and immortal. Metal, element, organic substance and every other term one could mention would necessarily imply that a matching negative term exists: non-metallic, compound, inorganic substance. In this respect, then, every term is certainly relative. Logicians, however, have been content to treat as relative terms only those that imply some special and striking kind of relation, arising from position in time or space, from the connection of cause and effect, and so on. It is in this special sense that the student should use the distinction.

Now that the most important kinds of terms have been explained, the reader should get thoroughly familiar with them by working through the exercises at the end of this lesson. For each of the terms given there, the reader is to decide:

1. Whether it is a categorematic or syncategorematic term.

2. Whether it is a general or a singular term.

3. Whether it is collective or distributive.

4. Whether it is concrete or abstract.

5. Whether it is positive, or negative, or privative.

6. Whether it is relative or absolute.

The next lesson will show in full that most terms have more than one meaning. One meaning may be general and another singular, one concrete and another abstract, and so on. So it is absolutely necessary for the reader first of all to choose one exact meaning of the term being examined. And in answering the questions, it is a good idea to state which way you are taking it. Take the word sovereign. We may first pick the meaning in which it is equivalent to monarch. In this meaning it is a general term, insofar as it is the name of any one of many monarchs, living or dead. But it is singular with respect to the people of any one country. It is clearly categorematic, concrete and positive, and obviously relative to the monarch's subjects.

Read Mr. Mill's chapter on Names, System of Logic, Book I. chap. 2.

Questions and Exercises

1. Define a name or term.

2. What is a categorematic term?

3. Explain the distinction between a collective and a general term.

4. Distinguish the collective and the distributive use of the word all in the following:

(1) Non omnis moriar (that is, I shall not all die).

(2) "All men find their own in all men's good, And all men join in noble brotherhood." Tennyson.

(3) Non omnia possumus omnes (that is, we cannot all do all things).

5. Which of the following are abstract terms? Act, ingratitude, home, hourly, homeliness, introduction, individuality, truth, true, trueness, yellow, yellowness, childhood, book, blue, intention, reason, rationality, reasonableness.

6. Define a negative term, and give the mark by which you can recognize one.

7. Distinguish a privative term from a negative term, and find some examples of privative terms.

8. Describe the logical character of each of the following terms, taking the precautions given earlier in this lesson.

Lesson 4: Of the Ambiguity of Terms

No part of logic is more genuinely useful than the part that deals with the ambiguity of terms: the uncertainty, and the variety, of the meanings that words carry. Nothing matters more for good habits of thinking and reasoning than a thorough knowledge of how imperfect language is. Comparatively few terms have one clear meaning and one meaning only. And whenever two or more meanings get mixed up without our noticing, we are bound to commit a logical fallacy. Suppose someone argues that "punishment is an evil," and that by the principles of morality "no evil is to be allowed even with the purpose of doing good." At first we might not see how to escape the conclusion that "no punishments should be allowed," since they cause evil. A little thought shows that the word evil is being used here in two completely different senses. In the first statement it means physical evil, or pain. In the second it means moral evil, or wrongdoing. Moral evil must never be committed, but it does not follow that physical evils must never be inflicted, because they are often the very means of preventing moral evil.

Here is another very convincing fallacy, which has been put forward in many forms: "A thoroughly benevolent man cannot possibly refuse to relieve the poor; and since a person who cannot possibly act otherwise than he does can claim no merit for his actions, it follows that a thoroughly benevolent man can claim no merit for his actions." By this kind of argument, the more virtuous a person became, and so the harder he found it to act wrongly, the less credit he would deserve. Everyone feels sure that the conclusion is false. But the cause of the fallacy can only be found by noticing that the words cannot possibly have a double meaning. In the first case they refer to the pull of moral motives, or good character. In the second they refer to circumstances entirely beyond a person's control, such as being forced by the law, lack of money, or lack of personal freedom. The more closely you study the subtle shifts in the meaning of common words, the more convinced you will be that the tools you have to use in every conversation and argument are dangerous ones. So I must ask you to pay close attention to this lesson.

Terms are called univocal when they can bring only one single, definite meaning to mind. They are called equivocal, or ambiguous, when they have two or more different meanings. Notice, though, that a term is not equivocal just because it can be applied to many objects, as long as it is applied to all of them in the same sense. Cathedral, for instance, is the name of St Paul's, of York Minster, and of the principal churches of Salisbury, Wells, Lincoln and a number of other cities. But it is not ambiguous, because these are all just different instances of the same meaning: they are all objects of the same description, or kind. The word cathedral is probably univocal, with only one logical meaning. The word church, on the other hand, is equivocal. Sometimes it means the building where religious worship takes place. Sometimes it means the body of people who belong to one sect or denomination and meet in churches. And sometimes "the church" means the clergy, as distinct from the laity (the ordinary members). So there is a clear difference in the sense in which the word is used at different times.

Univocal terms are found mostly in technical and scientific language. Steam-engine, gasometer (a large gas-storage tank), railway train, permanent way (the finished track bed of a railway), and a great many other technical names for distinct, familiar objects are univocal enough. In everyday life the names penny, mantelpiece, teacup, and bread and butter have a definite and single enough meaning. In chemistry, too, oxygen, hydrogen, sulphate of copper, alumina, lithia and thousands of other terms are very precise. Often the words themselves were invented quite recently, and their meanings were fixed exactly and kept unchanged. Every science has, or ought to have, a set of terms just as precise and certain in meaning (see Lesson 33). The names of individual objects, buildings, events or people are also usually quite certain and clear: Julius Caesar, William the Conqueror, the first Napoleon, St Peter's, Westminster Abbey, the Great Exhibition of 1851, and so on.

But however many univocal terms we can list, equivocal terms are astonishingly common. They include most of the nouns and adjectives we use every day in ordinary life. They are called ambiguous, from the Latin verb ambigo, to wander, hesitate or be in doubt. They are also called homonymous, from the Greek ὁμός (homos), same, and ὄνομα (onoma), name. Whenever someone uses equivocal words in a way that mixes up their different meanings, and so falls into error, we can say he commits the fallacy of Equivocation, in the logical sense of that name (see Lesson 20). In everyday life, though, we only say someone equivocates when he uses words knowingly and dishonestly, in a way designed to confuse the true meaning with the apparent one.

I will now describe the different kinds and causes of ambiguity in words, following in part the interesting chapters on the subject in Dr. Watts's Logic. First, we can distinguish three classes of equivocal words, depending on whether they are:

	1. Equivocal in sound only.
	2. Equivocal in spelling only.
	3. Equivocal both in sound and spelling.

The first two classes matter comparatively little, and they don't often lead to serious error. They produce what we would call trivial mistakes. When spoken aloud, for example, we can confuse the words right, wright and rite (a ceremony); also rein, rain and reign, might and mite, and so on. Partly because of careless pronunciation, people have been known to confuse the four words air, hair, hare and heir.

Words equivocal in spelling but not in sound include tear (a drop from the eye) and tear, pronounced "tare," meaning a rip in cloth; or lead, the metal, and lead, as in following someone else's lead. Such resemblances can cause little more than a moment's misunderstanding, so we will move straight on to the class of words that are equivocal both in sound and in spelling. I will divide these into three groups, according to whether the equivocation comes:

1. From the accidental confusion of different words.

2. From the transfer of meaning by the association of ideas.

3. From the logical transfer of meaning to analogous objects.

I. In the first group we put a certain number of curious but not very important cases. Here the ambiguity comes from the confusion of entirely different words. They came from different languages, or from different roots in the same language, but over time they took on the same sound and spelling. Take the word mean. It can mean what is medium or mediocre, from the French moyen and the Latin medius, which are related to the Anglo-Saxon mid, or middle. Or it can mean what is small-minded and base, and then it comes from the Anglo-Saxon gemæne, meaning "belonging to the mæne, or many": in short, whatever is common or vulgar. The verb to mean can hardly be confused with the adjective mean, but it comes from a third, separate root, probably related to the Sanskrit verb for "to think."

Here are some other examples of this accidental kind of ambiguity. Rent can be a money payment, from the French rente (from rendre, to give back). Or it can be a tear, the result of the action of rending. This second word is of Anglo-Saxon origin, and belongs to the large group of words beginning with r or wr that imitate, more or less closely, the sound of the action they name. Pounds, from the Latin pondus, a weight, gets confused with pound in the sense of a village pinfold, the pen where stray cattle were shut up, which comes from the Saxon pyndan, to pen up. Fell, a mountain, is a completely different word from fell, a skin or hide (a sense already old-fashioned by the 1870s). And pulse, a throb or beat, and pulse, meaning peas, beans or a soup made from them, though both come from Greek or Latin, are probably quite unrelated words. Curiously, gin in the sense of a trap or machine is a shortened form of engine, while gin the alcoholic drink is a corruption of Geneva, the place where the spirit was first made.

Some important cases of confusion have been found in grammar. One example is the confusion between the number one, which comes from an Aryan root (what we now call Indo-European) through the Latin unus, and the indefinite pronoun one (as in "one ought to do one's duty"). The pronoun is really a corrupted form of the French word homme, meaning man. To this day the Germans use man in this sense, as in man sagt, that is, "one says."

2. By far the largest share of equivocal words became equivocal through a transfer of meaning. The word moved from the thing it first named to some other thing that was regularly connected with it, so that the two became closely linked in our thoughts. In the language of Parliament, for example, "the House" means either the chamber where the members meet, or the body of members who happen to be gathered in it at any time. In the same way, the word church originally meant the building (κυριακόν, kyriakon, "the Lord's House") where any religious worshippers gather, but from there it has picked up a variety of meanings. It can mean a particular group of worshippers who regularly meet in one place, which is the sense it has in Acts 14:23. It can mean any body of people who hold the same beliefs and are joined in one organization, as in the Anglican, Greek or Roman Catholic Church. It is sometimes used to include the laity as well as the clergy. But perhaps more often the clergy and religious authorities of a sect or country are so strongly associated with the act of worship that they are called the church in a special sense, as if they were the church above all others. It is also quite clear that the word means something entirely different depending on whether it is used by a member of the Anglican, Greek, Roman Catholic, Scottish Presbyterian, or any other existing church.

The word foot has gone through several curious but very clear transfers of meaning. It first meant the foot of a person or an animal, and it is probably distantly related to the Latin pes, pedis and the Greek πούς, ποδός (pous, podos). Because the length of a foot is a natural rough measure of length, the word came to be used for a fixed measure of length. And because the foot is at the bottom of the body, the name was extended by analogy to the foot of a mountain or the feet of a table. By a further extension, any position, plan, reason or argument that we stand on and rely on is called our foot, or footing. The same word also means soldiers who fight on their feet, that is, infantry, and the measured unit of a line of verse, which has a definite length. These very different meanings are naturally connected with the original one, and we can see this from the fact that the Latin and Greek words for foot go through exactly the same series of ambiguities.

It would be a long job to trace out all the various, and often contradictory, meanings of the word fellow. Originally a fellow was one who follows another, that is, a companion. From there it came to mean the other one of a pair, as one shoe is the fellow of the other, or simply an equal, as when we say that Shakespeare "hath not a fellow." From the plain meaning of companion, it also came to mean, vaguely, a person, as in the question "What fellow is that?" But there is a curious mixture of scorn and affection in the word. In the 1870s, to call a man a mere fellow, or simply a fellow in a certain tone of voice, was to show him severe contempt. Change the tone of voice, or the words around it, even slightly, and it becomes one of the warmest and most affectionate names there is, as when we speak of a dear fellow or a good fellow. We can add the technical meanings of the word too, as in a Fellow of a college, or of a learned society.

Another good example of many different meanings growing from a single root is the word post. Originally a post was something posited, that is, placed firmly in the ground, such as an upright piece of wood or stone. That meaning survives in a lamp-post, a gate-post, a signal-post, and so on. A post was often used to mark a fixed spot of ground, as a mile-post does, so the word came to mean the fixed or appointed place where the post stood, as in a military post, or the post of danger or of honor. In the days of the Roman Empire, the fixed stations where horses were kept ready, so that people could travel fast, were called posts in this way. From there the whole system for carrying people or news came to be called the posts. The word still has exactly this meaning in most of Europe, and we still use it in post-chaise (a hired fast carriage of the horse-drawn era), post-boy and post-horse, and in postillion (the rider on one of the horses). A postal system for letters has now been running for about two centuries in England and other countries, so this is perhaps the meaning most closely tied to the word post today, and a number of expressions have arisen from it, such as post-office, postage, postal-guide, postman, postmaster, postal-telegraph and so on. Curiously enough, we now have iron letter-posts (the pillar boxes where letters are mailed), and in them the word post is brought back to exactly its original meaning.

I chose the words above for their curious variety of meanings, but I do not hesitate to claim that most common nouns have several meanings, some more and some fewer. Dr. Watts, in his Logic, suggests that the words book, bible, fish, house and elephant are univocal terms, but the reader will easily find ambiguities in each of them. Fish, for instance, means something very different in natural history from what it means to people who are not scientists. They include under it not only true fishes but shellfish, or mollusks, and the cetacea, such as whales and seals; in short, every swimming animal, whether it has the character of a true fish or not. Elephant, in a stationer's or bookseller's shop, meant a large size of paper in the 1870s rather than a large animal. Bible sometimes means a particular copy of the Bible, and sometimes the collection of works that makes up the Holy Scriptures. The word man is remarkably ambiguous. Sometimes it means man as distinguished from woman. At other times it is certainly used to include both sexes. In certain recent election cases, the lawyers could not decide whether the word man, as used in the Reform Act of 1867, ought or ought not to be read as including women. On other occasions man means a grown male as distinguished from a boy, and it often means someone who is emphatically a man, with a manly character. Now and then it is used the way groom is, to mean a servant, as in the proverb, "Like master, like man." At other times it means a husband in particular.

3. Third, among ambiguous words we must pick out those that get their different meanings in a somewhat different way, namely by analogy, or real resemblance. When we speak of a sweet taste, a sweet flower, a sweet tune, a sweet landscape, a sweet face, a sweet poem, we are plainly applying one and the same word to very different things. Something as concrete as a lump of sugar can hardly be compared directly with something as intellectual as Tennyson's poem "The May Queen." Even so, if the word sweet is to be called ambiguous, it is ambiguous in a different way from the words we looked at before. All these things are called sweet because of a particular kind of pleasure they give, and that pleasure cannot be described except by comparing it with sugar. In the same way we call a pain sharp, a disappointment bitter, a person's temper sour, the future bright or gloomy, an achievement brilliant. All these adjectives imply a comparison with the simplest kinds of bodily sensation. The adjective brilliant comes from the French briller, to glitter or sparkle, and it keeps this meaning fully when we speak of a brilliant diamond, a brilliant star and so on. But by what a subtle analogy we speak of a brilliant position, a brilliant achievement, brilliant talents, a brilliant style! We cannot speak of a clear explanation, indefatigable perseverance, a perspicuous style, or a sore calamity, without using in each of these phrases a double analogy to physical impressions, actions or events. (Perspicuous means clear, literally "see-through"; indefatigable means "that cannot be tired out.") We will see in Lesson 6 that we owe the creation of all the names for mental feelings and mental things to this process.

Read Watts's Logic, Chapter iv.

Locke's Essay on the Human Understanding, Book III, Chapters ix. and x.

5. Explain the ambiguity of any of the following terms. Assign each one to its proper cause, and trace, as far as you can, how each separate meaning grew out of the original meaning.
