Lesson 6: The Growth of Language

We have seen that words become equivocal in at least three different ways. Different words can get confused by accident. A word can change its meaning because it is habitually associated with things other than the thing it first meant. And a word can be carried over, by analogy, to objects of a similar nature. We now need to look more closely at certain changes in language that come from that last cause, because they are going on all the time. In fact, we can almost watch language being created and extended, and for the logician the subject is a very instructive and important one. Two great processes, working in opposite directions, change language in this way:

1. Generalization, by which a name comes to be applied to a wider class of objects than before, so that the extension of its meaning is increased and its intension diminished.

2. Specialization, by which a name comes to be restricted to a narrower class, so that its extension is decreased and its intension increased.

The first change happens in the most obvious way. We notice a resemblance between some new object that has no name and some object we know well, and to express the resemblance we instinctively apply the old name to the new object. We are all well acquainted with glass, for example. If we come across some substance with the same glassy nature and look, we will be quick to call it a kind of glass, and if we meet this new kind of glass often enough, it will probably come to share the name equally with the old, original kind. The word coal has gone through a change like this. Originally it was the name of charked, or charred, wood, which was the main fuel in use five hundred years ago. When mineral coal came into use, it took its name from the older fuel, which it resembled more than it resembled anything else, but at first it was marked off as sea-coal or pit-coal. Now that it is by far the more common of the two, it has taken the plain name, and we mark off the charred wood as charcoal. Paper has gone through a similar change. It originally meant the papyrus used in the Roman Empire, and it was carried over to the new writing material made of cotton or linen rags, which came in at some quite uncertain date. The word character is interesting because of the way logic uses it. The Greek χαρακτήρ meant, strictly, a tool for engraving. By association it was carried over to the marks or letters engraved with that tool, and the word still keeps this meaning when we speak of Greek characters or Arabic characters, meaning figures or letters. But objects often have natural marks, signs or tokens that point them out just as well as artificial characters do, so the name was generalized. It now means any peculiar or distinctive mark or quality by which an object is easily recognized.

Changes of this kind are usually made by no one in particular and with no definite purpose. They come about through a sort of unconscious instinct among the many people who use the name. In the language of science, though, changes are often made on purpose, with a clear understanding of the generalization involved. In everyday life, for example, soap means only a compound of soda or potash with fat. Chemists, however, have deliberately widened the name to include any compound of a metallic salt with a fatty substance. So there are such things as lime-soap and lead-soap, and lead-soap is used in making ordinary diachylon plaster (a sticking plaster). Alcohol at first meant the product of ordinary fermentation, commonly called spirits of wine. Then chemists discovered that many other substances had a theoretical composition very like that of spirits of wine, and the name was adopted for the whole class. A long list of different kinds of alcohols can be found in Dr. Roscoe's lessons on chemistry, and the number of known alcohols can go on growing without limit as discoveries are made. Every one of the chemical terms acid, alkali, metal, alloy, earth, ether, oil, gas and salt can be shown to have gone through great generalizations.

The other sciences supply hardly fewer examples. A lens originally meant a lentil-shaped, or double convex, piece of glass, because that was the kind of glass opticians used most often. But as glasses of other shapes came into use alongside lenses, the name was extended to concave pieces of glass, and even to perfectly flat ones. The words lever, plane, cone, cylinder, arc, conic section, curve, prism, magnet, pendulum, ray, light and many others have been generalized in the same way.

In ordinary language we can see that even proper, or singular, names are often generalized. In Cicero's time, for example, a good actor was called a Roscius, after an actor of outstanding talent. The successor of Julius Caesar adopted the name Caesar as an official name of the Emperor, and it gradually came to mean the same thing as Emperor, so that in the present day the Kaisers of Austria and the Czars of Russia both take their title from Caesar. The abstract name Caesarism has even been formed, to express a kind of imperial system like the one Caesar set up. A king of Egypt built a famous tower on the island of Pharos, at the entrance to the harbor of Alexandria, and because of it lighthouses are called phares in French, and were once called pharos in English. And from the famous Roman general Quintus Fabius Maximus, anyone who avoids bringing a contest to a showdown is said to follow a Fabian policy.

In science, too, singular names are often extended, as when the fixed stars are called distant suns, or the companions of Jupiter are called his moons. There is even a theory, and a likely one, that all general names were created by this process of generalization in the early ages of human progress. Grasping general notions takes a higher intellect than grasping single, concrete things, so it seems natural that names should at first have stood for individual objects, and only afterward been extended to classes. We catch a glimpse of this process in the case of the Aboriginal Australians who had been used to calling a large dog Cadli. When horses were first brought into the country, they took up this name as the nearest description of a horse. Captain Cook tells a very similar story about the people of Otaheite (Tahiti). It can be objected, though, that some act of judgment must have been made before anyone could see that a name suited a particular thing. So it seems likely that specialization as well as generalization was at work at the very beginning of language, much as both are now.

Specialization is the exact opposite of generalization, and it is almost as important. It consists in narrowing the extension of a general name, so that it comes to be the name only of an individual, or of a small part of the original class. This is how we get the names we need for the many new tools, occupations and ideas we deal with as civilization advances. The name physician comes from the Greek φυσικός, natural, and φύσις, nature, so properly it means someone who has studied nature, especially the nature of the human body. It has become restricted, however, to people who use this knowledge for medical purposes, and those who investigate natural science have had to adopt the new name physicist. The name naturalist has been restricted in the same way, to people who study living things. The name surgeon originally meant a handicraftsman. It is a corrupted form of chirurgeon, which comes from the Greek χειρουργός, hand-worker. But it has long been specialized to the people who carry out the mechanical parts of the healing art.

Language is full of examples just as good. Minister originally meant a servant, someone who acted as the minor, or lesser, of another. Now it often means, specifically, the most important man in the kingdom. A chancellor was a clerk, or even a doorkeeper, who sat in a space closed off by bars, or cancelli, in the offices of the Roman Emperor's palace. Now it is always the name of a high official, or even the highest. A peer was an equal (Latin, Par), and we still talk about being tried by our peers; but now, through the strange accidents of language, it means the few who rank above the rest of the Queen's subjects. Deacon, Bishop, Clerk, Queen, Captain and General are all words that have been through a similar process of specialization. In words like telegraph, rail, signal and station, and many other words to do with new inventions, we can follow the change happening within a single lifetime.

One effect of specialization is that it very soon creates a difference between any two words that happen, for some reason, to be synonymous. Two or more words are called synonymous (from the Greek σύν, with, and ὄνομα, name) when they have the same meaning, as perhaps teacher and instructor do, or similarity and resemblance, beginning and commencement, sameness and identity, hypothesis and supposition, intension and comprehension. But the truth is that words commonly called synonymous are seldom perfectly so. There are almost always shades of difference in their meaning or use, which are explained in books like Crabb's English Synonyms. A process that Coleridge called desynonymization, and Herbert Spencer called differentiation, is always going on, and it tends to specialize one word of a synonymous pair to one meaning and the other to another. Wave and billow, for example, originally meant exactly the same physical effect. But poets have now taken over the word 'billow,' while wave is used mainly in practical and scientific matters. Undulation is a third synonym, and in time it will probably become the only scientific term for a wave. Cab was originally just a short form of cabriolet, and so meant much the same thing, but it is now specialized to mean almost nothing but a hackney cab, a cab for hire. In America, car is coming to be restricted to the meaning of a railway car.

We may note that it is a logical defect in a language to have a great many synonymous terms. We get into the habit of using them interchangeably, without being sure that they are free of ambiguities and hidden differences of meaning. English suffers especially from this inconvenience, because it has a complete series of words from Greek or Latin roots that are nearly synonymous with other words of Saxon or French origin. The same statement can, in fact, be put into either Saxon or classical English, and, as Whately has rightly remarked, we often seem to prove a statement merely by repeating it in different words. The wealth and variety of words may add to the rhetorical power of the language, but it also sets traps for all kinds of fallacies. (See Lessons 20 and 21.)

Besides generalization and specialization, a vast number of additions and changes come into language through the analogical, or metaphorical, extension of the meaning of words. No doubt this change could be said to be a kind of generalization, since there must always be some resemblance between the new and the old uses of the term. But the resemblance is often a very distant and obscure one, the kind we would call analogy rather than identity. Every word used metaphorically, or as a comparison, is a case of this process of extension. The name metaphor comes from the Greek words μετά, over, and φέρειν, to carry, and apparently expresses the carrying over of a word from its ordinary use to a special one. The old comparison of a ruler to the pilot of a ship, for example, has given rise to many metaphors, as when we say the Prime Minister is at the Helm of the State. In fact the word governor, with everything derived from it, is one result of this metaphor, since it is just a corrupted form of gubernator, steersman. The words compass, polestar, ensign, anchor and many others connected with navigation are constantly used metaphorically. Horses and hunting give us another set of metaphors: taking the reins of government, overturning the government, taking the bit between the teeth, the Government Whip, being heavily weighted, and so on. No doubt we could show that every other familiar occupation in life has supplied its own stock of metaphors.

It is easy to show, however, that this process does not only go on consciously today. It must have been at work throughout the history of language, and we owe to it almost all, and probably all, the words that express refined mental or spiritual ideas. The very word spirit, now the most refined and immaterial of ideas, is only the Latin spiritus, a gentle breeze or breathing. Inspiration, esprit (or wit) and many other words come from this metaphor. It is a truly curious fact that almost all the words in different languages for mind or soul imply the same comparison with breath. Soul is from the Gothic root meaning a strong wind or storm. The Latin words animus and anima are thought to be connected with the Greek ἄνεμος, wind. ψυχή certainly comes from ψύχω, to blow. πνεῦμα, air or breath, is used in the New Testament for Spiritual Being. And our word ghost has been said to have a similar origin.

Almost all the terms used in mental philosophy, or metaphysics, for the actions and phenomena of the mind come in the end from metaphors. Apprehension is putting out the hand to take hold of something; comprehension is taking things together in a handful; extension is spreading out; intention, bending toward; explication, unfolding; application, folding to; conception, taking up together; relation, carrying back; experience is going thoroughly through a thing; difference is carrying apart; deliberation, weighing out; interruption, breaking between; proposition, placing before; intuition, seeing into. The list could be extended almost without end. Our English name for reason, the understanding, obviously contains some physical metaphor that has never been fully explained, and the Latin intellect contains a metaphor too.

Every sense gives rise to words with refined meanings. Sapience, taste, insipidity and gout (in the sense of taste or relish) come from the sense of taste, and sagacity from the dog's extraordinary power of smell. But the sense of sight is by far the sharpest and most intellectual, so it gives rise to the larger part of language. Clearness, lucidity, obscurity, haziness, perspicuity and countless other expressions come from this sense.

It is truly astonishing how much power language has, through generalization, specialization and metaphor, to create many words from one single root. Professor Max Müller has given a remarkable example in the root spec, which means sight. It appears in the Aryan (Indo-European) languages: in the Sanskrit spas, in the Greek σκέπτομαι (with the consonants swapped round), in the Latin specio, and even in the English spy. Here is an incomplete list of the words developed from this one root: species, special, especial, specimen, spice, spicy, specious, speciality, specific, specialization, specie (gold or silver coin), spectre, specification, spectacle, spectator, spectral, spectrum, speculum, specular, speculation. The same root also combines with various prefixes, and so we get a series of words: suspect, aspect, circumspect, expect, inspect, prospect, respect, retrospect, introspection, conspicuous, perspicuity, perspective. Each of these, in turn, has a number of derivatives attached to it. From suspect, for example, we get suspicion, suspicable, suspicious, suspiciously, suspiciousness. By my own count there are at least 246 words in all, used at one time or another in the English language, that undoubtedly come from the one root spec.

J. S. Mill's Logic, Book IV. Chap. v. On the Natural History of the Variations in the Meanings of Terms.

Archbishop Trench, On the Study of Words.

Max Müller, Lectures on the Science of Language.

Questions and Exercises

1. Trace the generalization or specialization that has taken place in any of the following words: kind, genus, class, species, order, rank, Augustus, president, speaker, Utopia, rock, Commons, doctor.

2. Point out metaphors that come from the notions of weight, straightness, rock and wind.

3. Distinguish as precisely as you can the meanings of the following synonyms: sickness, malady; mud, mire; confutation, refutation; boundary, limit; mind, intellect; recollection, reminiscence; procrastination, dilatoriness; converse, reverse, obverse, inverse.

4. Make lists of all the words derived from any of the following roots:

(1) Tendere, to stretch, as in intention, attention.

(2) Ponere, to place, as in position, supposition.

(3) Genus, tribe or kind, as in genus, generation.

(4) Munus, gift, as in remuneration, common (Latin, Communis).

(5) Modus, shape or fashion, as in mood, moderate.

(6) Scribere, to write, as in scribe, inscription, describe.

(7) Capere, to take, as in deception, incipient.

Lesson 7: Leibnitz on Knowledge

When we study terms, we need to understand clearly what it takes to have a perfect notion of what a term means. When someone uses a name like monarch, or civilization, or autonomy, it points the mind toward some thing or some idea, and if possible we ought to have perfect knowledge of that thing or idea before we use the word. So what does this perfect knowledge consist of? What are its necessary characters? The famous mathematician and philosopher Leibniz tried to answer this question in a short treatise, or tract, first published in 1684. Several recent books on logic base what they say on the subject on this tract, and Mr. Baynes has added a complete translation of it at the end of his translation of the Port Royal Logic. Leibniz's own remarks are not always easy to understand, so I will not stick to his exact words. Instead I will try to give the simplest possible statement of his views, as Dr. Thomson and Sir William Hamilton have interpreted them.

Knowledge is either obscure or clear; either confused or distinct; either adequate or inadequate; and lastly, either symbolical or intuitive. Perfect knowledge must be clear, distinct, adequate and intuitive. If it falls short in any one of these respects, it is more or less imperfect. So we can classify knowledge as in the following scheme:

A notion, which simply means our knowledge of a thing, is obscure when it does not let us recognize the thing again and tell it apart from everything else. We have a clear notion of a rose, and of most common flowers, because we can recognize them with certainty and don't mix them up with each other. We also have a clear notion of our close friends, and of people we meet regularly, because we recognize them whenever we see them, with complete certainty and without hesitating. It is said that a shepherd, with practice, gets a clear notion of each sheep in his flock, so that he can pick out any one of them on its own. In the same way, a keeper of hounds learns the name and character of each hound, while other people have only an obscure idea of the hounds in general and could not tell one from another. But the geologist cannot give a clear idea of what sandstone, or conglomerate, or schist, or slate, or trap rock (a dark volcanic rock) consists of. Rocks vary endlessly in degree and character, and it is often barely possible to say whether a rock is sandstone or conglomerate, schist or slate, and so on. Among the lowest forms of life, the naturalist hardly has a clear notion of animal life as distinct from plant life: it is often hard to decide whether a protophyte (a simple one-celled organism) should be classed with the animals or with the plants.

Clear knowledge, in turn, is confused when we cannot pick out the parts and qualities of the thing we know, and can only recognize it as a whole. Anyone knows a friend instantly and could tell him apart from everyone else, yet would usually find it impossible to say how he knows him, or by what marks. He could not describe his friend's figure or features except in the very roughest way. Someone with no practice in drawing who tries to sketch even a familiar animal like a horse or a cow soon finds that he has only a confused notion of its shape, while an artist has a distinct idea of the shape of every limb. A chemist has a distinct as well as a clear notion of gold and silver. He can not only tell with certainty whether a piece of metal really is gold or silver, but can also name and describe exactly the qualities by which he knows it, and could, if necessary, list a great many other qualities as well. We have a very distinct notion of a chessboard, because we know it consists of 64 squares. All our ideas of geometrical figures, such as triangles, circles, parallelograms, squares, pentagons, hexagons and so on, are, or ought to be, perfectly distinct. But when we talk about a constitutional government, or a civilized nation, we have only the vaguest idea of what we mean. We cannot say exactly what it takes to make a government constitutional without also taking in governments we don't mean to include; and the same goes for civilized nations. These terms have meanings that are neither distinct nor clear.

Notice that no simple idea, such as the idea of the color red, can be distinct in the sense meant here, because nobody can analyze the color red or describe to someone else what it is. A person who has been blind from birth cannot be brought to imagine it, and the only way we can define what it is like is to put an actual red object in front of someone's eyes. The same is generally true of all simple sensations, whether tastes, smells, colors or sounds. So these may be known clearly, but not distinctly, in the sense Leibniz gives that word.

It is not easy to explain the difference Leibniz meant by the names adequate and inadequate. He says, "When everything which enters into a distinct notion is distinctly known, or when the last analysis is reached, the knowledge is adequate, of which I scarcely know whether a perfect example can be offered —the knowledge of numbers, however, approaches near to it."

So to have adequate knowledge of things, we must be able to pick out not only the parts that make up our notion of a thing, but also the parts that make up those parts. For instance, we might be said to have an adequate notion of a chessboard. We know it is made up of 64 squares, and we know each of those squares distinctly, because each one is made up of 4 equal straight lines joined at right angles. Even so, we cannot be said to have a distinct notion of a straight line, because we cannot really define it, or break it down into anything simpler. To be completely adequate, our knowledge would have to allow analysis after analysis, forever, and that would make adequate knowledge impossible. But, as Dr. Thomson remarks, we can count any knowledge as adequate if it carries the analysis far enough for the purpose we have in mind. An engineer, for instance, has adequate knowledge of a machine if he knows not only its various wheels and parts, but also what those parts are for, what they are made of, what shapes they have and how they act, provided that he also knows all the mechanical properties of the materials and all the geometrical properties of the shapes that could affect how the machine works. But nobody expects him to go further still and explain why iron or wood of a particular quality is strong or brittle, why oil works as a lubricant, or what axioms the principles of mechanical forces rest on.

Last of all, we must notice the very important distinction between symbolical and intuitive knowledge. Going by the original meaning of the word, intuitive would mean what we gain by seeing (from the Latin intueor, "to look at"). Any knowledge we have directly through the senses, or that comes straight into the mind, is called intuitive. So we may learn intuitively what a square or a hexagon is, but hardly what a chiliagon, a figure with 1000 sides, is.

We could not tell the difference by sight between a figure with 1000 sides and a figure with 1001 sides. Nor can we picture any such figure completely in the mind. We know it only by name, or symbolically. All large numbers, like the ones that give the speed of light (186,000 miles per second) or the distance of the sun (91,000,000 miles), are known to us only by symbols, and they are beyond the power of our imagination.

We know infinity in a similar way. So we can become acquainted, with the intellect, with something our senses could never tell us about. We also speak of nothing, of zero, of what contradicts itself, of the non-existent, and even of the unthinkable or inconceivable. These words stand for things that can never be pictured in the mind, much less perceived intuitively through the senses; they can only be handled in a purely symbolical way.

In arithmetic and algebra we deal mostly with symbolical knowledge alone. When we work through a long arithmetic question or an algebra problem, we don't need to stop at each step and picture to ourselves what the numbers and symbols mean. Algebra teaches us that if we multiply the sum of two quantities by their difference, we get the difference of their squares; in symbols, (a+b)(a−b)=a2−b2. It is easy to see that this is true, like this:

a+ba−ba2+ab−ab−b2a2+0−b2.

Here we are working in the dark, symbolically. We use the letters a and b according to certain fixed rules, without knowing or caring what they mean. And whatever meaning we later give to a and b, we can be sure the process still holds and the conclusion is true, without going over the steps again.

In geometry, though, we argue by seeing intuitively that each step is true. We actually hold a picture of the figures in our mind, and satisfy ourselves that the figures really do have the properties required. So the algebraic truth shown above in symbols can easily be proved true of lines, and of the rectangles formed by those lines, as a corollary of the 5th Proposition of Euclid's Second Book.

There is a lot to be said about the advantages of the intuitive and the symbolical methods compared with each other. The symbolical method is usually far less laborious, and gives the most widely applicable answers. But it seldom or never gives the same command and understanding of a subject as the intuitive method does. That is why the study of geometry is always indispensable in education, even though the same truths can often be proved more easily by algebra. It is Newton's special glory that he managed to explain the motions of the heavenly bodies by the geometrical, or intuitive, method, while the greatest of his successors, such as Lagrange and Laplace, have dealt with these motions with the help of symbols.

What is true of mathematics applies to every kind of reasoning. Words are symbols just as much as A, B, C or x, y, z are, and it is possible to argue with words without being aware of what they mean at all. Suppose I say that "selenium is a dyad element, and a dyad element is one capable of replacing two equivalents of hydrogen." Nobody who knows no chemistry will be able to attach any meaning to these terms, and yet anybody will be able to conclude that "selenium is capable of replacing two equivalents of hydrogen." Such a person argues in a purely symbolical way. In the same way, whenever we use words in everyday life without having their full and exact meaning in mind at that moment, we have symbolical knowledge only.

There is no worse habit for a student or a reader to fall into than accepting words in place of a knowledge of things. Reading a book of natural history about Infusoria, Foraminifera, Rotifera and the like is perhaps worse than useless if those names do not bring clear images to mind. And a student who has not watched experiments, and examined the substances with his own eyes, will not get much out of books on chemistry and physics (natural philosophy). He will meet hundreds of new terms in them, and to him they will be nothing but empty and confusing signs. For this reason we should never miss a chance to get to know, through our own senses, the forms, properties and changes of things. That way, the language we use can be used intuitively as far as possible, and we can be saved from the absurdities and fallacies we might otherwise fall into. In short, we should follow Bacon's advice, ipsis consuescere rebus: to accustom ourselves to things themselves.

Hamilton's Lectures on Logic. Lect. IX.

Baynes' Port Royal Logic. Part I. Chap. 9, and Appendix.

Questions and Exercises

1. What are the characters of perfect knowledge?

2. Describe the kind of knowledge we have of each of the following notions or objects: A syllogism. Electricity. Motion. A triangle. Eternity. The weight of the earth (5852 trillions of tons, counting a trillion, as the British did then, as a million million million). The color of the sky.

3. Explain exactly what you mean by intuitive knowledge.

Propositions

Lesson 8: Kinds of Propositions

A term standing alone cannot express a truth. All it does is direct the mind to some object, or class of objects, about which something may be affirmed or denied; the term itself affirms or denies nothing. "Sun," "air," "table" each call up an object of thought in every mind, but we cannot say that "sun is true," or "air is mistaken," or "table is false." Before words or terms can express the reasoning acts of the mind that we call true or false, we have to join them into sentences, or propositions. "The sun is bright," "the air is fresh," "the table is unsteady" are statements that may be true or may be false, and in any circumstances we can at least ask whether they are true. Now, just as the logical term was defined as any combination of words expressing an act of simple apprehension, a logical proposition is any combination of words expressing an act of judgment. In short, the proposition is the result of an act of judgment put into the form of language.

What the logician calls a proposition, the grammarian calls a sentence. But although every proposition is a sentence, we must not suppose that every sentence is a proposition. There are in fact several kinds of sentence that are more or less distinct from a proposition: the Sentence Interrogative, or Question; the Sentence Imperative, or Command; the Sentence Optative, which expresses a wish; and the Exclamatory Sentence, which expresses an emotion of wonder or surprise. These kinds of sentence can perhaps be turned, by a more or less roundabout way of speaking, into the form of a Sentence Indicative, which is the grammatical name for a proposition. But until that has been done they have no proper place in logic, or at least no place that logicians have so far explained well enough.

The name proposition comes from the Latin words pro, before, and pono, I place. It means laying or placing before someone the result of an act of judgment. Every act of judgment, or comparison, must involve the two things being compared. So every proposition will naturally have three parts: the two terms, or names, standing for the things compared, and the copula, or verb, showing the connection between them as it was found in the act of judgment. Take the proposition "Gold is a yellow substance." It expresses an agreement in color between gold and certain other substances already called yellow. Gold and yellow substance are clearly the two terms, and is is the copula.

The first term of a proposition is always called the subject, because it stands for the underlying matter, so to speak, about which something is asserted (from the Latin sub, under, and jactum, laid). The second term is called the predicate, which simply means what is affirmed or asserted. This name comes from the Latin prædicare, to assert, which also gave French its word prédicateur, worn down in English into our "preacher." Don't confuse this Latin verb with the rather similar prædicere, which has an entirely different meaning: to predict, or foretell. I strongly suspect that newspaper writers and others who pedantically use the verb "to predicate" sometimes fall into this confusion and really mean to predict. In any case, a purely technical term like predicate should not be dragged into ordinary language without need, when there are so many other good words that could be used instead. This and every other technical term of science should be kept to its proper scientific use. Neglecting that rule damages the language of everyday life and the language of science at the same time.

Propositions fall into two kinds, depending on whether they make their statement conditionally or unconditionally. The proposition "If metals are heated they are softened" is conditional. It does not make an assertion about metals in general, only about metals in the circumstance of being heated. Any circumstance that has to be granted or supposed before the assertion applies is a condition. Conditional propositions are of two kinds, Hypothetical and Disjunctive, but it is best to leave them for a later lesson (Lesson 19). For some time to come we shall be concerned only with unconditional propositions. These are usually called Categorical Propositions, from the Greek verb κατηγορέω (kategoreo, to assert or affirm).

The following diagram gives a convenient picture of the classification of sentences and propositions as far as we have gone:

Now we need to look carefully at the different kinds of categorical propositions. They are classified according to quality and according to quantity. As regards quality they are either affirmative or negative; as regards quantity they are either universal or particular.

An affirmative proposition is one that asserts a certain agreement between the subject and the predicate, so that the qualities or attributes of the predicate belong to the subject. The proposition "gold is a yellow substance" states that gold agrees with other yellow substances in such a way that we know it has the color yellow, along with whatever qualities are implied in the name substance. A negative proposition, on the other hand, asserts a difference or disagreement, so that some at least of the qualities of the predicate do not belong to the subject. "Gold is not easily fusible" denies that the quality of being easily melted belongs to gold.

Propositions are also divided according to quantity into universal and particular propositions. If the proposition affirms that the predicate belongs to the whole of the subject, it is a universal proposition. An example is "all metals are elements," which affirms that the quality of being undecomposable, or simple in nature, is true of all metals. But if we say "some metals are brittle," the quality of brittleness is affirmed only of some indefinite portion of the metals, and nothing in the proposition lets us be sure that any particular metal you might name is brittle. The name particular comes from the diminutive of the Latin pars, a part, so you would expect it to mean a small part. In logic, though, it must be carefully understood to mean any part at all, from the smallest fraction up to nearly the whole. Particular propositions do not include cases where a predicate is affirmed of the whole of the subject, or of none of it, but they include every case between those limits. So we can count all propositions like the following as particular:

A very few metals are less dense than water.

Most elements are metals.

Many of the planets are comparatively small bodies.

Not a few distinguished men have had distinguished sons.

The reader must pay careful attention to a rather subtle point, explained further on: a particular proposition, although it asserts the predicate only of a part of the subject, does not deny that it is true of the whole.

Aristotle, in fact, held that there were four kinds of proposition altogether as regards quantity, namely:

	Proposition:
	    Universal.
	    Particular.
	    Singular.
	    Indefinite.

A singular proposition is one that has a singular term for its subject, as in:

	Socrates was very wise.
	London is a vast city.

But we can fairly treat a singular proposition as a universal one, because it clearly refers to the whole of its subject, which in this case is one single individual thing.

Indefinite, or indesignate, propositions are those that carry no mark of quantity at all, so that the wording gives us no way of judging whether the predicate applies to the whole of the subject or only to part of it. "Metals are useful" and "comets are subject to the law of gravitation" are indefinite propositions. In reality, however, such propositions have no distinct place in logic at all, and the logician cannot properly deal with them until their true and precise meaning has been made clear. The predicate must be true either of the whole of the subject or of part of it, so the proposition as it stands is plainly incomplete. But if we try to fix this by supplying the marks of quantity ourselves, we step outside the proper boundaries of logic, because we are assuming that we know the subject matter, or the science, that the proposition is about. We can safely take the examples above to mean "some metals are useful" and "all comets are subject to the law of gravitation," but not on logical grounds. So we may strike the class of indefinite propositions out of logic altogether, on the understanding that they must be made definite before we deal with them. I should point out, though, that in the lessons that follow I shall often use propositions in the indefinite form as examples, on the understanding that where no sign of quantity appears, the universal quantity is to be assumed. Probably, wherever a term is used on its own, it ought to be taken to mean the whole of its class. But however that may be, we need not recognize the indefinite proposition as a separate kind. And since singular propositions have been resolved into universals, only two kinds remain: Universal and Particular.

Remembering now that there are two kinds of proposition as regards quality, and two as regards quantity, we can form four varieties altogether, like this:

The vowels placed on the right are symbols, or shorthand names, that are always used for the four kinds of proposition. Their meaning is easy to remember once we notice that A and I occur in the Latin verb affirmo, I affirm, and E and O in nego, I deny.

There will usually be no difficulty in putting a proposition we meet in our reading into its proper class. The mark of universality is usually some adjective of quantity, such as all, every, each, any, none; but whenever the predicate is clearly meant to apply to the whole of the subject, we may treat the proposition as universal. The signs of a particular proposition are the adjectives of quantity some, certain, a few, many, most, or any others that clearly indicate part at least.

A negative proposition is recognized by the adverbial particle not joined to the copula. But in the proposition E, that is, the universal negative, we often put the particle no or none in front of the subject instead. So "no metals are compound" and "none of the ancients were acquainted with the laws of motion" are familiar forms of the universal negative.

The student must also always be ready to meet misleading or ambiguous ways of putting things. The proposition "all the metals are not denser than water," for instance, might be taken as E or as O, depending on whether we read it as "no metals are denser than water" or as "not all the metals are denser than water," and the second, of course, is the true sense. The little adjective few is especially liable to a subtle ambiguity of this kind. If I say "few books are at once learned and amusing," I may fairly be taken to assert that a few books certainly are both; but what I really mean to draw attention to is my belief that "the greater number of books are not at once learned and amusing." A proposition of this kind should generally be classed as O rather than I. The word some suffers from exactly the same sort of ambiguity: it can mean "some but not all," or "some at least, and perhaps all." The second appears to be the correct interpretation, as the next lesson shows (see the table in Lesson 9 of how the truth of each proposition bears on the others).

As we meet propositions in ordinary language, they come with various inversions and other departures from the simple logical form.

(1) It is quite common, especially in poetry, to find the predicate placed first, for emphasis or variety, as in "Blessed are the merciful," "Comes something down with eventide," "Great is Diana of the Ephesians." Such an inversion of the terms is usually easy to spot, and the sentence must then be put back into the regular order before logic can deal with it.

(2) The subject can sometimes be mistaken for the predicate when it is described by a relative clause standing at the end of the sentence, as in "no one is free who is enslaved by his appetites." Here free is clearly the predicate, although it stands in the middle of the sentence, and "one who is enslaved by his appetites" is the real subject. This proposition is clearly of the form E.

Propositions are also expressed in various ways that differ from the simple logical order, and we must notice some of the kinds that result.

Exclusive propositions contain words such as only, alone, none but, which limit the predicate to the subject. In "elements alone are metals," we are told that the predicate "metal" cannot be applied to anything except "elements," but we are not to understand that "all elements are metals." The same meaning is expressed by "none but elements are metals," or again by "all that are not elements are not metals," and we shall see in the next lesson that this is really equivalent to "all metals are elements." Arguments that look fallacious at first sight will often turn out to be correct when they contain exclusive propositions and these are properly interpreted.

Exceptive propositions affirm a predicate of all the subject except certain stated cases, to which, by implication, the predicate does not belong. "All the planets, except Venus and Mercury, are beyond the earth's orbit" is clearly a proposition equivalent to two: that Venus and Mercury are not beyond the earth's orbit, and that the rest are. If the exceptions are not actually named, an exceptive proposition must often be treated as a particular one. Suppose I say "all the planets in our system except one agree with Bode's law" and do not name that one exception. Then the reader cannot, on the strength of the proposition, positively assert of any planet that it does agree with Bode's law.

Some propositions are distinguished as explicative, or essential, because they simply affirm of their subject a predicate that everyone who can define the subject knows belongs to it. Such propositions only unfold what is already contained in the subject. "A parallelogram has four sides and four angles" is an explicative, or essential, proposition. "London, which is the capital of England, is the largest city of Europe" contains two propositions, and one of them only directs our attention to a fact everyone may be supposed to know, namely that London is the capital of England.

Ampliative propositions, on the other hand, join a new predicate to the subject. So, for anyone who does not know how the cities of Europe compare in size, the last example contains an ampliative proposition. Most propositions are of this kind.

Tautologous, or Truistic, propositions are those that simply affirm the subject of itself and give no information whatever, as in "whatever is, is," or "what I have written, I have written."

It is no part of formal logic to teach us how to interpret the meanings of sentences as we meet them in our reading; that is more the work of the grammarian and the philologist. Logic deals with the relations between different propositions and the inferences that can be drawn from them. Even so, it is a good idea for the reader to get some familiarity with the real logical meaning of conventional or peculiar forms of expression, and a number of examples will be found in the Examples of Propositions at the end of Lesson 10, which the reader is asked to classify and treat as directed.

Besides the distinctions already noticed, it has long been usual to distinguish propositions as pure or modal. The pure proposition simply asserts that the predicate does or does not belong to the subject. The modal proposition asserts this cum modo, that is, "with a mode": with some indication of the mode or manner in which the predicate belongs to the subject. Any adverb of time, place, manner, degree and so on, or any expression equivalent to an adverb, makes a proposition modal. "Error is always in haste," "justice is ever equal," "a perfect man ought always to be conquering himself" are examples of modal propositions in this sense of the name. Other logicians, however, have taken a different view, and treat modality as the degree of certainty or probability with which a judgment is made and asserted. So we may say "an equilateral triangle is necessarily equiangular," "men are generally trustworthy," "a falling barometer probably indicates a coming storm," "Aristotle's lost treatises may possibly be recovered," and each of these assertions is made with a different degree of certainty, or modality. Dr. Thomson is no doubt right in holding that modality does not affect the copula of the proposition, and that the subject could only be properly dealt with in a work on Probable Reasoning.

Many logicians have also divided propositions into true and false, and this might well seem an important distinction. All the same, it lies wholly outside the logician's province to consider whether a proposition is true or not in itself. All the logician has to determine is the comparative truth of propositions, that is, whether one proposition is true when another is. Strictly speaking, logic has nothing to do with a proposition on its own. The work of reasoning consists only in converting or transforming certain propositions into certain others, and the truth of the conclusion is in question only insofar as it follows from the truth of what we shall call the premises. It is the duty of each special science, in its own sphere, to decide which propositions are true and which are false. If logic took that duty on itself, it would be just another name for the whole of knowledge.

See Mr. J. S. Mill's System of Logic, Book I, Chap. iv, which generally agrees with what is given above. Chapters V and VI contain Mill's views on the Nature and Import of Propositions, a subject that can be studied further in Mill's Examination of Sir William Hamilton's Philosophy, Chap. xviii; Hamilton's Lectures on Logic, No. XIII; and Mansel's Prolegomena Logica, Chap. II. But the subject is too metaphysical in character to be dealt with in this book.

Questions and Exercises

1. Define a proposition, and name the parts it is made of.

2. How are propositions classified?

3. Name the four kinds of categorical propositions, and their symbols.

4. Under which classes are singular and indefinite propositions placed?

5. List the most usual signs of the quantity of a proposition.

6. What are modal propositions according to the early logicians, and according to Thomson?

7. How far do logicians consider propositions with regard to their truth or falsity?

Lesson 9: The Opposition of Propositions

We have found that logicians recognize four distinct kinds of propositions: the Universal affirmative, the Particular affirmative, the Universal negative, and the Particular negative, usually marked by the symbols A, I, E, O. Now we need to compare these four kinds fairly closely, looking at what each one means and how each is used. That way we will learn clearly how the truth of one affects the truth of the others, and how the same truth can be put into different forms of words.

The proposition A expresses the fact that the thing, or class of things, denoted by the subject is included in the class of things denoted by the predicate, and forms part of it. So "all metals are elements" means that the metals form a part of the class of elements, but not the whole of it. In the 1870s, 63 elements were known altogether, and 48 of them were metals, so we cannot say that all elements are metals. In fact the proposition does not tell us anything about elements in general. It is not really about elements at all: metals are the subject, and it is the metals it gives us information about. The best way to show this is with a kind of diagram first used by the famous mathematician Euler, in his letters to a German princess. In Fig. 1, imagine the metals shut inside the small circle, rather as sheep might be shut in a pen; this circle holds all the metals and nothing else. Imagine the larger circle holding, in the same way, all the elements and nothing else. Since the small circle lies wholly inside the larger one, all the metals must be counted as elements. But about the part of the elements lying outside the circle of metals, the proposition tells us nothing.

The particular affirmative proposition I means exactly the same kind of thing as A, except that only part of the subject is brought into question. When I say that "some metals are brittle," I mean that if we gathered together all the different metals, at least a few could be picked out that would turn out to be brittle. But the word some is extremely vague. It shows neither exactly how many metals are brittle, nor how we are to tell them from the others, except, of course, by testing whether they are brittle. In Euler's way, this proposition is properly shown by two intersecting circles, one supposed to enclose all metals and the other all brittle substances. The mere fact that the two circles intersect proves that some part of one class must coincide with some part of the other, which is just what the proposition means to express. About the parts of the circles that do not overlap, the proposition tells us nothing.

The universal negative proposition E denies that there is any agreement or coincidence at all between the subject and the predicate. From "no metals are compound substances," for example, we learn that no metal is to be found among compound substances. And it follows necessarily that no compound substance can be found among the metals. For if there were a compound substance among the metals, there would plainly be at least one metal among the compound substances. Euler's method shows this complete separation of the two classes in thought very well, by two circles that do not touch.

The reader will easily see what sets the proposition E apart from A and I. E gives us some information about the whole of the predicate, because we learn that none of the things included in the predicate can be found among the things included in the subject. The affirmative propositions, on the other hand, entitled us to hold that the things denoted by the subject, or some particular part of them, were included in the predicate. But they give us no right to say that any particular part of the predicate is in the subject. If all we know about a substance is that it is an element, the proposition "all metals are elements" does not tell us whether it is a metal or not. And from the proposition "some metals are brittle" we certainly cannot find out whether any particular brittle substance is a metal. We have to get that information from somewhere else. But from "no metals are compounds" we learn, of any compound substance, that it is not a metal, and also, of any metal, that it is not a compound substance.

In technical language, this important difference is expressed by saying that the proposition E distributes its predicate, while the affirmative propositions A and I do not distribute their predicates. The distribution of a term simply means taking it universally, or referring to all parts of it. Whether an argument or syllogism is valid will usually depend on whether its terms are sufficiently distributed, so we cannot pay too much attention to this point.

Judging from the examples we have had, we can see that the universal affirmative distributes its subject, but not its predicate: it gives us some information about all metals, but not about all elements. The particular affirmative distributes neither subject nor predicate: our example teaches us nothing about all metals, or about all brittle substances. But the universal negative distributes both subject and predicate, because we learn something about all metals and also about all compound substances.

The particular negative proposition O turns out to distribute its predicate, but not its subject. When I say that some metals are not brittle, I deliberately refer to only part of the metals, and I shut them out of the class of brittle substances. But in doing so I cannot help referring at the same time to the whole of the brittle substances. If the metals in question coincided with any part at all of the brittle substances, they could not be said to be shut out of that class. To shut a thing out of any space, such as one particular room of a house, it is not enough to remove it from some part of the room; it must be kept out of every part, out of the whole of that space or room. Euler's diagram for this proposition can be drawn in the same way as the one for the proposition I, like this:

Here, although part of the metals falls inside the circle of brittle substances, the rest of the metals are shut out of every part of the predicate.

We can set out the result we have now reached in the following form:

Subject.Predicate.
UniversalAffirmative A.Distributed.Undistributed.
UniversalNegative E.Distributed.Distributed.
ParticularAffirmative I.Undistributed.Undistributed.
ParticularNegative O.Undistributed.Distributed.

Now we can very easily work out how the four propositions are related to one another, each to each; that is, the ways in which they are opposed to each other. Clearly, the truth of one proposition may interfere, more or less completely, with the truth of another proposition that has the same subject and predicate. If "all metals are elements," then it is impossible that "some metals are not elements," and still more obviously impossible, so to speak, that "no metals are elements." So the proposition A is inconsistent with both E and O, and, the other way around, E and O are inconsistent with A. In the same way, E is inconsistent with A and I. But there is an important difference to notice here. If A is false, O is necessarily true, but E may or may not be true. If it is not true that "all men are sincere," it follows that "some men are not sincere," but it does not in the least follow that "no men are sincere." We express this difference by saying that A and O are contradictory propositions, while A and E are called contrary propositions. A and E, as in "all men are sincere" and "no men are sincere," plainly stand for the greatest possible opposition of circumstances. To prove that A is false, it is enough to establish that O is true; it is unnecessary to prove E, even if that could be done. In the same way, E is disproved by proving I, and it is unnecessary to prove A. Anyone who asserts a universal proposition, either A or E, takes on the burden of explaining away or disproving every single exception that is brought against it. An opponent can always stick to the much easier job of finding cases that seem to contradict, or really do contradict, the universality of the statement. But if he takes it on himself to assert the direct contrary, he is the one open to easy attack. Suppose someone asserted, for instance, that "All Christians are more moral than Pagans." It would be easy to bring forward examples showing that "Some Christians are not more moral than Pagans." But it would be absurd to think it necessary to go to the opposite extreme and show that "No Christians are more moral than Pagans." In short, A is sufficiently, and best, disproved by O, and E by I. And it is easy to see that, the other way around, O is disproved by A, and I by E. In fact, there is no other way at all of disproving these particular propositions.

When we compare the propositions I and O, we find that they are opposed in nature in a certain sense, since one is affirmative and the other negative, and yet they are consistent with each other. It is true, for instance, that "Some metals are brittle": antimony, bismuth and arsenic, for example. But it is also true that "Some metals are not brittle." And the reader will notice that when I assert "Some metals are elements," nothing in this prevents "Some metals are not elements" from being true as well, although we know on other grounds that it is not true. The propositions I and O are each called the subcontrary of the other. The name implies a lesser degree of contrariety than there is between A and E.

As for the relation of A to I, and of E to O, the truth of the universal plainly includes and requires the truth of the particular. Whatever can be affirmed or denied of all parts of a class can certainly be affirmed or denied in the same way of some part of the class. But from the truth of the particular we have no right to infer either the truth or the falsity of the universal of the same quality. These pairs of propositions are called subalterns, meaning "one under the other" (from the Latin sub, under, and alter, the other of two). Or, to put it more exactly, I and O are the subalternates of A and E respectively, and each of A and E is a subalternans.

The relations of the propositions we have just described are all shown clearly in the following scheme:

It is so very important to grasp the consistency or opposition of propositions completely and quickly that I will put the matter in another form. If we take any two propositions with the same subject and predicate, they must come under one of the following statements:

1. Of contradictory propositions, one must be true and one false.

2. Of contrary propositions, both cannot be true, and both may be false.

3. Of subcontrary propositions, only one can be false, and both may be true.

4. Of subalterns, the particular is true if the universal is true; but the universal may or may not be true when the particular is true.

I put the same matter in yet another form in the following table, which shows how the truth of each of A, E, I and O affects the truth of each of the others.

A isE isI isO is
If A be truetruefalsetruefalse.
If E be truefalsetruefalsetrue.
If I be truedoubtfulfalsetruedoubtful.
If O be truefalsedoubtfuldoubtfultrue.

Clearly, we get more information from affirming universals than from affirming particulars. It follows that we can get more information from denying particulars than from denying universals: that is, fewer cases are left doubtful, as the table above shows.

The reader should be warned, though, against an ambiguity that has misled even some of the most eminent logicians. In particular propositions, the adjective some must be carefully understood as meaning some, and there may or may not be more, or all. If we took it to mean some, but not more and not all, it would really give the proposition the force of I and O combined. If I say "some men are sincere," I must not be taken to imply that "some men are not sincere." I must be understood to predicate sincerity of some men, and to leave the character of the rest entirely untouched. It follows that when I deny the truth of a particular, I must not be understood to imply the truth of the universal of the same quality. It might seem very natural to deny the truth of "some men are mortal," on the ground that not just some but all men are mortal. But then the proposition being denied would really be "some men are not mortal," that is, O, not I. So when I deny that "some men are immortal," I mean that "no men are immortal"; and when I deny that "some men are not mortal," I mean that "all men are mortal."

It has long been the custom to compare propositions according to the kind of subject matter they refer to. What is technically called the matter was divided into three kinds: necessary, contingent, and impossible. Necessary matter is any subject in which the proposition A can be affirmed; impossible matter is any subject in which E can be affirmed. Any subject or branch of knowledge in which universal statements usually cannot be made is called contingent matter, and it implies the truth of I and O. So "comets are subject to gravitation," although it is an indefinite or indesignate proposition (see Lesson 8), may be read as A, because it belongs to a part of natural science where general laws of that kind hold. But "men are sincere" would properly be read as particular, or I, because the matter is clearly contingent. The truth of the following statements is evident.

In necessary matter A and I are true; E and O false.

In contingent matter I and O are true; A and E false.

In impossible matter E and O are true; A and I false.

In reality, though, this part of logical doctrine is thoroughly illogical. As we have already explained (in Lesson 8), when we deal with a proposition we have no right to assume that we know the science it refers to. Our job is to draw out the exact consequences of whatever statements we are given. In logic we must learn to transform information in every possible way, but not to add facts from outside.

Questions and Exercises

1. State the quantity of the subject and of the predicate in each of the propositions A, E, I, O.

2. From the following propositions, pick out pairs of contrary, contradictory, subaltern, and subcontrary propositions:

(1) Some elements are known.

(2) No elements are known.

(3) All elements are known.

(4) Not all elements are known.

(5) Some elements are not known.

(6) All elements are not known.

3. What propositions are true, false, or doubtful:

(1) when A is false,

(2) when E is false,

(3) when I is false,

(4) when O is false?

4. Prove, by means of the contradictory propositions, that subcontrary propositions cannot both be false.

5. Show, by means of the subcontrary propositions, that contrary propositions may both be false.

6. What quantity would you assign to each of the following propositions?

(1) Knowledge is power.

(2) Nebulae are material bodies.

(3) Light is the vibration of an ether.

(4) Men are more to be trusted than we think.

(5) The Chinese are industrious.

7. Why is it better, in an argument, to refute a statement by its contradictory rather than by its contrary?

Lesson 10: Conversion of Propositions, and Immediate Inference

We infer whenever we draw one truth from another truth, or pass from one proposition to another. As Sir William Hamilton puts it, Inference is "the carrying out into the last proposition what was virtually contained in the antecedent judgments." Teaching the principles on which this act of inference must be carried out is the true business of logic. Everything we have said so far about terms and propositions is useful, or even relevant, only so far as it helps us understand how inference works. So we now have to look, one after another, at all the ways in which the same information can be recast into different forms of words, forms that often seem to say quite different things. Logicians do not agree exactly about what should be counted as inference and what should not. Everyone would agree that we make an inference when we see drops of water on the ground and believe that it has rained. That is a fairly complicated act of inference, and we will come to it in later lessons, under the heading of Induction. Few people, or none, would say that we make an inference when we pass from "The Duke of Cambridge is the Commander-in-chief" to "The Commander-in-chief is the Duke of Cambridge." (The Duke of Cambridge was then the head of the British Army.) But without worrying too much about what to call the process, I will point out in this lesson every way in which we can pass from a single proposition of the form A, E, I or O to another proposition.

We are said to convert a proposition when we swap its subject and predicate around. The proposition we get is called the converse, or converted proposition, and the one we started from is called the convertend, meaning the one to be converted. For the converse to be validly inferred from the convertend, we must keep two rules: (1) the quality of the proposition, affirmative or negative, must stay the same; and (2) no term may be distributed in the converse unless it was distributed in the convertend.

Suppose that in "all metals are elements" we simply swapped the terms, giving "all elements are metals." That would claim some knowledge about all elements. But we have already seen clearly that the predicate of A is undistributed, so the convertend really tells us nothing about all elements. All we can infer is that "some elements are metals." This converse obeys the rule, and the process by which we pass in this way from A to I is called Conversion by Limitation, or per accidens.

When the converse has exactly the same form as the convertend, the process is called simple conversion. From "some metals are brittle substances" I can infer "some brittle substances are metals," because every term here is undistributed. So I is simply converted into I.

Again, from "no metals are compounds" I can pass straight to "no compounds are metals." Both of these propositions are E propositions, so all their terms are distributed. Euler's diagram in Lesson 9 (Figure 3) shows clearly that if all the metals are kept apart from all the compounds, then all the compounds are necessarily kept apart from all the metals. So the proposition E is simply converted into E.

When we try to convert the proposition O, though, we run into a peculiar difficulty. Its subject is undistributed, and yet conversion would make that subject the predicate of a negative proposition, and a negative proposition distributes its predicate. Take the proposition "some existing things are not material substances." Converted directly, it would become "all material substances are not existing things," which is plainly absurd. The fallacy comes from the fact that "existing things" is distributed in the converse, while it was particular in the convertend; and the rules of Aristotle's logic do not allow us to put a sign of particular quantity in front of the predicate. The converse would be just as untrue and fallacious if we made the subject particular, as in "some material substances are not existing things." So we must conclude that the proposition O cannot be dealt with either by simple conversion or by conversion by limitation. We need a new process, which we may call Conversion by Negation. It consists in first changing the convertend into an affirmative proposition, and then converting it simply. If we attach the negation to the predicate instead of to the copula, our proposition becomes "some existing things are immaterial substances." Converting this simply, we get "some immaterial substances are existing things," and this can truly be inferred from the convertend. So the proposition O can be converted only by this exceptional method, by negation.

There is another process of conversion that can be applied to the proposition A, known as conversion by contraposition. From "all metals are elements" it necessarily follows that "all not-elements are not metals." If this is not obvious at first, a little thought will make it so. Figure 5 shows that if all the metals are among the elements, then whatever is not an element, that is, whatever lies outside the circle of elements, must also lie outside the circle of metals. If we may look ahead to what Lesson 23 contains, we can also prove that the contrapositive proposition is true in this way. Suppose something that is not-element were a metal. Then by the original proposition it would have to be an element, so it would be an element and not an element at once. That is impossible by the Primary Laws of Thought (Lesson 14), since nothing can both have and not have the same property. It follows that whatever is not-element must be not-metal.

It is easy to make mistakes in contrapositive conversion, for a reason that will become clearer in Lesson 22. We are very apt to infer from a proposition of the form "all metals are elements" that all not-metals are not-elements. That is not only a false statement in itself; it is not in the least justified by the original proposition. Figure 5 makes it plain that a thing which lies outside the circle of metals does not necessarily lie outside the circle of elements, which is wider than the circle of metals. All the same, people make this mistake often in everyday life. The reader will do well to remember that conversion by contraposition consists only in this: we take the negative of the predicate of the proposition A as a new subject, and we affirm of it, universally, the negative of the old subject.

Contrapositive conversion cannot be applied at all to the particular propositions I and O, nor to the proposition E as it stands. But we can change E into A by attaching the negation to the predicate, and then the process can be applied. So "no humans are perfect" can be changed into "all humans are not-perfect," that is, "are imperfect." Then by contraposition we infer "all not-imperfect beings are not-humans." But not-imperfect really means the same as perfect, so our new proposition really amounts to "all perfect beings are not humans," or "no perfect beings are humans" (E), which is the simple converse of the original proposition.

That leaves certain deductions that can be drawn from a proposition without converting its terms. We may call them immediate inferences, and Archbishop Thomson has described them very clearly in his "Outline of the Necessary Laws of Thought" (pp. 156, &c.).

Immediate Inference by Privative Conception consists in passing from any affirmative proposition to a negative proposition that is implied in it or equivalent to it, or the other way round, passing from a negative proposition to the affirmative that corresponds to it. It is also called Obversion.

The following table takes a proposition of each kind and changes it, by privative conception, into an equivalent proposition:

A all metals are elements.E no metals are compounds.
E no humans are perfect.A all humans are imperfect.
I some humans are trustworthy.O some humans are not untrustworthy.
O some humans are not trustworthy.I some humans are untrustworthy.

The truth of any of these can be clearly shown by diagrams. For instance, it will be plain that if the whole circle of metals lies inside the circle of elements, no part of it can lie outside that circle, among the compounds. Any of the propositions above can be converted, but the results will mostly be ones we have already obtained. The simple converse of "no metals are compounds" is "no compounds are metals," which is to say "no not-elements are metals," the contrapositive of "all metals are elements." From the last example in the table we also get, by simple conversion, "some untrustworthy beings are humans," which is obviously the converse by negation explained above. Applying this kind of conversion to "some humans are not untrustworthy," we get "some not-untrustworthy beings are humans." Finally, from "all humans are imperfect" we can obtain, by conversion by limitation, "some imperfect beings are humans."

Immediate Inference by Added Determinants consists in attaching an adjective, or some similar qualification, to both the subject and the predicate of a proposition, so that the meaning of each term becomes narrower, or more fully determined. As long as nothing else is altered, the truth of the new proposition follows necessarily from the truth of the original in almost every case.

So from "all metals are elements" we may infer that "all very heavy metals are very heavy elements." From "a comet is a material body" we infer "a visible comet is a visible material body." But if we use this kind of inference too boldly, we can arrive at fallacious and absurd results. From "all kings are men" we might infer "all incompetent kings are incompetent men." But it does not follow at all that someone who is incompetent as a king would be incompetent in other positions. In this case and many others, the qualifying adjective is apt to carry different meanings in the subject and in the predicate, and the inference is necessarily true only when the meaning is exactly the same in both. This kind of inference will seldom work with comparative terms. From "a cottage is a building," for example, we cannot infer "a huge cottage is a huge building," since a cottage may be large compared with other cottages but not compared with buildings in general.

Immediate Inference by Complex Conception is very like the last kind. It consists in using the subject and predicate of a proposition as parts of a more complex conception. From "all metals are elements" I can pass to "a mixture of metals is a mixture of elements." From "a horse is a quadruped" I infer "the skeleton of a horse is the skeleton of a quadruped." But here again the reader must beware of applying the process where the new complex conception means something different in the subject and in the predicate. From "all Protestants are Christians," it does not follow that "a majority of Protestants are a majority of Christians," nor that "the most excellent of the Protestants is the most excellent of the Christians."

I recommend that the student get thoroughly familiar with all the transformations of propositions, or immediate inferences, described in this lesson, and plenty of examples are given for the purpose. A good exercise is to put the same proposition through a series of changes so that it comes back to its original form at the end, which proves that all the changes along the way were sound. But if conversion by limitation has been used anywhere in the chain, the original universal proposition cannot be recovered, only the particular proposition that corresponds to it.

On Immediate Inference, see Archbishop Thomson, Outline of the Laws of Thought, §§ 85—92.

Questions and Exercises

8. Sort the propositions below into the following four groups (an expedient act is one that is useful or advantageous): a. Those that can be inferred from (1). b. Lesson 11: Logical Analysis of Sentences

The propositions we meet in ordinary writing and speech seldom show the simple form we have seen to be the proper logical construction: a subject, a copula and a predicate joined together. Often the copula is mixed up with the predicate. Often, too, several propositions are packed into one grammatical sentence. For a full account of how sentences are analyzed, I will point the reader to several excellent little books on the subject. Here I will only try to sketch the different ways a sentence can be built.

Footnote: W. S. Dalgleish's Grammatical Analysis, or J. D. Morell's Analysis of Sentences. Alex. Bain's English Composition and Rhetoric, pp. 91–117, deals with the construction of sentences.

In ordinary language the copula is so often joined to the predicate that grammarians treat a proposition as having only two parts: the subject, and the predicate, or verb. Take the proposition "The sun rises." It seems to contain nothing but a subject, "the sun," and a predicate, "rises." But it really means the same as "the sun is rising," where the copula is plainly shown. So we will treat the verb, or grammatical predicate, as containing both the copula and the logical predicate. In Latin a single word can hold all three parts of a proposition, as in sum, "I am." Caesar's famous exclamation Veni, vidi, vici, "I came, I saw, I conquered," holds three separate and complete propositions in three words. But these odd cases arise only because the parts of the proposition have been blended together and disguised inside one word. In the Latin sum, the letter m is a leftover of the pronoun me, which is the real subject of the proposition. If we knew the grammar of any language perfectly, it would probably not contradict the logical view of a sentence. Instead it would probably explain how the separate parts of the complete proposition got blended together and seemingly lost, just as the words will and not are blended in the everyday "I won't."

A grammatical sentence can contain any number of separate propositions. They could be pulled apart, but they are combined to save words. In the sentence

	"Art is long and Time is fleeting,"

there are two separate subjects, Art and Time, and two predicates, "long" and "fleeting." So we simply have two propositions joined by the conjunction and. But we can also have several separate subjects with one and the same predicate, as in

	"Thirty days hath September,
	April, June, and November."

In this well-known couplet the predicate, "having thirty days," is put first for emphasis, and there are four subjects, September, April and the rest, and the predicate is affirmed of each of them. So these lines really contain four separate propositions.

Or there can be one subject with several predicates, so that several different propositions are asserted without repeating the subject and the copula. The sentence

"Nitrogen is a colourless, tasteless, inodorous gas, slightly lighter than air," has only one subject, Nitrogen, but four or five predicates ("inodorous" means odorless). It plainly means the same as "Nitrogen is colourless," "Nitrogen is tasteless," "Nitrogen is a gas," and so on.

Last, we can have several subjects and several predicates all combined in one sentence with only one copula, so that each predicate is asserted of each subject. Then a great many separate propositions are squeezed into one short sentence. In the sentence "Iron, Copper, Lead and Zinc are abundant, cheap and useful metals," we clearly have four subjects, and we can say we have four predicates: "abundant," "cheap," "useful" and "metal." Nothing stops us from applying each predicate to each subject, so the sentence really contains 16 separate propositions in only 11 words: "Iron is abundant," "Iron is cheap," "Copper is abundant," "Copper is cheap," and so on. In this curious sentence,

"Hearts, tongues, figures, scribes, bards, poets, cannot think, speak, cast, write, sing, number, his love to Antony," Shakespeare has joined six subjects and six predicates, or verbs, so that, strictly speaking, there are six times six, or thirty-six, propositions.

Footnote: Antony and Cleopatra, Act III. Sc. i.

In all the cases above, the sentence is said to be compound, and the separate propositions combined in it are said to be coordinate with each other, that is, of the same order or rank. They are called that because they do not depend on each other or affect each other's truth in any way. There is no need to state how abundant, cheap or useful iron is in the same sentence as the qualities of copper, lead or zinc. But since the predicates happen to be the same, we save a good deal of trouble in speaking or writing by attaching as many subjects as we can to the same set of predicates. It is truly said that brevity is the soul of wit, and one of the great arts of writing is to pack as many statements as possible into the fewest words, as long as the meaning does not get muddled.

Propositions are combined in a completely different way when one proposition forms part of the subject or the predicate of another. In the sentence "The man who is upright need not fear accusation," there are two verbs and two propositions, but one of them only describes the subject of the other.
