"Who is upright" clearly limits the predicate "need not fear accusation" to one part of the class "man." The meaning of the whole sentence could be put like this:

	"The upright man need not fear accusation."

Here we can clearly see that the clause, or apparent proposition, has taken the place of an adjective. A clause or proposition like this is called subordinate, because it only helps to build the principal sentence and has no meaning apart from it. Any sentence that contains a subordinate clause is said to be complex. Almost any part of a sentence can be replaced by a subordinate clause in this way. In "Oxygen and Nitrogen are the gases which form the largest part of the atmosphere," a subordinate clause makes up part of the predicate, and the meaning could be expressed almost as well like this: "Oxygen and Nitrogen are the gases forming the largest part of the atmosphere."

Take a modal proposition (see Lesson 8), one that states the manner in which the predicate belongs to the subject. Its mode can be expressed either by an adverb or by a subordinate clause. "As a man lives so he dies" is a proposition of this kind. It means "a man dies as he lives," and "as he lives" does the job of an adverb: if he lives well, he dies well; if he lives badly, he dies badly. Adverbs or adverbial clauses can also specify the time, the place, or any other circumstance the truth of the main proposition depends on.

Assuming the reader knows the grammatical terms involved, we can now list the parts that even the most complex sentence must be made of. The subject may be made up of:

1. A noun, as in "The Queen reigns."

2. A pronoun, as in "She reigns."

3. An adjective turned into a noun, as in "The brave are honored."

4. A gerund, as in "Seeing is believing."

5. An infinitive, as in "To see is to believe."

6. A subordinate clause, as in "Who falls from virtue is lost."

The subject may be qualified or restricted by joining an attribute to it, and the attribute can be expressed in any of these ways:

1. An adjective, as in "Fresh air is wholesome."

2. A participle, as in "Falling stars are often seen."

3. A noun used as an adjective, as in "Iron ships are now much employed."

4. A noun and a preposition, as in "ships of iron are now much employed."

5. A possessive case, as in "Chatham's son was the great minister Pitt."

6. A noun in apposition, as in "The Metropolis London is the most populous of cities."

7. A gerund or a dative infinitive (an infinitive saying what something is for), as in "The desire to go abroad is common in Englishmen."

The predicate is almost always made up of a verb, which often has an object or some qualifying words. So it may be:

1. A simple tense of a complete verb, as in "The sun rises."

2. A compound tense, as in "The sun has risen."

3. An incomplete verb and its complement, as in "The sea seems rough."

4. The verb "to be" and an adjective, as in "Time is fleeting."

5. A verb with an object, as in "Warmth melts ice."

6. A verb with an adverbial, as in "The snow falls thickly."

The object of a verb is usually a noun or a pronoun, but any of the six kinds of expression that can serve as a subject can also serve as an object.

The adverbial that qualifies a verb, and expresses the manner, time, place or some other circumstance affecting the proposition, may be:

1. An adverb, as in "The days pass slowly."

2. A noun and a preposition, as in "The resolution was passed by a large majority."

3. An absolute phrase, as in "The snow melts, the sun having risen."

4. A dative infinitive, as in "She stoops to conquer."

5. Any phrase that does the work of an adverb, as in "The dividends are paid twice a year."

People have invented various ways of showing how sentences are built, using symbols and names for the different parts. But I believe that by far the simplest and most effective way is to show the construction as a diagram. Any two or more parts of a sentence that are coordinate with each other, or that stand in the same relation to some other part, are written side by side and joined by a bracket. So the diagram

shows clearly that there are four coordinate subjects and four coordinate predicates in the example we took earlier.

Whenever one part of a sentence is subordinate to another part, we can connect the two with a line drawn in whatever direction is convenient. The analysis of the following sentence is easily shown by the diagram below it:

"No one who is a lover of money, a lover of pleasure, and a lover of glory, is likewise a lover of mankind; but only he who is a lover of virtue."

We see that the sentence is both compound and complex. That is, it contains two principal coordinate propositions with a shared predicate, "a lover of mankind." The first proposition is negative, and its subject is described by three subordinate clauses. The second proposition is affirmative and has one subordinate clause.

I will end this rather long lesson by analyzing a few sentences. The first is made up of some remarkably complex lines from a poem by Burbidge:

	"He who metes, as we should mete,
	Could we His insight use, shall most approve,
	Not that which fills most space in earthly eyes,
	But what—though Time scarce note it as he flies—
	Fills, like this little daisy at my feet,
	Its function best of diligence in love."
	"Most sweet it is with unuplifted eyes
	    To pace the ground, if path there be or none,
	While a fair region round the traveller lies
	    Which he forbears again to look upon;
	Pleased rather with some soft ideal scene,
	    The work of fancy, or some happy tone
	Of meditation slipping in between,
	    The beauty coming, and the beauty gone."
	                                        Wordsworth.

In this sentence there is clearly one subject, "to pace the ground," which is connected by the pronoun it to the predicate "most sweet." But the main part of the sentence is made up of three adverbials, which express the manner and the surrounding circumstances, and the third adverbial is developed in a very complicated way. The sentence is not compound, but it is complex, because of its four subordinate propositions.

In the next sentence there is, strictly speaking, only one principal proposition, "We find." But this is only a way of introducing what the sentence is really saying: "the two classes of intellectual operations have much that is different, much that is common."

"When the notions with which men are conversant in the common course of life, which give meaning to their familiar language and which give employment to their hourly thoughts, are compared with the ideas on which exact science is founded, we find, that the two classes of intellectual operations have much that is different, much that is common."

Here "the two classes" make a collective term, and they have two coordinate predicates, which so far makes the sentence a compound one. But most of the sentence is a complicated subordinate sentence that works like an adverbial: it expresses the time or occasion when this is found to be the case.

As a last example we take the sentence below:

"The law of gravitation, the most universal truth at which human reason has yet arrived, expresses not merely the general fact of the mutual attraction of all matter; not merely the vague statement that its influence decreases as the distance increases, but the exact numerical rate at which that decrease takes place; so that when its amount is known at any one distance it may be exactly calculated for any other."

Questions and Exercises

1. How does the grammatical predicate differ from the logical predicate?

2. Explain the difference between a compound sentence and a complex sentence, and between coordinate and subordinate propositions.

3. List the grammatical expressions that can form

(1) A subject.

(2) An attribute.

(3) A predicate.

(4) An object.

(5) An adverbial.

4. Examine the following sentences. Work out which are compound and which are complex, and point out the coordinate or subordinate propositions.

(1) Happy is the man that findeth wisdom, and the man that getteth understanding.

(2) Heat, being motion, can be converted into mechanical force.

(3) Ceres, Pallas, Juno, and Vesta are minor planets, or asteroids.

(4) Knowledge comes, but wisdom lingers.

(5) Fortune often sells to the hasty what she gives to those who wait.

(6) Thousands at His bidding speed, And post o'er land and ocean without rest; They also serve who only stand and wait.

(7) Pride that dines on vanity, sups on contempt.

(8) Nobody can be healthful without exercise, neither natural body, nor politic.

(9) Nature is often hidden, sometimes overcome, seldom extinguished.

(10) It is impossible to love and be wise.

(11) Though gods they were, as men they died.

(12) He that is not industrious envieth him that is.

(13) Ye are my friends, if ye do whatsoever I command you.—John 15:14.

(14) The wisdom that is from above is first pure, then peaceable, gentle, and easy to be intreated, full of mercy, and good fruits, without partiality, and without hypocrisy.—James 3:17.

5. Analyze any of the following sentences in the form of a scheme or diagram:

(1) The first aphorism of Bacon's Novum Organum, quoted in Lesson 27.

(2) Some judgments are merely explanatory of their subject, having for their predicate, a conception which it fairly implies, to all who know and can define its nature.

(3) There be none of the affections which have been noted to fascinate or bewitch, but love and envy: they both have vehement wishes; they frame themselves readily into imaginations and suggestions; and they come easily into the eye, especially upon the presence of the objects, which are the points that conduce to fascination, if any such there be.

For more examples to analyze, see Dalgleish's Grammatical Analysis with Progressive Exercises (Oliver and Boyd, Edinburgh, 1866; price 9d.).

Lesson 12: The Predicables, Division, and Definition

Before we go any further, the reader should get a precise grasp of a few logical terms known as the Predicables. These are the kinds of terms, or attributes, that can always be predicated of any subject. There are five of them: genus, species, difference, property, and accident. Used properly, they are extremely useful and important in logic. Ancient writers gave the predicables many different and subtle meanings, and it would be neither possible nor worthwhile to try to describe them all in this book. All we can give here is the simplest and most useful view of the subject.

Any class of things can be called a genus (from the Greek γένος, genos, race or kind) if we think of it as made up of two or more species. "Element" is a genus when we think of it as divided into the two species "metallic and non-metallic." Triangle is a genus with respect to the species acute-angled, right-angled, and obtuse-angled.

A species, on the other hand, is any class that we think of as forming part of the next larger class. So the terms genus and species are relative to each other. The genus is the larger class that gets divided, and the species are the two or more smaller classes it is divided into.

It is essential, though, to take these words in the two senses of extension and intension. From the explanation of those two senses in Lesson 5, it should be clear that the extent of a genus or species is simply the number of individuals it includes, and that there will always be fewer individuals in the species than in the genus. In extent, the genus book includes all books of every size, language, and subject. If we divide it by size, the species of book are folio, quarto, octavo, duodecimo, and so on (the old printers' names for book sizes, from the number of times the printed sheet was folded). Each of these species, of course, contains far fewer individual books than the whole genus.

In intension, the genus means not the individual things it contains but the sum of the qualities common to all those things, enough to mark them out clearly from other classes. In the same way, the species means the sum of the qualities common to all the individuals in that species, enough to mark them out from the rest of the genus as well as from everything else. So there must be more qualities implied in the meaning of the species than in the meaning of the genus. The species must contain all the qualities of the genus, plus some extra quality or qualities that tell the species apart from one another. These extra qualities form the difference. The difference can be defined as the quality, or sum of qualities, that marks out one part of a genus from the other part or parts. The difference (in Latin differentia, in Greek διαφορά, diaphora) has a meaning only in intension. When we use all these terms purely in intension, we can say that the difference added to the genus makes the species. If "building" is the genus, for example, and we add the difference "used for a dwelling," we get the species "house." If we take "triangle" as the genus, it means the sum of the qualities "three-sided rectilineal figure" (a figure bounded by straight lines). If we add the quality "having two sides equal," we get the species "isosceles triangle."

It is easy to see that the same class of things can be a genus and a species at the same time, depending on whether we think of it as divided into smaller classes or as part of a larger class. Triangle, which is a genus with respect to isosceles triangle, is a species with respect to right-lined geometrical figures. House is a species of building, but a genus with respect to mansion, cottage, villa, and the other kinds of houses. In fact we can have an almost endless chain of genera and species, each class being a species of the class just above it and a genus with respect to the class just below. Take the genus British subject. It has the species Born in the United Kingdom, Colonial-born, and Naturalized. Each of these becomes a genus with respect to the species male and female. Each of those species can be divided again into adult and minor; educated and uneducated; employed in some occupation or unemployed; self-supporting, supported by friends, or a pauper (living on public poor relief); and so on. The subdivision can go on like this until we reach a class so narrow in extent that it cannot be divided any further except into individuals. That class is called the lowest species, or infima species. All the genera and species in between are called subaltern (from the Latin sub, under, and alter, the other of two), because they stand one under another. If there is a genus that is not thought of as a species, that is, as part of any higher genus, it is called the summum genus, the highest genus, or genus generalissimum, the most general genus.

It is doubtful whether we can really put any such upper limit on the chain of classes. The class British subject is certainly not an absolute summum genus, since it is only a species of man, which is a species of animal, living being, part of the earth, substance, and so on. If there were any real summum genus, it would probably be "Being," or "Thing," or "Object that can be conceived." But we can usefully use the term to mean the highest class of things taken in by any science or classification. So "material substance" is the summum genus that the science of chemistry examines, and "inhabitant of the United Kingdom" is the summum genus that the British census counts and classifies. Logical terms are only a species of words or phrases, but they are the summum genus as far as logic is concerned, because logic has nothing to do with the parts of speech or with the relations of words, syllables, and letters that grammarians study.

Several very useful expressions come from the words genus and species. When a thing is so peculiar and so unlike other things that it can't easily be put into one class with them, we say it is sui generis, or "of its own genus." The rings of Saturn, for instance, are so different from anything else among the heavenly bodies that they can fairly be called sui generis. In zoology, the Ornithorhynchus, or Australian duck-bill (the platypus), the Amphioxus (the lancelet), and a few other animals are so peculiar that they can be called sui generis. When a substance is the same in all its parts, or when a number of things are all alike, we say they are homogeneous (from the Greek ὁμός, homos, like, and γένος, genos, kind), that is, of the same nature. Otherwise they can be called heterogeneous (from the Greek ἕτερος, heteros, other).

We must carefully separate the purely logical use of genus and species from their special use in natural history. There, a species is a class of plants or animals supposed to have descended from common parents and to be the narrowest class that has a fixed form, and the genus is the next class up. But if we accept Darwin's theory of the origin of species, this definition of species becomes entirely illusory, because according to that theory different genera and species must have descended from common parents. Species then means just an arbitrary amount of resemblance that naturalists choose to settle on, and that can't be defined any more exactly. So this use of the word has nothing whatever to do with the logical use, in which any class of things at all is a species, as long as we think of it as part of a wider class, or genus.

The fourth of the Predicables is Property (in Latin proprium, in Greek ἴδιον, idion, one's own). It is hard to define in a way that is free from objections and difficulties, but perhaps the best description is this: a property is any quality that is common to the whole of a class but is not needed to mark that class out from other classes. For example, it is a property of the genus "triangle" that its three inside angles add up to two right angles. This is a very remarkable fact, and it is always true of triangles. But it is not made part of the genus, and it is not used in defining a triangle, because having three straight sides is enough of a mark. Geometrical figures have a great many properties. The Second Book of Euclid is taken up with proving a few properties of rectangles, and the Third Book does the same for circles. As we usually use the word, a property may or may not belong to other things as well as the ones in question. Some properties of the circle may also belong to the ellipse, and some properties of human beings, such as the power of memory or of anger, may belong to other animals too.

Logicians have invented various subtle divisions of properties, but it is enough to say this. A peculiar property is one that belongs to the whole of a class and to that class only, as laughter is supposed to belong only to human beings; the property of enclosing the greatest area within a boundary of a given length is peculiar to circles. A property that is not peculiar may belong to other classes of things as well as to the class it is called a property of. We can also distinguish the Generic Property, which belongs to the whole of the genus, from the Specific Property, which belongs to the whole of a lowest species.

Last, an accident (in Latin accidens, in Greek συμβεβηκός, symbebekos) is any quality that may belong to a class or not, as the case may be, without affecting the class's other qualities. The word means what befalls or happens by chance, and has no necessary connection with the nature of a thing. The absolute size of a triangle, for example, is a pure accident as far as its geometrical properties go. Whether the side of a triangle is 1/10 of an inch or a million miles, whatever Euclid proves true of one is true of the other. A man's birthplace is an accident with respect to him, and so are the clothes he is wearing, the position he is resting in, and so on. Some writers distinguish separable and inseparable accidents. The clothes a man is wearing are a separable accident, because they can be changed, and so can his position and many other circumstances. But his birthplace, his height, his first name, and so on are inseparable accidents, because they can never be changed, even though they have no necessary or important connection with his general character.

To illustrate part of the scheme of classification described under the name of the Predicables, I will give here, as logic textbooks usually do, the Tree of Porphyry. It is a sort of model classification invented by one of the early Greek logicians, Porphyrius (Porphyry). I have simplified the usual form of it by translating the Latin names and leaving out unnecessary words.

In this Tree we see a succession of genera and species: Substance, Body, Living Being, Animal, and Man. Of these, Substance is the summum genus, because it is not treated as a species of any higher class. Man is the infima species, because it is a class that is not divided into any lower class, only into individuals, and the usual individuals to name are Socrates and Plato. Body, Living Being, and Animal are called subaltern genera and species, because each one is a species with respect to the next genus up and a genus with respect to the next species down. The qualities meant by the adjectives Corporeal, Animate, Sensible (that is, capable of feeling), and Rational are the successive differences that divide each genus into species. Notice that the negative parts of the genera, namely Incorporeal Substance, Inanimate Body, and so on, could also be subdivided. That has not been done, to avoid cluttering the figure.

Logical division is the name for the process by which we pick out the species a genus is made up of. We are said to divide the genus "book," for instance, when we think of it as made up of the groups folio, quarto, octavo, duodecimo books, and so on. The size of the books is in this case the ground, basis, or principle of division, usually called the Fundamentum Divisionis. For a quality or circumstance to serve as the basis of a division, it must be present in some members and absent in others, or it must vary among the different species the genus contains. A generic property, of course, is present in the whole of the genus, so it can't be used for dividing. A sound and useful division must obey three rules:

1. The constituent species must exclude each other.

2. The constituent species, added together, must be equal to the genus.

3. The division must be founded on one principle or basis.

It would obviously be absurd to divide books into folio, quarto, French, German, and dictionaries. These species overlap: there may be French or German dictionaries that happen to be quarto or folio, and so belong to three different species at once. A division like this is called a Cross Division, because it uses more than one principle of division, and so the species cross one another and cause confusion. If I divided rectilineal figures into triangles, parallelograms, rectangles, and polygons of more than four sides, I would commit every possible fault in a single division. The species parallelogram and rectangle don't exclude each other, since all rectangles must be parallelograms. The species added together don't make up the whole genus rectilineal figure, since irregular four-sided figures that are not parallelograms have been left out. And there are three principles of division at work: the number of sides, the directions of those sides, and the angles they contain. But when we subdivide, treating each species as a genus that can be split up further, we may, and in fact must, use a new principle of division each time. So I can divide rectilineal figures according to the three principles mentioned above:

Here the principles of division are the number of sides and, for four-sided figures, whether their sides are parallel. Triangles can't be divided in this second respect. We can make a further division of parallelograms, taking the equality of the sides and the size of the angles as the principles, like this:

From a logical point of view, the most perfect divisions come from dividing each genus again and again into two species by a difference. The Tree of Porphyry is an example of this. The process is called Dichotomy (from the Greek δίχα, dicha, in two, and τέμνω, temno, to cut). It is also called Exhaustive Division, because it always and necessarily obeys the second rule and provides a place for every possible thing. By a Law of Thought that we will consider in Lesson 14, everything must either have a given quality or not have it, so it must fall into one part or the other of the genus. This process of exhaustive division will turn out to be quite important in Lesson 23. In practice, though, it is by no means always necessary or convenient. For instance, it would make a needlessly long classification if we divided rectilineal figures like this:

We know beyond all doubt that every figure must have 3, 4, 5, 6, or more sides, and that no figure can belong to more than one of these groups. So it is much better to list the parts straight away: Triangle, Quadrilateral, Pentagon, Hexagon, and so on. Again, it would be very awkward to divide the counties of England into Middlesex and not-Middlesex, the second of these into Surrey and not-Surrey, and that again into Kent and not-Kent. Dichotomy is useless in cases like these, and even looks absurd, because a much shorter division lets us obey the rules of division with certainty. But in less certain branches of knowledge, our divisions can never be safe from a possible oversight unless they proceed by dichotomy. Suppose we divide the population of the world into three branches, Aryan (Indo-European), Semitic, and Turanian, as the scholars of the 1870s did. Some race might eventually be discovered that is distinct from all of these, and for which no place has been provided. But suppose we had proceeded like this:

Then the new race would obviously fall into the last group, the one that is neither Aryan, Semitic, nor Turanian. All the divisions of naturalists are exposed to this inconvenience. If we divide Vertebrate Animals into Mammalia, Birds, Reptiles, and Fish, a new form might be discovered at any time that belongs to none of these, and so upsets the division.

One more precaution is needed in Division: don't go straight from a high or wide genus to a low or narrow species. As the saying goes, divisio non faciat saltum, "the division should not make a leap." The species should always be those of the proximate genus, the genus immediately above them. It would obviously be awkward, for instance, to begin by dividing geometrical figures into those that have parallel sides and those that don't. Yet this principle of division is perfectly proper when it is applied to the proximate genus.

Logical division must not be confused with physical division, or Partition, in which an individual object, such as a tree, is thought of as made up of its separate parts: root, trunk, branches, leaves, and so on. There is even a third, distinct process, called Metaphysical Division, which consists in thinking of a thing as a collection of qualities and separating these in our minds, as when we distinguish the shape, color, taste, and smell of an orange.

Closely connected with the subject of this Lesson is the process of Logical Definition, by which we settle the common qualities, or marks, of the objects that belong to a given class. A definition must give the briefest possible statement of the qualities that are enough to distinguish the class from other classes and to fix its position in the general classification of our ideas. We can do this by treating the class as a species and giving the proximate genus and the difference. Here the word genus is used in its intensive meaning: it stands for the qualities that belong to everything in the genus and are enough to mark those things out. Since the difference then marks out the part of the genus we want, we get a perfect definition of the species. But we should be careful not to put any superfluous marks into a definition. If the extra marks are accidents and do not belong to the whole class, the definition is wrongly narrowed, as it would be if we defined Quadrilateral Figures as figures with four equal sides. If the extra marks belong to everything being defined, they are Properties, and they make no difference to the definition at all. Suppose I define parallelograms as "four-sided rectilineal figures, with the opposite sides equal and parallel, and the opposite angles equal." I have added two properties, the equality of the opposite sides and the equality of the opposite angles. These follow necessarily from the sides being parallel, and they only make the definition more complicated without making it any more precise.

Logic books usually give certain rules that sum up the precautions needed in definition.

1. A definition should state the essential attributes of the species defined. As far as the expression "essential attributes" can be given any exact meaning, it means, as explained above, the proximate genus and the difference.

2. A definition must not contain the name defined. The purpose of a definition is to make the species known, and as long as the species is not known, it can't serve to make itself known. When this rule is broken, there is said to be circulus in definiendo, or "a circle in defining," because the definition brings us right back round to the word we started from. This fault is usually committed by putting into the definition a word that is really a synonym of the name being defined. Examples would be defining "Plant" as "an organized being possessing vegetable life," or elements as simple substances, since vegetable is really equivalent to plant, and simple to elementary. If I defined metals as "substances possessing metallic luster," I would either be committing this fault, or else using the term metallic luster in a sense that would let in other substances too, and so breaking the next rule.

3. The definition must be exactly equivalent to the species defined. That is, it must be an expression whose denotation is neither narrower nor wider than the species, so that it takes in exactly the same objects. In short, the definition must denote the species, the whole species, and nothing but the species, and this can really be taken as a description of what a definition is.

4. A definition must not be expressed in obscure, figurative, or ambiguous language. In other words, every term used in the definition must be exactly known. Otherwise the purpose of the definition, which is to make us acquainted with the sufficient marks of the species, is obviously defeated. There is no worse logical fault than to define ignotum per ignotius, the unknown by the still more unknown. Aristotle's definition of the soul as "The Entelechy, or first form of an organized body which has potential life," certainly seems open to this objection.

5. Last, a definition must not be negative where it can be affirmative. This rule, though, often does not apply, and it is by no means always binding.

On the nature of Classification and the five Predicables, read Mr. J. S. Mill, System of Logic, Book I. Chap. VII. For the ancient Scholastic views on Definition, see Mansel's Artis Logicae Rudimenta (Aldrich), App. Note C.

Questions and Exercises

1. Define each of the five predicables.

2. In what sense can we say that the genus is part of the species, and in what sense that the species is part of the genus?

3. From the terms in question 6 of Lesson 5, pick out the ones that are genera, species, highest genera, or lowest species of other terms.

4. Explain the expressions sui generis, homogeneous, heterogeneous, summum genus, infima species, Tree of Porphyry.

5. Name a property and an accident of each of the following classes: Circle, Planet, Bird, Member of Parliament, Ruminant Animal.

6. What are the rules of correct logical division?

7. The first name in each of the following series of terms is a class. Divide and subdivide it so as to include all the smaller classes listed after it, in accordance with the laws of division.

	(1) People: Laity, Aliens, Naturalized Subjects, Peers, Natural-born Subjects, Clergy, Baronets, Commons.
	(2) Triangle: Equiangular, Isosceles, Right-angled, Scalene, Obtuse-angled.
	(3) Reasoning: Induction (Imperfect), Deduction, Mediate Inference, Induction, Hypothetical Syllogism, Disjunctive Syllogism.

8. Divide any of the following classes: Governments, Sciences, Logical terms, Propositions.

9. What does a logical definition consist of?

10. What are the rules of correct definition?

11. Which rules do the following definitions break?

(1) Life is the sum of the vital functions.

(2) Genus is the material part of the species.

(3) Illative conversion is that in which the truth of the converse can be inferred from that of the convertend.

(4) Mineral substances are those which have not been produced by the powers of vegetable or animal life.

(5) An equilateral triangle is a triangle whose sides and angles are respectively equal.

(6) An acute-angled triangle is one which has an acute angle.

Lesson 13: Pascal and Descartes on Method

It is doubtful whether anyone has ever had a sharper or more perfect mind than Blaise Pascal. He was born in 1623 at Clermont, in Auvergne, and showed signs of a remarkable character from his earliest years. At first his father tried to stop him studying geometry. But Pascal's genius for the subject, and his love of it, were so strong that by the age of twelve he had worked out many of the propositions of Euclid's first book by himself, with no help from any person or any book. It is hard to say what he deserves most admiration for: his mathematical discoveries, his invention of the first calculating machine, his wonderful Provincial Letters written against the Jesuits, or his profound Pensées, or Thoughts, a collection of his reflections on scientific and religious subjects.

Among these Thoughts is a remarkable fragment on logical method, and the substance of it also appears in the Port Royal Logic. It is the second article of the Pensées, and its title is Réflexions sur la géométrie en général (Reflections on Geometry in General). I know of no piece of writing that comes closer to perfect truth and perfect clearness of expression. So in this lesson I am going to give a free translation of the most important parts of this fragment, and add to it the rules of method from the Port Royal Logic and from Descartes's famous Essay on Method. Pascal's words are nearly as follows.

"The true method would give us demonstrations of the highest excellence, if only it were possible to use it fully. It consists in keeping two main rules. The first rule is never to use any term whose meaning we have not clearly explained. The second rule is never to put forward any proposition that we cannot demonstrate from truths already known. In a word: define all the terms, and prove all the propositions. But if I am to keep the rules of the very method I am explaining, I must first say what is to be understood by Definition.

"In geometry we recognize only the definitions that logicians call Nominal Definitions. These are definitions that give a name to things that have been clearly pointed out in terms that are perfectly understood. Those are the only definitions I am talking about."

What such definitions are worth, and what they are for, is to make what we say clearer and shorter. They let us "express in the one name we give what could otherwise be said only in several words. The condition is that the name we give must be stripped of any other meaning it might have, so that it carries only the meaning we intend it to stand for.

"For example, suppose we need to tell apart the numbers that can be divided into two equal parts from those that cannot. To avoid repeating this distinction over and over, we give it a name, like this: we call every number that can be divided into two equal parts an Even Number.

"This is a geometrical definition. We have first clearly pointed out a thing, namely any number that can be divided into two equal parts. Then we give it a name, stripped of every other meaning the name might have, so that it carries only the meaning we have pointed out.

"It follows that definitions are entirely free, and that they can never be contradicted. Nothing could be more permissible than giving any name we like to a thing we have clearly pointed out. We need only take care not to abuse this freedom to name things by giving the same name to two different things. Even that would be permissible, as long as we did not mix up the results and carry what is true of one over to the other. But if we do fall into this fault, we have a sure and unfailing remedy. It is to put the definition, in our minds, in place of the thing defined. We must keep the definition so constantly in mind that every time we speak of an even number, say, we understand exactly that it is a number divisible into two equal parts. The two must be so joined and inseparable in our thinking that whenever one is spoken of, the mind goes straight to the other.

"Geometers, and everyone who works methodically, give names to things only to make what they say shorter, not to reduce or alter their ideas of the things they are talking about. They expect the mind always to fill in the whole definition behind the short terms, which they use only to avoid the confusion that a mass of words would cause.

"Nothing stops the cunning fallacies of the sophists faster or more effectively than this method. We should always use it, and it is enough by itself to clear away every kind of difficulty and equivocation.

"With these things well understood, I return to my explanation of the true method, which, as I said, consists in defining everything and proving everything.

"This method would certainly be an excellent one, if it were not absolutely impossible. Plainly, the first terms we tried to define would need earlier terms to explain them. In the same way, the first propositions we tried to prove would depend on other propositions that came before them in our knowledge. So it is clear that we would never reach the first terms or the first propositions.

"So as we push our inquiries further and further back, we necessarily arrive at basic words we cannot define, and at principles so clear that we cannot find any clearer ones to prove them by. It follows that human beings are by nature, and unavoidably, unable to treat any science whatever by a perfect method. But it does not follow that we should give up every kind of method. The most perfect method open to us is neither to define everything and demonstrate everything, nor to define nothing and demonstrate nothing. It is to take the middle course: not to define things that are clear and understood by everyone, but to define all the rest; and not to prove truths that everyone knows, but to prove all the rest. People go wrong in this method both ways: those who try to define and prove everything, and those who fail to do it for things that are not self-evident."

This admirable passage makes one thing plain: as long as we use words, we can never avoid appealing in the end to things themselves. Every definition of a word needs one or more other words, and those words need defining too, and so on forever. Nor may we ever circle back to words we have already defined. If we define A by B, and B by C, and C by D, and then D by A, we commit what may be called a circulus in definiendo, a "circle in defining." This is a very serious fallacy, because it can lead us to think we know the nature of A, B, C and D when in fact we know nothing about them at all.

Pascal summed up his view of the geometrical method clearly in the following rules, which he put into the Port Royal Logic.

Footnote: Mr. Spencer Baynes's translation, p. 317.

1. To admit no terms in the least obscure or equivocal without defining them.

2. To employ in the definitions only terms perfectly known or already explained.

3. To demand as axioms only truths perfectly evident.

4. To prove all propositions which are at all obscure, by employing in their proof only the definitions which have preceded, or the axioms which have been accorded, or the propositions which have been already demonstrated, or the construction of the thing itself which is in dispute, when there may be any operation to perform.

5. Never to abuse the equivocation of terms by failing to substitute for them, mentally, the definitions which restrict and explain them.

The reader will easily see that these rules are much easier to lay down than to follow. Even geometers do not agree about which are the simplest axioms to assume, or which are the best definitions to make. There are many different opinions about the true definition of parallel lines, and about the simplest assumptions to make about their nature. If that is so in geometry, how much harder must it be to follow Pascal's rules with any confidence in the less certain branches of science? After geometry, mechanics is perhaps the most perfect science. Yet the best authorities have been far from agreeing on the exact definitions of such ideas as force, mass, moment, power and inertia, and very different opinions are still held about the simplest axioms from which the law of the composition of forces can be proved. All the same, if we keep steadily in mind, while studying each science, that we need to define every term as far as we can, and to prove every proposition that can be proved from a simpler one, we will do a great deal to clear away error and confusion.

I also want to give here the rules that the famous Descartes proposed for guiding the reason toward the truth. They are as follows:

1. Never to accept anything as true unless we clearly know it to be true. That is, we must carefully avoid haste and prejudice, and include nothing more in our judgments than what presents itself to the mind so clearly and distinctly that we have no room at all to doubt it.

2. To divide each difficulty we examine into as many parts as possible, or as many as are needed to solve it.

3. To direct our thoughts in an orderly way, starting with the simplest objects and the easiest to know, so as to climb step by step to knowledge of the most complex.

4. In every case, to make our lists so complete and our reviews so wide that we can be sure we have left nothing out.

Descartes first stated these rules in his admirable Discourse on Method, where he sets out his thoughts on the right way to conduct the reason and to search for truth in any of the sciences. This little book is easy to get hold of in the original French, and it has also been translated into English by Mr. Veitch. I strongly advise the reader to study it. It is impossible always to follow the rules of Descartes and Pascal, or even to know whether we are following them properly in every case. But it must still be valuable to know what we ought to aim at.

Footnote: Published at Edinburgh in 1850.

Read Locke's short Essay on the Conduct of the Understanding, which contains admirable remarks on how to acquire exact and logical habits of thought.

Questions and Exercises

(1) What is the use of nominal definitions?

(2) How must we use definitions in order to avoid confusion?

(3) How far can we be said to be free to use any name for any object?

(4) According to Pascal, what is the true method of avoiding error?

(5) How do we learn the meanings of words that cannot be defined?

(6) Give examples of words that can be clearly defined, and of others that cannot.

(7) State the five rules of method given in the Port Royal Logic.

(8) Explain Descartes's rules for reaching the truth.

Syllogism

Lesson 14: The Laws of Thought

The next lessons deal with the most common form of reasoning, known as the syllogism. Before the reader goes on to them, it is worth paying careful attention to the very simple laws of thought on which all reasoning must ultimately rest. These laws describe the very simplest truths: truths on which everyone must agree, and which at the same time apply to every notion we can conceive. We cannot think correctly, or avoid plain self-contradiction, unless we obey what are called the Three Primary Laws of Thought. They can be stated like this:

1. The Law of Identity. Whatever is, is.

2. The Law of Contradiction. Nothing can both be and not be.

3. The Law of Excluded Middle. Everything must either be or not be.

Stated this way, these laws may seem absurdly obvious, and Locke and others made fun of them for exactly that reason. But I have found that students are seldom able to see their full meaning and importance at first. In Lesson 23 we will see that until recent years logicians overlooked a very simple way of explaining all arguments, once these self-evident laws are granted. It is not too much to say that the whole of logic will be plain to anyone who constantly uses these laws as the key.

The first law may be taken as the best definition we can give of identity, or sameness. If anyone did not know what the word Identity meant, it would be enough to tell him that everything is Identical with itself.

The second law needs more thought. It means that nothing can have contradictory and inconsistent qualities at the same time and in the same place. A piece of paper may be blackened in one part while it is white in other parts. Or it may be white at one time and later become black. But we cannot conceive of it being both white and black in the same place at the same time. A door that has been open may be shut, but it cannot be shut and open at once. Water may feel warm to one hand and cold to the other, but it cannot be both warm and cold to the same hand. No quality can be both present and absent at the same time, and this seems to be the simplest and most general truth we can assert about all things. It is the very nature of existence that a thing cannot be other than it is. We can safely say that all fallacy and error come from reasoning, without realizing it, in a way that is inconsistent with this law. Any statement or inference that implies a combination of contradictory qualities must be taken as impossible and false, and breaking this law is the mark of its falseness. It is easy to show that if Iron is a metal, and every metal is an element, then Iron must be an element, or else it can be nothing at all, since it would combine qualities that are inconsistent (see Lesson 23).

The Law of Excluded Middle is much less self-evident than the other two, and the reader may not see at first that it is just as important and necessary as they are. The best way to explain it is this: it is impossible to name any thing, and any quality or circumstance, without granting that the quality or circumstance either belongs to the thing or does not belong to it. The name of the law expresses the fact that there is no third, or middle, course; the answer must be Yes or No. Let the thing be rock and the quality hard; then rock must be either hard or not-hard. Gold must be either white or not white; a line must be either straight or not straight; an action must be either virtuous or not virtuous. In fact, even when we know nothing about the terms we are using, we can still make assertions about them in agreement with this law. The reader may not know, and in fact chemists may not really know for certain, whether vanadium is a metal or not a metal. But anyone knows that it must be one or the other. Some readers may not know what a cycloid is, or what an isochronous curve is, but they must know that a cycloid either is an isochronous curve or is not an isochronous curve.

Still, the law of excluded middle is not so evident that no plausible objections can be raised against it. Someone might argue that rock is not always either hard or soft, because it may be halfway between, a little hard and a little soft at the same time. This objection points to a distinction of great logical importance, and neglecting it often leads to fallacy. The law of excluded middle said nothing about hard and soft; it spoke only of hard and not-hard. If the reader chooses to put soft in place of not-hard, he falls into a serious confusion between opposite terms and contradictory terms. A thing may quite possibly be neither hard nor soft, being halfway between. But in that case it cannot fairly be called hard, so the law still holds. In the same way, water must be either warm or not-warm, but it does not follow that it must be warm or cold. The alternative not-warm plainly includes all the cases where it is cold, and also the cases where it is at a middling temperature, so that we would call it neither warm nor cold. So we must carefully separate questions of degree, or quantity, from questions of simple logical fact. Where a thing or a quality can exist to a greater or lesser extent, there are many alternatives. Warm water, for instance, may have any temperature from perhaps 70° up to 120° (Fahrenheit). Exactly the same question comes up in geometrical reasoning. Euclid, in his Elements, often argues from the self-evident truth that any line must be either greater than, equal to, or less than any other line. While logic has only two alternatives to choose from, mathematics has three. So one line, compared with another, may be:

Another objection to the third law of thought is even more plausible. Take virtue as the thing and triangular as the quality. The Law of Excluded Middle lets us assert at once that virtue is either triangular or not-triangular. At first sight it might seem false and absurd to say that an immaterial notion like virtue is either triangular or not. Virtue has nothing in common with the material substances that occupy space, which are the only things the notion of shape belongs to. But the absurdity does not come from any falseness in the law. It comes from misreading the expression not-triangular. If, when we say that a thing is "not triangular," we are taken to imply that it has some shape, only not a triangular one, then of course the expression cannot be applied to virtue or to anything immaterial. In strict logic, however, no such implied meaning is allowed. Not-triangular includes both the things that have some shape other than triangular and the things that have no properties of shape at all; and it is in this second sense that it applies to an immaterial thing.

So these three laws are universally and necessarily true, whatever things they are applied to, and they become the foundation of reasoning. Every act of reasoning starts from certain judgments. An act of judgment consists in comparing two things or ideas and finding out whether they agree or differ, that is, whether they are identical in any of their qualities. The laws of thought tell us the very nature of this identity, which is what all thought is concerned with. But in the work of discourse, or reasoning, we need some additional laws, or axioms, or self-evident truths. They can be stated like this:

1. Two terms agreeing with one and the same third term agree with each other.

2. Two terms of which one agrees and the other does not agree with one and the same third term, do not agree with each other.

These self-evident truths are usually called the Canons, or Fundamental Principles, of Syllogism, and they are true whatever kind of agreement we are dealing with. The example we used in Lesson 1, where the terms "most useful metal" and "cheapest metal" agreed with the common third term "iron," was simply an instance of the first Canon, and there the agreement was complete identity. In the case of "the earth," "planets," and "bodies revolving in elliptical orbits," the agreement was less complete. The earth is only one of many planets, and the planets are only a small part of all the heavenly bodies that revolve in such orbits, which also include satellites, comets, meteors and double stars.

The second Canon applies to cases where there is disagreement, or difference, as in this example:

	Venus is a planet.
	Planets are not self-luminous.
	Therefore Venus is not self-luminous.

The first of these propositions states that a certain agreement exists between Venus and planet, just as in the earlier case of the earth. But the second proposition states a disagreement between planet and self-luminous bodies. From these we infer a disagreement between Venus and self-luminous body. But the reader should notice carefully that from two disagreements we can never infer anything. If the following were put forward as an argument, it would be plainly absurd:

	Sirius is not a planet.
	Planets are not self-luminous.
	Therefore Sirius is not self-luminous.

Both of the premises, or propositions given, are true, and yet the conclusion is false: all the fixed stars, Sirius among them, are self-luminous, which means they shine by their own light. In fact we may state a third Canon:

3. Two terms both disagreeing with one and the same third term may or may not agree with each other.

Self-evident rules of exactly the same kind as these three Canons are the basis of all mathematical reasoning, where they are usually called axioms. Euclid's first axiom is that "Things which are equal to the same thing are equal to one another;" and it holds true whether we apply it to the lengths of lines, the sizes of angles, areas, solids, numbers, degrees, or anything else that can be equal or unequal. So if the lines A and B are each equal to C, it is evident that each is equal to the other.

Euclid gives no axioms matching the second and third Canons, but they are really used in geometry. If A is equal to B, but D is not equal to B, it follows that A is not equal to D. In other words, things of which one is equal and the other unequal to the same third thing are unequal to each other. Lastly, A and E are two lines that are both unequal to D and unequal to each other, while A and B are two lines that are both unequal to D but equal to each other. So we can plainly see that "two things both unequal to the same thing may or may not be equal to each other."

From all this it will be clear that every piece of reasoning needs at least one agreement. If there are two agreements, we can reason to a third agreement. If there is one agreement and one difference, we can reason to a second difference. But if there are only two differences, we cannot reason to any conclusion at all. In the next lesson these self-evident principles will help explain some of the rules of the syllogism.

Logicians, however, have not kept to these Canons alone. They have often put the same truth into a different form, in an axiom called the Dictum de omni et nullo of Aristotle. This famous Latin phrase means "the statement about all and none," and the axiom, or rather pair of axioms, is usually given in these words:

Whatever is predicated of a term distributed whether affirmatively or negatively, may be predicated in like manner of everything contained under it.

Or more briefly:

What pertains to the higher class pertains also to the lower.

In plain, untechnical language this just means two things. First, whatever can be said of all the things of some sort or kind can be said of any one of them, or any part of them. Second, whatever can be denied of all the things in a class can be denied of any one of them, or any part of them. Whatever can be said of "all planets" can be said of Venus, the earth, Jupiter or any other planet. All the planets can be said to revolve in elliptical orbits, so this can be asserted of Venus, the earth, Jupiter or any other planet. In the same way, by the negative half of the Dictum, we can deny that the planets are self-luminous; and knowing that Jupiter is a planet, we can deny that Jupiter is self-luminous. A little thought will show that the affirmative Dictum is really the first Canon in a less complete and less general form, and that the negative Dictum is, in the same way, the second Canon. In fact these Dicta apply only to those cases of agreement between terms where one term is the name of a smaller class and the other is the name of a larger class that contains it. Logicians have mostly, and strangely, overlooked the important cases where one term agrees with another so far as to be identical with it. But this is not the place to discuss that subject at any length. It is treated in my little book called The Substitution of Similars.

Footnote: Macmillan and Co. 1869.

Some logicians have held that besides the three laws called the Primary Laws of Thought, there is a fourth, called "the Principle, or Law, of Sufficient Reason." Leibniz stated it in these words:

Nothing happens without a reason why it should be so rather than otherwise. For example, suppose there is a pair of scales, exactly alike in every respect on each side, with exactly equal weights in each pan. It must stay motionless and in balance, because there is no reason why one side should go down rather than the other. It is certainly a basic assumption of the science of mechanics that if a body is pushed by two perfectly equal forces in different directions, it will move evenly between them, because there is no reason why it should move more to one side than to the other. Mr. Mansel, Sir William Hamilton and others, however, think that this law has no place in logic, even if it can be held to be self-evident at all. The question seems open to doubt, and we need not discuss it here.

I have used the word axiom so freely in this lesson that I should make its meaning as clear as I can. Philosophers do not entirely agree about where it comes from or exactly what it means, but it certainly comes from the Greek verb ἀξιόω (axioō), which is translated "to think worthy." It generally means a self-evident truth so simple that it must be assumed to be true. Since it cannot be proved from any simpler proposition, it must itself be taken as the basis of reasoning. In mathematics it is clearly used in this sense.

See Hamilton's Lectures on Logic, Lectures 5 and 6.

Questions and Exercises

1. State the three Fundamental Laws of Thought, and apply them to the following notions:

(1) Matter, organic, inorganic.

(2) Undulations, polarized, non-polarized.

(3) Figure, rectilinear, curvilinear.

2. Is it wrong to assert that an animal cannot be both vertebrate and invertebrate, given that some animals are vertebrate and some are not?

3. From the following terms, pick out those that are negatives of the others, and those that are opposites: light, plenum, gain, heat, decrease, loss, darkness, cold, increase, vacuum.

4. How does Aristotle's dictum apply to the following arguments?

(1) Silver is a good conductor of electricity; for such are all the metals.

(2) Comets cannot be without weight; for they are composed of matter, which is not without weight.

Lesson 15: The Rules of the Syllogism

Syllogism is the common name for Mediate Inference: inference through a medium, or middle term. It is to be distinguished from Immediate Inference, which is inference carried out without any third, or middle, term.

We use a middle term, or medium, whenever we can't compare two things with each other directly but can compare each of them with some third thing. We can't compare the sizes of two halls by putting one inside the other. But we can measure each of them with a foot rule or some other suitable measure. That measure is common to both, and it lets us find out their relative size as accurately as we need. Or suppose we have two lots of cotton cloth and want to compare them. We don't have to carry the whole of one lot over to the other. We cut off a sample that represents exactly the quality of the first lot, and the two lots agree or differ according to whether the sample agrees with the second lot or not.

A middle term in a syllogism works very much like the medium in these cases, though not exactly the same way. Suppose, for example, that we want to find out whether or not "whales are viviparous" (that is, give birth to live young), and we have no chance to observe the fact directly. We could still show that it is so if we knew that "whales are mammalian animals" and that "all mammalian animals are viviparous." It would follow that "whales are viviparous." And as far as the inference is concerned, it makes no difference what meaning we give to the words viviparous and mammalian. In this case "mammalian animal" is the middle term.

The name Syllogism means the joining together of two propositions in thought. It comes from the Greek words σύν (syn), together, and λόγος (logos), thought. So it is exactly equivalent to the word Computation, which means thinking together (Latin con, together, and puto, to think), or reckoning. In a syllogism we unite in thought two premises, or propositions put forward, in such a way that we can draw from them, or infer, a third proposition, called the conclusion, by means of the middle term they contain. So we may define Syllogism as the act of thought by which we go from two given propositions to a third proposition, whose truth necessarily follows from the truth of the two given ones. When the argument is fully expressed in words, it is usual to call it, concretely, a syllogism.

The special rules of the syllogism are founded on the Laws of Thought and the Canons we looked at in the last lesson. They tell us exactly when one proposition can be inferred from two others. There are eight of them, as follows:

1. Every syllogism has three and only three terms.

These terms are called the major term, the minor term, and the middle term.

2. Every syllogism contains three, and only three, propositions.

These propositions are called the major premise, the minor premise, and the conclusion.

3. The middle term must be distributed at least once, and must not be ambiguous.

4. No term may be distributed in the conclusion that was not distributed in one of the premises.

5. From negative premises nothing can be inferred.

6. If one premise is negative, the conclusion must be negative; and the other way around, to prove a negative conclusion one of the premises must be negative.

From these rules we can deduce two subordinate rules, which it will be convenient to state right away all the same.

7. From two particular premises no conclusion can be drawn.

8. If one premise is particular, the conclusion must be particular.

All these rules are so extremely important that the student should not only understand perfectly what they mean and why they are true, but also learn them by heart. In the rest of this lesson we will look at what they mean and what force they have.

Since the syllogism consists in comparing two terms by means of a middle term, there obviously can't be fewer than three terms. Nor can there be more. Suppose there were four terms, say A, B, C, D, and we compared A with B and C with D. Then either we would have no common medium at all between A and D, or we would need a second syllogism: first comparing A and C with B, and then A and D with C.

You can always recognize the middle term by the fact that it does not appear in the conclusion. The major term is always the predicate of the conclusion, and the minor term is always the subject. They get these names because in the universal affirmative proposition (A) the predicate is necessarily a wider, or greater, or major term than the subject. In "all humans are mortals," for example, the predicate takes in all the other animals as well as humans, so it is obviously a major, or wider, term than humans.

Next, the syllogism necessarily consists of three parts. There is the major premise, in which the major and middle terms are compared. There is the minor premise, which in the same way compares the minor and middle terms. And there is the conclusion, which contains only the major and minor terms. In a strictly correct syllogism the major premise always comes before the minor premise, but in ordinary writing and speech this rule is seldom kept. Whichever premise contains the major term is still the major premise, wherever it stands.

The third rule is a very important one, because many fallacies come from ignoring it. When we say the middle term must be distributed at least once, we mean (see Lesson 9) that the whole of it must be referred to universally in one premise, if not in both. Take the two propositions

	All Frenchmen are Europeans,
	All Russians are Europeans.

They do not distribute the middle term at all, because both are affirmative propositions, and affirmative propositions have undistributed predicates (see Lesson 9). Plainly, Frenchmen are one part of the Europeans and Russians are another part, as Euler's method shows in Fig. 6, so there is no real middle term. These propositions would allow Russians to be Frenchmen just as well as not to be. Whether the two inner circles overlap or not, both are still inside the larger circle of Europeans. Or take the two propositions

	All Frenchmen are Europeans,
	All Parisians are Europeans.

They do not let us infer that all Parisians are Frenchmen. Of course we know that all Parisians are included among Frenchmen, but the premises would let them be placed anywhere inside the circle of Europeans. This example shows that the premises and the conclusion of an apparent argument may all be true, and the argument may still be fallacious.

The part of the third rule about an ambiguous middle term hardly needs explaining. We said in Lesson 4 that an ambiguous term is one with two different meanings, implying different connotations. It is really the same as two different terms that happen to be spelled the same way, so that they are easily mistaken for each other. Suppose we argued that "all metals are elements, and brass is metal, therefore it is an element." We would be committing a fallacy, because we would be using the middle term metal in two different senses. In one sense it means the pure, simple substances that chemists call metals. In the other it means a mixture of metals that is commonly called metal in the trades, but that chemists call an alloy. In many of the examples found in logic books, the ambiguity of the middle term is extremely obvious. But the reader should always be ready to meet cases where the ambiguity is extremely subtle and hard to see. For instance, someone might argue that "what is right should be enforced by law, and charity is right, and should therefore be enforced by the law." Here it is clear that right is used in one place for what the conscience approves, and in the other for what public opinion holds to be necessary for the good of society.

The fourth rule forbids us to distribute a term in the conclusion unless it was distributed in the premises. The only purpose of the syllogism is to prove the conclusion from the premises. So obviously we must not make a statement about anything unless that thing was mentioned in the premises in a way that justifies the statement. Suppose we argued that "because many nations are capable of self-government, and nations capable of self-government should not receive laws from a despotic government, therefore no nation should receive laws from a despotic government." We would plainly be going beyond what our premises contain. The minor term, many nations, was particular in the minor premise, and must not be made universal in the conclusion. The premises justify a statement only about the many nations that are capable of self-government. So this argument would be fallacious, and its technical name would be an illicit process of the minor term, meaning that we have treated the minor term improperly.

A breach of the fourth rule like this one is extremely easy to detect, and so it is very seldom committed.

But an illicit process, or improper treatment, of the major term is more common, because it is not so obviously false. Suppose we argued that "because all Anglo-Saxons love liberty, and Frenchmen are not Anglo-Saxons, therefore they do not love liberty." The fallacy would be fairly plain, but without knowing some logic it would not be easy to explain clearly what is wrong. The major term, loving liberty, is undistributed in the major premise, so we must take the Anglo-Saxons to be only a part of those who love liberty. So shutting Frenchmen out of the class of Anglo-Saxons does not necessarily shut them out of the class of those who love liberty (see Fig. 8). The conclusion of the false argument is negative, so it distributes its predicate, the major term. Since that term is undistributed in the major premise, we have an Illicit Major, as we may call this fallacy for short. Here is a less obvious example of the same fallacy: "Few students are capable of excelling in many branches of knowledge, and those who can excel in this way are deserving of high praise"; hence "few students are deserving of high praise." The little word "few" here has the double meaning explained earlier (see Lesson 8): it means "a few are, and so on, and the rest are not." So the conclusion is really a negative proposition, and it distributes the major term "deserving of high praise." But this major term is clearly undistributed in the major premise. That premise only says that those who can excel in many branches of knowledge are deserving of praise. It says nothing, and implies nothing, about the other students.

The fifth rule is clearly founded on the principle we noticed in the last lesson: inference can only go forward where there is agreement, and two differences, or disagreements, allow no reasoning at all. As the third Canon states, two terms may both differ from a common term and still may or may not differ from each other. Suppose we argued that Americans are not Europeans and Virginians are not Europeans. Both terms disagree with the middle term Europeans, and yet they agree with each other. In other cases the two negative premises may be plainly true while it stays quite uncertain whether the major and minor terms agree or not. It is true, for instance, that "colonists are not Europeans, and Americans are not Europeans." But this gives us no right to infer either that colonists are Americans or that they are not. Fig. 9 represents the two negative premises by keeping the circles of Colonists and Americans outside the circle of Europeans. But they can be kept outside it whether Colonists and Americans coincide partly, or wholly, or not at all. A breach of this rule of the syllogism may conveniently be called the fallacy of negative premises. We must not suppose, though, that just having a negative word (not or no) in a proposition makes it negative in the sense this rule means. Take the argument

	"What is not compound is an element.
	Gold is not compound;
	Therefore Gold is an element."

It has negatives in both premises, but it is valid all the same, because in both cases the negative belongs to the middle term, which is really the negative term not-compound.

The truth of the sixth rule depends on the truth of the axiom that if two terms agree with a common third term, they agree with each other. Remember that a negative proposition asserts disagreement. Then it is clear that a negative conclusion could not be drawn from premises that are really affirmative. The matching negative axiom prevents us from drawing an affirmative conclusion if either premise is really negative. Only practice, however, will let the student apply this rule and the ones before it with confidence, since a fallacy can be hidden and disguised by all sorts of ways of putting things. The questions at the end of Lessons 18 and 21 give many examples on which the student can become skilled at analyzing arguments.

The remaining rules of the syllogism, the 7th and the 8th, are by no means self-evident. They are in fact corollaries of the first six rules, that is, consequences that follow from them. So we will have to show in Lesson 17 that they really do follow. We may call a breach of the 7th rule a fallacy of particular premises, and a breach of the 8th rule the fallacy of a universal conclusion from a particular premise. But these fallacies can really be reduced to those of Illicit Process or Undistributed Middle.

For many details about the Aristotelian and Scholastic views of the Syllogism, and of Formal Logic in general, see the full critical notes in Mansel's edition of Aldrich's Artis Logicæ Rudimenta. 2nd Ed. Oxford. 1852.

Questions and Exercises

1. Distinguish mediate and immediate inference.

2. Define syllogism, and say what word it is synonymous with.

3. What are the six principal and two subordinate rules of the syllogism?

4. In the following syllogisms, point out in turn the conclusion, the middle term, the major term, the minor term, the major premise and the minor premise, keeping to exactly this order.

(1) All human beings are fallible; All kings are human beings; Therefore all kings are fallible.

(2) Platinum is a metal; All metals combine with oxygen; Therefore Platinum combines with oxygen.

(3) The Khoikhoi are capable of education; for the Khoikhoi are human beings, and all human beings are capable of education.

5. Explain carefully what is meant by non-distribution of the middle term.

Lesson 16: The Moods and Figures of the Syllogism

We now have in hand all the principles of reasoning, and the rules built on them, that let us tell a true syllogism from one that only looks like one. Our job in this lesson is to find out all the different shapes in which a mediate inference, or syllogism, can turn up. We know that every syllogistic argument must contain three propositions, and three distinct terms, each appearing twice in those propositions. As far as we know so far, each proposition of the syllogism can be either affirmative or negative, and either universal or particular. So it is not hard to work out the largest possible number of ways a syllogism could conceivably be put together. In short, any one of the four propositions A, E, I or O can be taken as the major premise; it can be joined to any one of the same four as the minor premise; and any one of the four again can be added as the conclusion. That gives us a series of combinations, or ways of joining the letters A, E, I, O. Here are a few of them written out:

AAAAEAAIAAOAEAAEEA
AAEAEEAIEAOEEAEEEE
AAIAEIAIIAOIEAIEEI
AAOAEOAIOAOOEAO&c.

Clearly there will be 4x4x4, or 64, such combinations in all, and only 23 of them are given above. The student can easily write out the rest by carrying on the same orderly changes of the letters. Starting with AAA, we change the right-hand letter to E, then I, then O. Then we do the same starting from AEA instead. Once the middle letter has been carried through all its changes, we start changing the left-hand letter. With each change of the left-hand letter we have to repeat all sixteen changes of the other two, so there are plainly 64 different conceivable ways of arranging propositions into syllogisms.

We call each of these triplets of propositions a mood, or form, of the syllogism (from the Latin modus, shape). Now we have to work out how many of these forms can really be used in valid arguments, as opposed to those that break one or more of the rules of the syllogism. The mood AEA, for example, would break the 6th rule, that if one premise is negative the conclusion must be negative too. AIE breaks the other half of the same rule, that a negative conclusion can only be proved by means of a negative premise. EEA, EEE and so on break the 5th rule, which forbids us to reason at all from two negative premises. Examples of any of these moods are easy to invent, and their falsity is very obvious. For AEA we might take:

	All Austrians are Europeans,
	No Australians are Europeans;
	Therefore, all Australians are Austrians.

Many of the 64 conceivable moods are ruled out by the 7th and 8th rules of the syllogism. AIA and EIE break the rule that if one premise is particular, the conclusion must be particular too. IIA, IOO, OIO and many others break the rule against two particular premises. Some combinations break more than one rule. OOO has both negative premises and particular premises, and OOA breaks the 6th rule as well. It is an excellent exercise in using the rules of the syllogism to write out all 64 combinations and then strike out every one that breaks a rule. If you go about it systematically, the job will not be as long or as tedious as it might seem. You will find that only twelve moods escape being ruled out, and so far these can be considered good forms of reasoning. They are:

AAAEAEIAIOAO
AAIEAO(IEO)
AEEEIO
AEO
AII
AOO

Of these, though, IEO will soon have to be thrown out, because we will find that it really breaks the 4th rule and involves an Illicit process of the major term. So there are only eleven moods of the syllogism that are really valid, and we can now account for all sixty-four moods:

Excluded byNumber of moods.
Negative premises,Rule 516
Particular premisesRule 712
One negative premiseRule 612
One premise particularRule 88
Negative conclusionRule 64
Illicit majorRule 41
Total excluded53
Valid moods11
Total64

We have by no means used up all the possible varieties of the syllogism yet. So far we have only settled the character of the propositions: affirmative or negative, general or particular. We have not settled the ways the terms can be arranged inside them. The major term must be the predicate of the conclusion, but it can be either the subject or the predicate of the major premise. In the same way, the minor term, the subject of the conclusion, can be either the subject or the predicate of the minor premise. This gives four different ways in which the terms can be arranged, and these are called Figures. The four figures of the syllogism are shown in the following scheme, taking

	X to denote the major term
	Y to denote the middle term
	Z to denote the minor term
1st Fig.2nd Fig.3rd Fig.4th Fig.
Major PremiseY XX YY XX Y
Minor PremiseZ YZ YY ZY Z
ConclusionZ XZ XZ XZ X

These figures must be carefully learned by heart, and the best way to do it is to notice where the middle term stands. In the 1st Figure it stands first, as the subject of the major premise. In the 2nd Figure it stands second, as the predicate of both premises. In the 3rd Figure it stands first again, as the subject of both premises. In the 4th Figure it takes an in-between position. In the conclusion, of course, the major and minor terms always have the same fixed places, so once the middle term is correctly placed in any figure, the rest of the syllogism is easy to fill in.

The reader will hardly be pleased to hear that each of the eleven valid moods has to be examined in each of the four figures separately. That leaves 44 possible cases, and the valid syllogisms have to be picked out from among them. The mood AEE in the first figure, for example, would look like this:

	All Y's are X's,
	No Z's are Y's;
	Therefore no Z's are X's.

This would break the 4th rule and be an Illicit Major, because X is distributed in the conclusion, which is a negative proposition, but not in the major premise. In the second figure the same mood would be valid:

	All X's are Y's,
	No Z's are Y's;
	Therefore no Z's are X's.

In the third figure it becomes

	All Y's are X's,
	No Y's are Z's,
	No Z's are X's,

and once again it breaks the 4th rule with respect to the major term. Finally, in the 4th figure it is valid, as the reader can easily check for himself.

When all the valid moods have been picked out of the 44 possible ones, there turn out to be 24 altogether. Here they are:

	Valid Moods of the Syllogism.
First Figure.Second Figure.Third Figure.Fourth Figure.
AAAEAEAAIAAI
EAEAEEIAIAEE
AIIEIOAIIIAI
EIOAOOEAOEAO
OAOEIO
[AAI][EAO]EIO
[EAO][AEO][AEO]
