Five of these moods are set apart in brackets because, although they are valid, they are of little or no use. They are said to have a weakened conclusion, because the conclusion is particular when a general one could have been drawn. AAI in the first figure, for example, is represented by this:

	All material substances gravitate,
	All metals are material substances;
	Therefore some metals gravitate.

Obviously the conclusion states only part of the truth: in reality all metals gravitate. It is not actually a wrong conclusion. As we must carefully remember from Lesson 9, affirming a subaltern, or particular, proposition does not deny the general proposition that goes with it. It is perfectly true that some metals gravitate, and it must be true, because all of them do. But when we can just as easily prove that all of them gravitate, that is the conclusion we ought to draw.

If we follow most logicians and set aside the five syllogisms with weakened conclusions, nineteen remain that are both valid and useful. In the next lesson we will be given some ancient memory-verses, and those verses are about the only means by which most people could keep these 19 combinations in their heads. In the meantime, the reader can see from the table of valid moods above that the following remarks about the particular character of each figure of the syllogism are true.

The first figure is the only one that proves the proposition A, that is, the only one with A as a conclusion. It is also the only figure that can prove every one of the four propositions A, E, I and O. As for its premises, it is especially important to notice that the major premise is always universal (A or E) and the minor premise is always affirmative (A or I). We will look at this peculiarity further in the next lesson.

The second figure proves only negative conclusions (E or O), and the reason is easy to see. In this figure the middle term is the predicate of both premises. So if both premises were affirmative, the middle term would be undistributed in both of them, and we would commit the fallacy of undistributed middle that we saw illustrated in Lesson 15 (the Frenchmen and the Russians). It follows that one premise must be negative, and of course only one. So of the major and minor terms, one must be wholly included in the middle term or wholly excluded from it, and the other must at the same time be excluded from it or included in it, at least partly. To illustrate this, let X, Y and Z stand, as before, for the major, middle and minor terms of a syllogism. The four moods of this figure are then

EAEAEE
no X's are Y's,all X's are Y's,
all Z's are Y's;no Z's are Y's;
∴ no Z's are X's.∴ no Z's are X's.
EIOAOO
no X's are Y's,all X's are Y's,
some Z's are Y's;some Z's are not Y's;
∴ some Z's are not X's.∴ some Z's are not X's.

The nature of the moods of the second figure is shown clearly in the following figures:

Notice also that in the second figure the minor premise can be any of the four, A, E, I or O.

The third figure proves only particulars (I or O), and its minor premise is always affirmative (A or I). It also has the largest number of moods, because none of its conclusions is a weakened one.

The fourth figure is usually considered unnatural and fairly useless, because the same arguments can be set out more clearly in the form of the first figure, which it resembles in some ways. It proves every proposition except A, that is, E, I and O, and its first mood, AAI, is really a weakened form of AAA in the first figure. Many logicians, including Sir William Hamilton in recent times, have rejected this figure altogether.

Clearly the different figures of the syllogism have different characters, and logicians have thought that each figure is best suited to certain special purposes. A German logician, Lambert, stated these purposes briefly as follows: "The first figure is suited to discovering or proving the properties of a thing; the second to discovering or proving the differences between things; the third to discovering or proving instances and exceptions; the fourth to discovering, or ruling out, the different species of a genus."

We may add that the moods Cesare and Camestres are often used to disprove a statement, because they give a universal negative conclusion, based on shutting one class out of another. Suppose someone still claimed that light consists of material particles. We could answer him with the following syllogism:

	"Material particles pass on impetus to whatever they strike.
	Light does not pass on impetus to whatever it strikes;
	Therefore light is not material particles."

The moods Baroko and Festino are used less, but they allow a particular conclusion to be established.

When we want to raise objections or exceptions to a general statement, though, which is in fact the natural way of answering one, we use the third figure. The statement that "all metals are solids" would be disproved at once by the exception mercury, like this:

	Mercury is not solid,
	Mercury is a metal;
	Therefore some metal is not solid.

Suppose someone claimed that what is incomprehensible cannot exist. We answer at once with this argument: infinity is incomprehensible; but infinity certainly exists, because without it we cannot explain the nature of a curved line, or of a quantity that varies continuously; therefore something that is incomprehensible exists. In this case even one exception is enough to overturn the claim completely, since the claim really means that because a thing is incomprehensible it cannot exist. And if one incomprehensible thing does exist, others may too, and the statement loses all its authority.

Under the Aristotelian system, the third figure must also be used whenever the middle term is a singular term, because in Aristotle's view of the subject a singular term could not stand as the predicate of a proposition.

Questions and Exercises

1. Name the rules of the syllogism broken by each of the following moods, paying no attention to figure: AIA, EEI, IEA, IOI, IIA, AEI.

2. Write out all 64 moods of the syllogism and strike out the 53 invalid ones.

3. Show in which figures the following premises give a valid conclusion: AA, AI, EA, OA.

4. In which figures are IEO and EIO valid?

5. To which moods do the following valid syllogisms belong? Arrange them in correct logical order.

(1) Some Y's are Z's. No X's are Y's. Some Z's are not X's.

(2) All Z's are Y's. No Y's are X's. No Z's are X's.

(3) No fish suckles its young; The whale suckles its young; Therefore the whale is no fish.

6. Draw conclusions from the following premises, and say which mood each syllogism belongs to.

(1) Some amphibious animals are mammalian. All mammalian animals are vertebrate.

(2) All planets are heavenly bodies. No planets are self-luminous.

(3) Mammalian animals are quadrupeds. No birds are quadrupeds.

(4) Ruminant animals are not predacious. The lion is predacious.

7. Invent examples to show that false premises can give true conclusions.

8. Supply premises for the following conclusions:

(1) Some logicians are not good reasoners.

(2) The rings of Saturn are material bodies.

(3) Party government exists in every democracy.

(4) All fixed stars obey the law of gravitation.

Lesson 17: Reduction of the Imperfect Figures of the Syllogism

To make the nineteen valid and useful moods of the syllogism easier to remember, logicians invented, at least six centuries ago, a very curious system of artificial words, put together into verses that are easy to learn by heart. However clever the device is, it is barbarous and entirely unscientific. Still, a student of logic is expected to know how it is built and how to use it, so the verses are given and explained below:

	Barbara, Celarent, Darii, Ferioque, prioris;
	Cesare, Camestres, Festino, Baroko, secundæ;
	Tertia, Darapti, Disamis, Datisi, Felapton,
	Bokardo, Ferison, habet; Quarta insuper addit
	Bramantip, Camenes, Dimaris, Fesapo, Fresison.

The words that are not mood names are real Latin words. They tell us that the four moods whose artificial names are Barbara, Celarent, Darii and Ferio belong to the first figure ("prioris," of the first); that the next four belong to the second ("secundæ"); that the third ("Tertia") has six more; and that the fourth figure ("Quarta") adds five moods besides. Each artificial name contains three vowels, and these give the propositions that make up a valid mood. So CElArEnt stands for the mood of the first figure that has E for its major premise, A for its minor premise, and E for its conclusion. Taken all together, the artificial words contain exactly the combinations of vowels shown in the table of valid moods in Lesson 16, except those in brackets.

These verses also contain directions for proving each mood of the second, third and fourth figures by reducing it to a matching mood of the first figure. Aristotle regarded the first figure as an especially clear and convincing form of argument, because the Dictum de omni et nullo applies to it directly, and so he called it the Perfect Figure. He never recognized the fourth figure at all. It is often called the Galenian figure, because the famous Greek physician Galen is supposed to have discovered it. Aristotle knew the second and third figures as the Imperfect Figures. They had to be reduced to the first figure by certain conversions of the premises and by changing the order of the premises, and the artificial words contain the directions for doing this. The directions are as follows:

The letter s means that the proposition marked by the vowel just before it is to be converted simply.

The letter p means that the proposition is to be converted per accidens, or by limitation.

The letter m means that the premises of the syllogism are to be transposed: the old major premise becomes the minor premise of a new syllogism, and the old minor becomes the new major. The m comes from the Latin mutare, to change.

B, C, D, F, the consonants the names begin with, tell us which mood of the first figure the reduction produces. So Cesare, Camestres and Camenes reduce to Celarent; Darapti and the others beginning with D reduce to Darii; Fresison reduces to Ferio; and so on.

The letter k means that the mood must be reduced, or proved, by a separate process called Indirect reduction, or reductio ad impossibile, which we will come to shortly.

Now let us take a syllogism, say one in Camestres, and follow the directions for reducing it. Let the example be:

	All stars are self-luminous (1)
	No planets are self-luminous (2)
	Therefore no planets are stars (3)

The first s in Camestres tells us to convert the minor premise simply. The m tells us to change the order of the premises, and the final s tells us to convert the conclusion simply. When we have made all these changes, we get:

	No self-luminous bodies are planets, converse of (2)
	All stars are self-luminous (1)
	Therefore no stars are planets, converse of (3)

This, as we can check, is a syllogism in Celarent, which we could have known from the initial C of Camestres.

For another example, let us take Fesapo:

	No fixed stars are planets,
	All planets are round bodies;
	Therefore some round bodies are not fixed stars.

The directions in the name tell us to convert the major premise simply, and the minor premise by limitation. We then have this syllogism in Ferio:

	No planets are fixed stars,
	Some round bodies are planets;
	Therefore some round bodies are not fixed stars.

The reader will find it easy to apply the same process of conversion or transposition to the other moods, following the directions in their names. The only moods we need to look at specially are Bramantip, Baroko and Bokardo. As an example of Bramantip we may take:

	All metals are material substances.
	All material substances are gravitating bodies;
	Therefore some gravitating bodies are metals.

The name contains the letter m, which tells us to transpose the premises, and the letter p, which means conversion by limitation. Making these changes, we have:

	All material substances are gravitating bodies,
	All metals are material substances;
	Therefore some metals are gravitating bodies.

This is not a syllogism in Barbara, as we might have expected. It is the weakened mood AAI of the first figure. Plainly these premises yield the conclusion "all metals are gravitating bodies," so in this mood we must take the letter p to mean that the conclusion is weaker than it could be. The truth is that the fourth figure is so imperfect and unnatural in form that Aristotle, the founder of logical science, never admitted that it existed at all. It contains nothing but badly arranged syllogisms, which would have been better stated in the first figure. It is a pity that such a needless addition was made to the forms of the syllogism, which are complicated enough already.

The two odd moods called Baroko and Bokardo cause a good deal of trouble, because they cannot be reduced directly to the first figure. To show how to handle them, we will let X, Y and Z stand for the major, middle and minor terms of the syllogism. Baroko can then be stated like this:

	All X's are Y's,
	Some Z's are not Y's;
	Therefore some Z's are not X's.

Now if we convert the major premise by contraposition (see Lesson 10), we get "all not-Y's are not X's." Making this the major premise of the syllogism, we have:

	All not-Y's are not X's,
	Some Z's are not-Y's;
	Therefore some Z's are not X's.

Both of these premises look negative, but this is really a valid syllogism in Ferio. Two of the negative particles only affect the middle term (see Lesson 15), and so we have carried out the reduction of the syllogism.

Stated in the same way, Bokardo is:

	Some Y's are not X's,
	All Y's are Z's;
	Therefore some Z's are not X's.

To reduce this, convert the major premise by negation, and then transpose the premises. We get:

	All Y's are Z's,
	Some not-X's are Y's;
	Therefore some not-X's are Z's.

This conclusion is the converse by negation of the original conclusion. So the truth of the original conclusion is proved by reducing the argument to a syllogism in Darii.

Both of these moods, Baroko and Bokardo, can also be proved by a special process of Indirect reduction, very much like the indirect proofs Euclid often uses in geometry. The process works like this. We suppose that the conclusion of the syllogism is false, and that its contradictory is therefore true. We can then easily build a new syllogism that leads to a conclusion contradicting one of the original premises. Now in logic it is absurd to call the truth of our own premises into question. The whole purpose of argument, or syllogism, is to draw a conclusion that will be true when the premises are true. The syllogism lets us restate, in a new form, the information already contained in the premises, just as a machine can hand back to us in a new form the material we put into it. The machine, or rather its maker, is not responsible for the quality of the materials fed into it. In the same way, the logician is not in the least responsible for the truth of his premises, only for handling them correctly. If he handles them at all, he must treat them as true. So a conclusion that requires one of our premises to be false is completely absurd.

To apply this method, we may take Baroko as before:

	All X's are Y's (1)
	Some Z's are not Y's (2)
	Therefore some Z's are not X's (3)

If this conclusion is not true, then its contradictory, "all Z's are X's," must necessarily be taken as true (see Lesson 9). Making this the minor premise of a new syllogism, with the original major premise, we have:

	All X's are Y's (1)
	All Z's are X's, contradictory of (3)
	Hence all Z's are Y's.

Now this conclusion, in A, is the contradictory of our old minor premise, in O. So we must either admit that one of our own premises is false or grant that our original conclusion is true. Of course the second is the one we choose.

We treat Bokardo in a very similar way:

	Some Y's are not X's (1)
	All Y's are Z's (2)
	Therefore some Z's are not X's (3)

If this conclusion is not true, then "all Z's are X's" must be true. Now we can make the syllogism:

	All Z's are X's, contradictory of (3)
	All Y's are Z's (2)
	Hence all Y's are X's.

This conclusion is the contradictory of (1), the original major premise. Since that cannot be allowed, we must either suppose that (2), the original minor premise, is false, which is just as impossible, or grant that our original conclusion is true.

Notice that in both of these cases of Indirect Reduction, or Indirect Proof, we use a syllogism in Barbara, and that is what the initial letters of Baroko and Bokardo indicate. The same process of indirect proof can be applied to any of the other moods, but it is not usual to do so, because the simpler process of direct reduction, often called ostensive reduction, is enough.

You will remember that when we looked at the rules of the syllogism toward the end of Lesson 15, there were two supplementary rules, the 7th and 8th, about particular premises. These were not at all self-evident, and they need to be proved from the six more fundamental rules. We have now come far enough to look at this proof with profit. The 7th rule forbids us to draw any conclusion from two particular premises. Two such premises must be II, IO, OI or OO. Of these, II contains no distributed term at all, so it must break the 3rd rule, which requires the middle term to be distributed. The premises OO plainly break the 5th rule, against negative premises. With the pair IO, the conclusion must be negative by the 6th rule, because one premise is negative. The major term will therefore be distributed in the conclusion. But the major premise is a particular affirmative, so the major term cannot be distributed there, and drawing the conclusion would commit the fallacy of illicit process of the major, against rule 4. Lastly, the premises OI contain only one distributed term, the predicate of the major premise. But since the conclusion must be negative by the 6th rule, the major term must be distributed. So we would need two distributed terms in the premises, one for the middle term and the other for the major term. The premises contain only one, so if we try to draw any conclusion at all, we must commit either the fallacy of undistributed middle or that of illicit process of the major term. We see, then, that in no possible case can a pair of particular premises give a valid conclusion.

The 8th rule of the syllogism tells us that if one premise of a syllogism is particular, the conclusion must also be particular. The only way to show this is true is to go through all the possible cases and observe that the six principal rules of the syllogism always require the conclusion to be particular. Suppose, for instance, the premises are A and I. Then they contain only one distributed term, the subject of A, and rule 3 requires it for the middle term. So the minor term cannot be distributed in the conclusion without breaking rule 4, and the conclusion must be the proposition I. The premises AO would contain two distributed terms, the subject of A and the predicate of O. But if we drew the conclusion E from them, both the major and the minor terms would have to be distributed, and the middle term would be left undistributed, against rule 3. The reader can easily prove the other cases, such as EI, by counting the distributed terms in the same way. It will always turn out that the premises do not distribute enough terms to allow a universal conclusion.

Questions and Exercises

1. State and explain the mnemonic lines Barbara, Celarent, and so on.

2. Construct syllogisms in each of the following moods, taking X, Y and Z for the major, middle and minor terms respectively, and show how to reduce them to the first figure:

Cesare, Festino, Darapti, Datisi, Ferison, Camenes, Fesapo.

3. What is the use of Reduction?

4. Prove that the following premises cannot give a universal conclusion: EI, IA, OA, IE.

5. Prove that the third figure must have an affirmative minor premise and a particular conclusion.

6. Reduce the moods Cesare and Camenes by the Indirect method, or Reductio ad Impossibile.

Lesson 18: Irregular and Compound Syllogisms

It may seem surprising, but the arguments we meet in books or in conversation are hardly ever set out in the form of regular syllogisms. Even when a complete syllogism does turn up now and then, it is usually there as a mere show of logical precision. In earlier centuries, it is true, every student at the universities had to take part in public disputations. In these, one side put forward elaborate syllogistic arguments and the other side refuted them with precise syllogisms of its own. The practice was given up at the University of Oxford not very long ago, and it is said to survive still at some universities on the Continent. But outside disputations of that kind, we have to admit, perfectly formal syllogisms are seldom used.

The truth is, though, that syllogistic arguments are not missing at all. Wherever one of the conjunctions therefore, because, for, since, hence, inasmuch as, consequently appears, we can be sure that an inference is being drawn, and very probably it is being drawn by a true syllogism. What usually gets left out is only the complete statement of the premises and the conclusion. The reader generally knows one premise or the other already, or can easily guess what is being assumed, and it is tedious, even irritating, to spell out at full length what the reader already knows. Suppose I say, "atmospheric air must have weight because it is a material substance." I am certainly using a syllogism. But I think it quite unnecessary to state the premise whose truth I am plainly assuming, namely "whatever is a material substance has weight." The conclusion of the syllogism is the first proposition, "atmospheric air has weight." The middle term is "material substance," which does not appear in the conclusion. The minor term is "atmospheric air," and the major term is "having weight." The complete syllogism is plainly this:

	All material substances have weight,
	Atmospheric air is a material substance;
	Therefore atmospheric air has weight.

This is in the very common and useful mood Barbara.

A syllogism that is stated incompletely is usually called an enthymeme. The name is often supposed to come from two Greek words, ἐν (en), "in," and θυμός (thymos), "mind." On that reading it means that some piece of knowledge is held in the mind and supplied as a tacit premise, that is, a silent or understood one. Most often the missing premise is the major premise, and then the enthymeme can be said to be of the First Order. Less often it is the minor premise that goes unstated, and then the enthymeme is of the Second Order. Here is an argument of that kind: "Comets must be subject to the law of gravitation; for this is true of all bodies which move in elliptical orbits." It is so obviously implied that comets move in elliptical orbits that it would be tedious to state it as the minor premise of a complete syllogism in the mood Barbara, like this:

	All bodies moving in elliptical orbits are subject to the law of gravitation;
	Comets move in elliptical orbits;
	Therefore comets are subject to the law of gravitation.

Occasionally it is the conclusion of a syllogism that is left unstated, and then the enthymeme can be said to belong to the Third Order. This happens with epigrams and other witty sayings, where the whole wit often lies in making an unstated truth obvious. Sir William Hamilton gives, as an example of this kind of enthymeme, the famous epigram that the English scholar Porson wrote about a German scholar of his own day, Gottfried Hermann:

	"The Germans in Greek
	Are sadly to seek;
	Not five in five score,
	But ninety-five more;
	All, save only Hermann,
	And Hermann's a German."

The writer pretends to make an exception of Hermann. But he cleverly slips in the suggestion that, since Hermann is a German, he does not really know Greek either. In the same way, the wonderful speech Antony makes over Caesar's body, in Julius Caesar, Shakespeare's greatest historical play, contains a whole series of syllogistic arguments whose conclusions are only hinted at.

Even a single proposition can have the force of a syllogism, if it clearly brings to mind a second premise from which a conclusion can then be drawn. A saying of the radical writer John Horne Tooke, "Men who have no rights cannot justly complain of any wrongs," seems to be a case in point. Very few people have never felt wronged at one time or another, and so they would be likely to argue, whether their premises were true or false, like this:

	Men who have no rights cannot justly complain of any wrongs;
	We can justly complain;
	Therefore we are not men who have no rights.

In other words, we have rights.

Syllogisms can be joined and combined in various ways, and it is convenient to have special names for the different parts of a complex argument. A syllogism that proves, or gives a reason for, one of the premises of another syllogism is called a Prosyllogism. A syllogism that has as one of its premises the conclusion of another syllogism is called an Episyllogism.

Take this example:

	All B's are A's,
	And all C's are B's;
	Therefore all C's are A's.
	But all D's are C's;
	Therefore all D's are A's.

This plainly contains two syllogisms in the mood Barbara. The first is a Prosyllogism with respect to the second, and the second is an Episyllogism with respect to the first.

The special name Epicheirema is given to a syllogism in which either premise is proved or supported by a reason, so that a prosyllogism is implied but only partly expressed. So the form

	All B's are A's, for they are P's,
	And all C's are B's, for they are Q's;
	Therefore all C's are A's,

is a double Epicheirema, since it gives reasons for both premises. The reader will easily break it down into three complete syllogisms in the mood Barbara.

A more interesting form of reasoning is the chain of syllogisms usually called the Sorites, from the Greek word σωρός (sōros), meaning a heap. It is usually stated like this:

	All A's are B's,
	All B's are C's,
	All C's are D's,
	All D's are E's;
	Therefore all A's are E's.

The chain can be carried on to any length, as long as it is perfectly continuous, so that every term except the first and the last appears twice, once as a subject and once as a predicate. It hardly needs pointing out that a sorites really contains a series of syllogisms stated incompletely, like this:

First Syllogism.Second Syllogism.Last Syllogism.
B's are C's,C's are D's,D's are E's,
A's are B's;A's are C's;A's are D's;
∴ A's are C's.∴ A's are D's.∴ A's are E's.

Each syllogism supplies a premise to the one after it, and so it is the prosyllogism of that one. Equally, any syllogism in the chain can be regarded as the episyllogism of the one before it.

In the sorites above, all the premises were universal and affirmative. But a sorites may contain one particular premise, provided it is the first, and one negative premise, provided it is the last. The reader can easily check by trying it that if any premise except the first were particular, the fallacy of undistributed middle would be committed. That is because one of the middle terms would then be the predicate of one affirmative premise and the subject of another, particular, premise. And if any premise except the last were negative, there would be a fallacy of illicit process of the major term.

We should not suppose that the forms of the syllogism described so far are the only kinds of reasoning actually used in science or in everyday life. Besides the hypothetical and disjunctive syllogisms, and some other forms to be described in later lessons, there are in fact many ways of reasoning that logicians have so far paid little attention to. This was clearly pointed out more than two hundred years ago by the writers of the Port Royal Logic. The book was first printed in 1662, but it has been reprinted many times since and translated into a great many languages. It takes its name from Port Royal, a place near Paris where a small religious community lived. The authors of the book, Arnauld and Nicole, and one contributor to it, the great philosopher and mathematician Pascal, were the most famous members of that community. The Port Royal Logic was to a large extent the basis of the well-known Watts's Logic, but the reader can now be referred to an excellent translation of the original work by Professor Spencer Baynes of St Andrews.

Many improvements to logic can be found in this book, such as the doctrine of extension and intension explained in Lesson 5. Moreover, in the 9th Chapter of the 3rd Part it wisely points out that "little pains are taken in applying the rules of the syllogism to reasonings of which the propositions are complex, though this is often very difficult, and there are many arguments of this nature which appear bad, but which are nevertheless very good; and besides, the use of such reasonings is much more frequent than that of syllogisms which are quite simple." The book gives some examples of the complex syllogisms it means, such as this one:

	The sun is a thing without feeling,
	The Persians worship the sun;
	Therefore the Persians worship a thing without feeling.

This argument cannot be proved by the rules of the syllogism. Yet it is not only plainly true, it is an extremely common kind of argument. Here is another example:

	The Divine Law commands us to honor kings;
	Louis XIV is a king;
	Therefore the Divine Law commands us to honor Louis XIV.

The reader will also find arguments that are perfectly valid and syllogistic, but are worded so that they seem to have four different terms, and so to break one of the rules of the syllogism. Suppose I say, "Diamonds are combustible, for they are composed of carbon and carbon is combustible." Four terms are used here: diamonds, combustible, composed of carbon, and carbon. But it is easy to rearrange the propositions so as to get a simple syllogism without really changing the sense, and then we have:

	What is composed of carbon is combustible;
	Diamonds are composed of carbon;
	Therefore diamonds are combustible.

Among the Miscellaneous Examples at the end of Lesson 21 are some short arguments, taken from Bacon's Essays and other writings, which the student can reduce to syllogistic form by easy changes of wording. But it should be clearly understood that changes like these are not part of logic itself. They belong more properly to the science of language.

I should explain here that the syllogism and the sorites can be expressed either in the order of extension or in the order of intension. In terms of the number of individual things, the noble metals are part of the metals, and the metals are part of the elements. But in terms of intension, that is, the qualities the names imply, element is part of metal, and metal is part of noble metal. Again, in extension the plant genus Anemone is part of the order Ranunculaceae (the buttercup family), and that order is part of the great class of Exogens (roughly, the dicots). But in intension the character of Exogen is part of the character of Ranunculaceae, and that is part of the character of Anemone. Syllogistic reasoning is just as valid and just as obvious either way, and we could show the two modes in ordinary language like this:

	Extensive Syllogism.
	All Ranunculaceae are Exogens;
	The Anemone is one of the Ranunculaceae;
	Therefore the Anemone is an Exogen.
	Intensive Syllogism.
	All the qualities of Ranunculaceae are qualities of Anemone;
	All the qualities of Exogen are qualities of Ranunculaceae;
	Therefore all the qualities of Exogen are qualities of Anemone.

Any sorites can be shown in the same way, in either extension or intension.

On the Aristotelian doctrine of the Enthymeme, see Mansel's Aldrich, App. Note F, and Hamilton's Lectures on Logic, Lecture XX. Port Royal Logic, translated by T. Spencer Baynes, 7th ed., Edinburgh.

Questions and Exercises

1. Explain the meaning of each of these terms: Enthymeme, Prosyllogism, Episyllogism, Epicheirema, Sorites.

2. Make up an example of a syllogism that has two prosyllogisms.

3. Construct a sorites with four premises, and break it down into separate syllogisms.

4. What rules must a sorites obey?

5. The reader is asked to analyze the following arguments, to pick out the ones that are false, and to find which rules of the syllogism they break. If an argument seems valid, the reader should find its figure and mood, state it in correct logical form, and then, if it is in an imperfect figure, prove it by reducing it to the first figure. The first six examples should be arranged in both the extensive and the intensive orders.

1. None but mortals are human beings. Monarchs are human beings. Therefore monarchs are mortals.

2. Personal deformity is an affliction of nature. Disgrace is not an affliction of nature. Therefore personal deformity is not disgrace.

3. Some statesmen are also authors; for such are Mr. Gladstone, Lord Derby, Lord Russell, and Sir G. C. Lewis.

4. This explosion must have been caused by gunpowder; for nothing else would have had enough force.

5. Every person should be moderate; for excess will cause disease.

6. Blessed are the merciful; for they shall obtain mercy.

7. Since almost all the organs of the body have a known use, the spleen must have some use.

8. Cogito, ergo sum. (I think, therefore I exist.)

9. Some speculative people are unworthy of trust; for they are unwise, and no unwise person can be trusted.

10. No idle person can be a successful writer of history; therefore Hume, Macaulay, Hallam and Grote must have been hard-working.

11. Whoever spares the rod hates his child; therefore the parent who loves his child does not spare the rod.

12. Comets must consist of heavy matter; for otherwise they would not obey the law of gravitation.

13. Lithium is an element; for it is an alkali-producing substance, which is a metal, which is an element.

14. Rational beings are accountable for their actions; brute animals, not being rational, are therefore exempt from responsibility.

15. A singular proposition is a universal one; for it applies to the whole of its subject.

16. Whatever tends to draw the mind away from low pursuits deserves to be encouraged; classical learning does this, since it gives us a taste for intellectual pleasures; therefore it deserves to be encouraged.

17. Bacon was a great lawyer and statesman; and since he was also a philosopher, we may infer that any philosopher may be a great lawyer and statesman.

18. Immoral companions should be avoided; but some immoral companions are intelligent people, so some intelligent people should be avoided.

19. Mathematical study undoubtedly improves the reasoning powers; but since the study of logic is not mathematical study, we may infer that it does not improve the reasoning powers.

20. Every fair-minded man acknowledges merit in a rival; every learned man does not do so; therefore every learned man is not fair-minded.

Lesson 19: Of Conditional Arguments

You will remember that when we dealt with propositions we divided them into two distinct kinds: Categorical Propositions and Conditional Propositions. So far we have looked only at the first kind. Now we must go on to describe Conditional propositions, and the arguments that can be built out of them.

Logicians have usually described a Conditional proposition as two or more Categorical propositions joined by a conjunction. They can be joined in two ways, and this gives two very different species of conditional, which we will call Hypothetical Propositions and Disjunctive Propositions. The diagram below shows how the different kinds of propositions are related:

We can also describe a conditional proposition as one that makes a statement under a certain condition, or qualification, which restricts how far the statement applies. In the hypothetical form the condition is brought in by the conjunction if, or by some other word that does the same job. So

	"If iron is impure, it is brittle"

is a hypothetical proposition made up of two distinct categorical propositions. The first, "Iron is impure," is called the Antecedent. The second, "It is brittle," is called the Consequent. Here "impurity" is the condition, or qualification, that limits the application of the predicate brittle to iron. In his celebrated book The Diversions of Purley, Horne Tooke claimed that all conjunctions are the remains, or worn-down forms, of verbs. This is certainly true of the hypothetical conjunction. In old English the word if is written gif or gyf, and it undoubtedly comes from the verb to give. Even today we can put in its place any verb of similar meaning, such as grant, allow, or suppose. So we can say:

	"Grant that iron is impure, and it is brittle."
	"Supposing that iron is impure, it is brittle."

The hypothetical proposition could be used in arguments of various forms, but only two of these are important enough to have been given special names. The hypothetical syllogism has two premises, called the major and the minor, just like the ordinary syllogism. The major premise is hypothetical in form. The minor premise is categorical, and depending on whether it is affirmative or negative, the argument is called a Constructive or a Destructive hypothetical syllogism. So the form

	If A is B, C is D;
	But A is B;
	Therefore C is D,

is a constructive hypothetical syllogism.

Notice carefully that the minor premise affirms the antecedent of the major premise. That is why the argument is said to be in the modus ponens, the mood that posits, or affirms. It is probably one of the most familiar and common kinds of argument there is. The form

	If A is B, C is D;
	But C is not D;
	Therefore A is not B,

is the matching Destructive hypothetical syllogism, also called the modus tollens, the mood that removes the consequent. Notice carefully, again, that it is the consequent, not the antecedent, that is denied.

Only one rule is needed for testing whether such syllogisms are valid, and it sums up what we have just seen: either the antecedent must be affirmed, or the consequent denied. Break this rule in either direction and you commit a serious fallacy. Take this apparent argument:

	If A is B, C is D;
	But C is D;
	Therefore A is B.

It is really a fallacy, which we can call the fallacy of affirming the consequent. It is easy to see why it fails. Nothing in the premise says that "A being B" is the only condition under which C is D. C might be D when E is F, or when G is H, or in a hundred other circumstances. So the mere fact that C is D is not enough to prove that A is B. For example, if a man has an avaricious, or greedy, character, he will refuse to give money for useful purposes. But it does not follow that everyone who refuses to give money for such purposes is avaricious. There may be many good reasons or motives for refusing. He may have no money; he may think the purpose is not a useful one; or he may have more useful purposes in mind.

A matching fallacy comes from denying the antecedent, as in this form:

	If A is B, C is D;
	But A is not B;
	Therefore C is not D.

The mistake can be explained the same way. Nothing says that "A being B" is the only condition of C being D. So we may deny that this one condition holds, and the consequent may still happen to be true for other reasons we know nothing about. If a man is not avaricious, then, we cannot conclude that he is sure to give money whenever he is asked. Or take this example:

"If the study of Logic furnished the mind with a multitude of useful facts, like the study of other sciences, it would deserve cultivation; but it does not furnish the mind with a multitude of useful facts; therefore it does not deserve cultivation."

This argument is plainly fallacious, because picking up a multitude of useful facts is not the only reason for recommending the study of a science. The reason Logic deserves to be cultivated is that it corrects and exercises our powers of judgment and reasoning. The argument above ignores that this other purpose exists, and it plainly commits the fallacy of denying the antecedent.

Books on logic usually describe the hypothetical proposition and syllogism as if they were different in nature from the categorical proposition and syllogism. But it has long been known that hypotheticals can be reduced to the categorical form and brought under the ordinary rules of the syllogism. As a general rule, a hypothetical proposition can easily be turned into a universal affirmative proposition (A) with exactly the same meaning. Our example, "If iron is impure, it is brittle," simply becomes "Impure iron is brittle." When we make this change in a hypothetical syllogism, we find we have to supply a new minor term. Take the case

	If iron is impure it is brittle;
	But it is impure;
	Therefore it is brittle.

Here we have to put "the iron in question" in place of the indefinite pronoun it, and we get a correct categorical syllogism in the mood Barbara:

	Impure iron is brittle;
	The iron in question is impure iron;
	Therefore the iron in question is brittle.

Sometimes the reduction needs a bigger change of wording. For instance,

	If the barometer is falling, bad weather is coming;
	But the barometer is falling;
	Therefore bad weather is coming,

can be put in this form:

	The circumstances of the barometer falling are the circumstances of bad weather coming;
	But these are the circumstances of the barometer falling;
	Therefore these are the circumstances of bad weather coming.

As an example of the Destructive Hypothetical syllogism we can take:

	If Aristotle is right, slavery is a proper form of society;
	But slavery is not a proper form of society;
	Therefore Aristotle is not right.

As a categorical, this becomes:

	The case of Aristotle being right is the case of slavery being a proper form of society;
	But this is not the case of slavery being a proper form of society;
	Therefore this is not the case of Aristotle being right.

When no other way of wording will reduce a hypothetical, the words "case of" always will.

Now we can easily show that the fallacy of affirming the consequent is really a breach of the third rule of the syllogism, and leads to an undistributed middle term. We can use the same example as before:

	If a man is avaricious he will refuse money;
	But he does refuse money;
	Therefore he is avaricious.

As a categorical syllogism this becomes:

	All avaricious men refuse money;
	But this man refuses money;
	Therefore this man is avaricious.

This is the mood AAA in the second figure. The middle term, refusing money, is undistributed in both premises, so the argument is entirely fallacious.

In the same way, the fallacy of denying the antecedent amounts to the illicit process of the major. Our earlier example (the one about the study of Logic, given earlier in this lesson) can be put like this:

"A science which furnishes the mind with a multitude of useful facts deserves cultivation; but Logic is not such a science; therefore Logic does not deserve cultivation."

This apparent syllogism is in the mood AEE in the first figure. It breaks the fourth rule of the syllogism, because the major term, deserving cultivation, is distributed in the negative conclusion but not in the affirmative major premise.

We now move on to the disjunctive proposition. Instead of a single predicate, it has several alternatives joined by the disjunctive conjunction or, and any one of them may be affirmed of the subject. "A member of the House of Commons is either a representative of a county, or of a borough, or of a University" is an example of such a proposition. It has three alternatives, but there can be any number, from two upward.

The disjunctive syllogism has a disjunctive major premise, with a categorical proposition, either affirmative or negative, as its minor premise. That gives two moods. The affirmative one is called by the Latin name modus ponendo tollens (the mood that denies by affirming), and it can be stated like this:

	A is either B or C,
	But A is B;
	Therefore A is not C.

This form of argument assumes that if one alternative of a disjunctive proposition is held to be true, the others cannot be true as well. Thus "the time of year must be either spring, summer, autumn or winter," and if it is spring, it cannot be summer, autumn or winter, and so on. But Whately, Mansel and Mill, along with many earlier logicians, have objected that this does not always hold. Suppose we say that "a good book is valued either for the usefulness of its contents or the excellence of its style." It certainly does not follow that because the contents of a book are useful, its style is not excellent. We generally choose alternatives that are inconsistent with each other, but logic does not require it.

The other form of disjunctive syllogism, called the modus tollendo ponens (the mood that affirms by denying), is always, necessarily, conclusive. It goes like this:

	A is either B or C,
	But A is not B;
	Therefore A is C.

So if we suppose that a book is valued only for the usefulness of its contents or for the excellence of its style, it follows that if a book is valued, but not for the first reason, it must be valued for the second; and the other way around. If the time of year is not spring, it must be summer, autumn or winter. If it is neither autumn nor winter, it must be either spring or summer, and so on. In short, if any of the alternatives are denied, the rest remain to be affirmed as before. Notice that the disjunctive syllogism is governed by completely different rules from the ordinary categorical syllogism. In the disjunctive syllogism a negative premise gives an affirmative conclusion; in the categorical syllogism it gives a negative one.

One more form of argument remains. It is called the Dilemma, because it starts by assuming two alternatives, usually called the horns of the dilemma, and still proves something whichever one holds (from the Greek δι- (di-), two, and λῆμμα (lēmma), assumption). Mr. Mansel defines this argument as "a syllogism, having a conditional major premise with more than one antecedent, and a disjunctive minor." It can be stated in at least three forms. The first is called the Simple Constructive Dilemma:

	If A is B, C is D; and if E is F, C is D;
	But either A is B, or E is F;
	Therefore C is D.

For example: "if a science furnishes useful facts, it is worth cultivating; and if studying it exercises the reasoning powers, it is worth cultivating; but either a science furnishes useful facts, or studying it exercises the reasoning powers; therefore it is worth cultivating."

The second form is the Complex Constructive Dilemma, which goes like this:

	If A is B, C is D; and if E is F, G is H;
	But either A is B, or E is F;
	Therefore either C is D, or G is H.

It is called complex because the conclusion is in disjunctive form. As an example we can take this argument: "If a statesman who sees that his former opinions were wrong does not change his course, he is guilty of deceit; and if he does change his course, he is open to a charge of inconsistency; but either he does not change his course or he does; therefore he is either guilty of deceit, or open to a charge of inconsistency." In this case, as in most dilemmas, the terms A, B, C, D and so on are not all different.

The Destructive Dilemma is always complex, because otherwise it could be broken up into two unconnected destructive hypothetical syllogisms. Its form is:

	If A is B, C is D; and if E is F, G is H;
	But either C is not D, or G is not H;
	Therefore either A is not B, or E is not F.

For instance: "If this man were wise, he would not speak irreverently of Scripture in jest; and if he were good, he would not do so in earnest; but he does it either in jest or in earnest; therefore he is either not wise, or not good."

Footnote: Whately.

Still, dilemmatic arguments are more often fallacious than not. It is rarely possible to find cases where two alternatives cover every possibility, unless one of them is simply the negative of the other, in keeping with the law of excluded middle (see Lesson 14). Suppose we argued that "if a pupil is fond of learning he needs no stimulus, and if he dislikes learning no stimulus will be of any use; but since he is either fond of learning or dislikes it, a stimulus is either needless or of no use." We would plainly be assuming the disjunctive minor premise without any right to it. Fondness and dislike are not the only two possible alternatives. Some pupils are neither fond of learning nor dislike it, and for them a stimulus in the shape of rewards may be a good thing. You can prove almost anything if you are allowed to pick out two of the possible alternatives that suit you and argue from those alone.

A dilemma can often be retorted, that is, rebutted by producing an equally conclusive dilemma that proves the opposite. According to Aristotle, an Athenian mother spoke to her son like this: "Do not go into public life; for if you say what is just, men will hate you; and if you say what is unjust, the gods will hate you." Aristotle suggests the following retort: "I ought to go into public life; for if I say what is just, the gods will love me; and if I say what is unjust, men will love me."

Footnote: Mansel's Aldrich, Appendix, Note I, on the Hypothetical Syllogism.

Questions and Exercises

1. What are the kinds of conditional propositions, and by what signs can you recognize them?

2. What are the rules of the hypothetical syllogism?

3. Which categorical fallacies do breaches of these rules correspond to?

4. Pick out those of the following that are valid arguments, and reduce them to the categorical form; explain what is fallacious in the reasoning of the others.

(1) Rain has fallen if the ground is wet; but the ground is not wet; therefore rain has not fallen.

(2) If rain has fallen, the ground is wet; but rain has not fallen; therefore the ground is not wet.

(3) The ground is wet, if rain has fallen; the ground is wet; therefore rain has fallen.

(4) If the ground is wet, rain has fallen; but rain has fallen; therefore the ground is wet. Note: in these, as in other logical examples, the student must argue only from the premises, and not from any other knowledge of the subject matter.

5. Show that the canons of the syllogism (see Lesson 14) can be stated equally well in the hypothetical or the categorical form.

6. State the following as a Disjunctive or a Dilemmatic argument, and name the kind it belongs to.

If pain is severe it will be brief; and if it lasts long it will be slight; therefore it is to be patiently borne.

Fallacies

Lesson 20: Logical Fallacies

To get a satisfactory knowledge of the rules of correct thinking, we need to become familiar with the most common kinds of fallacy, that is, the ways in which we often fall into bad reasoning by neglecting the rules of logic. In the earlier lessons we have been learning, so to speak, how to find the right road. Our job here is to find the turnings where we are most likely to take the wrong one.

In describing the fallacies I will follow the order and the way of classifying them that has been usual for more than 2,000 years, in fact ever since the great teacher Aristotle first explained the fallacies. Under this arrangement the fallacies are divided into two main groups: the logical fallacies and the material fallacies.

1. The logical fallacies are the ones that lie in the mere form of the statement, or, as the old Latin expressions put it, in dictione or in voce (in the wording, or in the words). So it is supposed that fallacies of this kind can be discovered without any knowledge of the subject-matter the argument is about.

2. The material fallacies, on the other hand, arise outside the mere verbal statement, or, as it is said, extra dictionem (outside the wording). So they are concerned with the subject of the argument, in re (in the matter), and they can only be detected and set right by people who know the subject.

The first group, the logical fallacies, can be divided further into the purely logical and the semi-logical. In the purely logical class we can include the plain breaches of the rules of the syllogism, which have already been described. So we can list the Purely Logical Fallacies as:

1. Fallacy of four terms (Quaternio Terminorum): a breach of Rule 1;

2. Fallacy of undistributed middle: a breach of Rule 3;

3. Fallacy of illicit process, of the major or the minor term: a breach of Rule 4;

4. Fallacy of negative premises: a breach of Rule 5; and also breaches of the 6th rule, which have been given no separate name. Breaches of the 7th and 8th rules can be resolved into the ones above (see Lesson 17), but they can also be described as they were at the end of Lesson 15.

The other part of the class of logical fallacies contains the Semi-logical fallacies. There are six of them:

	1. Fallacy of Equivocation.
	2. Fallacy of Amphiboly.
	3. Fallacy of Composition.
	4. Fallacy of Division.
	5. Fallacy of Accent.
	6. Fallacy of Figure of Speech.

I will describe and illustrate these one after another.

Equivocation is using the same term in two different senses. Any of the three terms of the syllogism can suffer from this fallacy, but usually it is the middle term, used in one sense in one premise and in another sense in the other. In that case it is often called the fallacy of ambiguous middle. When we separate the two meanings by expressing each of them in other suitable words, it becomes clear that the supposed syllogism contains four terms. So the fallacy of equivocation can be considered a disguised fallacy of four terms. Suppose someone argued that "all criminal actions ought to be punished by law; prosecutions for theft are criminal actions; therefore prosecutions for theft ought to be punished by law." In the first premise a "criminal action" is a crime. In the second it is the legal name for a criminal case brought in court. It is quite obvious that the term means totally different things in the two premises, and that there is no true middle term at all. Often, though, the ambiguity is subtle and difficult, so that people can hold different opinions about it. For example, we might argue:

"He who harms another should be punished. He who communicates an infectious disease to another person harms him. Therefore he who communicates an infectious disease to another person should be punished."

Whether this counts as a correct argument depends on what kinds of actions we think come under the term harm: whether we think carelessness, or only malice, is needed to make something harm. Many difficult legal questions are of this kind. For instance:

	Nuisances are punishable by law;
	To keep a noisy dog is a nuisance;
	To keep a noisy dog is punishable by law.

The question here would turn on how great a nuisance has to be before the law will step in to prevent it. Or again:

	Interference with another man's business is illegal;
	Underselling interferes with another man's business;
	Therefore underselling is illegal.

Here the question turns on the kind of interference, and it is obvious that underselling (selling more cheaply than a competitor) is not the kind of interference the major premise is talking about.

The Fallacy of Amphiboly, or amphibology, is an ambiguous grammatical structure in a sentence, which leads to a misunderstanding. A famous example comes in the prophecy of the spirit in Shakespeare's Henry VI: "The Duke yet lives that Henry shall depose." In plain words, "The Duke is still alive that Henry shall depose." The grammar leaves it completely uncertain whether the Duke will depose Henry or Henry will depose the Duke. This prophecy is no doubt an imitation of the ones the ancient oracle of Delphi is said to have given, and it seems that this fallacy was a great resource for oracles who were not confident in their own powers of foresight. The Latin language gives plenty of room for misreadings, because it does not require any fixed order for the words of a sentence. When there are two nouns in the accusative case with an infinitive verb, it may be hard to tell, except from the context, which of them is meant to come before the verb in sense, that is, which one is doing the action. The double meaning that can be given to "twice two and three" comes from amphiboly. It can be 7 or 10, depending on whether we add the 3 after multiplying or before. When sentences are carelessly built, it is often impossible to tell which part an adverb or a qualifying phrase belongs to. If someone says "I finished my business and returned the day after," the business may have been finished on the day after as well as the return. But it could just as well have been finished on the day before. An ambiguity of this kind can usually be avoided by simply changing the order of the words, for instance: "I finished my business, and, on the day after, returned." Amphiboly can also arise from mixing up the subjects and predicates in a compound sentence. Take "platinum and iron are very rare and useful metals." It would be amphiboly if I meant the predicate useful to go with platinum and rare with iron, which is not what the sentence intends. The word "respectively" is often used to show the reader that he is not free to apply every predicate to every subject: each predicate goes with its own subject, in order.

The Fallacy of Composition is a special case of equivocation. It comes from confusing a universal term with a collective term. In the premises of a syllogism we may say something about a class of things distributively, that is, about each one of them separately, and then in the conclusion infer the same thing about the whole put together. We can say, for example, that "all the angles of a triangle are less than two right angles," meaning that any one of the angles is less than two right angles. But we must not infer that all the angles put together are less than two right angles. We must not argue that because every member of a jury is very likely to judge wrongly, the jury as a whole is also very likely to judge wrongly. Nor must we argue that because each of the witnesses in a court case may give false or mistaken evidence, no confidence can be placed in the agreeing testimony of a number of witnesses. It is through a fallacy of Composition that protective duties (tariffs that shield a home industry from foreign competition) are still sometimes defended. Because any one trade, or any few trades, that enjoy protective duties are benefited by them, people suppose that all trades at once could be benefited in the same way. But this is impossible, because protecting one trade raises prices, and so injures all the others.

The Fallacy of Division is the reverse of the one before. It consists in using the middle term collectively in the major premise but distributively in the minor, so that the whole is divided into its parts. For example, someone might argue: "All the angles of a triangle are (together) equal to two right angles; ABC is an angle of a triangle; therefore ABC is equal to two right angles." Or again: "The inhabitants of the town consist of men, women and children of all ages; those who met in the Guildhall (the town hall) were inhabitants of the town; therefore they consisted of men, women and children of all ages." Or: "The judges of the court of appeal cannot misinterpret the law; Lord A.B. is a judge of the court of appeal; therefore he cannot misinterpret the law."

The Fallacy of Accent is any ambiguity that comes from a misplaced accent, or emphasis, on some word of a sentence. An absurd example is liable to happen when reading 1 Kings 13:27, where it is said of the prophet: "And he spake to his sons, saying, Saddle me the ass. And they saddled him." ("Saddle me the ass" means "saddle the donkey for me.") The word him is printed in italics because the translators of the Authorized Version (the King James Bible) supplied it themselves. It is meant to refer to the ass, but read with a stress on it, it suggests a very different meaning: that the sons saddled their father. The Commandment "Thou shalt not bear false witness against thy neighbor" can be made, by a slight emphasis of the voice on the last word, to imply that we are free to bear false witness against other people. Mr. De Morgan, who points this out, also notes that misquoting an author, by unfairly separating a word from its context, or by italicizing words that were never meant to be italicized, produces cases of this fallacy.

It is curious to see how many different meanings the same sentence can be given, depending on which word we put the emphasis on. Take the sentence "The study of logic is not supposed to give us a knowledge of many useful facts." It can be made to imply that the study of logic does give such knowledge, even though it is not supposed to; or that it gives a knowledge of a few useful facts; or that it gives a knowledge of many useless facts. We can explain this ambiguity as follows. If you deny that a thing has the group of qualities A, B, C, D, your statement is true as long as any one of those qualities is missing, and an accent in speaking is often used to point out the one the speaker believes is missing. If you deny that a particular fruit is ripe and sweet and well-flavored, it may be unripe and sweet and well-flavored; or ripe and sour and well-flavored; or ripe and sweet and badly flavored; or any two, or even all three, of the qualities may be missing. But if you deny that it is ripe and sweet and well-flavored, your denial would be understood to refer to the last quality. Jeremy Bentham was so afraid of being misled by this fallacy of accent that, as I have heard, he employed a person to read to him who had a peculiarly monotonous way of reading.

The Fallacy of the Figure of Speech is the sixth and last of the semi-logical fallacies, and it is a very trivial one. It seems to consist in any grammatical mistake, or any confusion between one part of speech and another. Aristotle quite seriously gives this example: "Whatever a man walks he tramples on; a man walks the whole day; therefore he tramples on the day." Here an adverbial phrase ("the whole day," meaning for how long he walks) is turned into the object of the verb, as if the day were something he walks, the way he walks the streets.

Lesson 21: Material Fallacies

We come now to the Material fallacies. They matter a great deal, although they are not easy to illustrate with short examples. Aristotle listed seven kinds, and later logicians have kept his list:

1. The Fallacy of Accident.

2. The Converse Fallacy of Accident.

3. The Irrelevant Conclusion.

4. The Petitio Principii.

5. The Fallacy of the Consequent, or Non sequitur.

6. The False Cause.

7. The Fallacy of Many Questions.

The first two are most easily described together. The fallacy of accident is the mistake of arguing from a general rule to a special case where some accidental circumstance makes the rule not apply. The converse fallacy is the mistake of arguing from a special case to a general rule. This second fallacy is usually described by the Latin phrase a dicto secundum quid ad dictum simpliciter, which means "from a statement made under a condition to a statement made simply, without that condition." Professor De Morgan, in his very interesting Chapter on Fallacies, has pointed out that we ought to add a third fallacy of this kind: arguing from one special case to another special case.

Footnote: Formal Logic, Chapter XIII.

I will try to illustrate these kinds of fallacy with a few examples. Be warned, though: it is often very hard to say which of the three a particular example belongs to. Here is a very ancient example, repeated in almost every logic handbook: "What you bought yesterday you eat today; you bought raw meat yesterday; therefore you eat raw meat today." The conclusion makes its claim about meat with the accidental quality of rawness added. But the first premise is plainly talking about the meat as a substance, without regard to its accidental condition. So this is a case of the direct fallacy. If, on the other hand, someone argues that because wine acts as a poison when taken in excess it is always a poison, we have the converse fallacy.

Here is another case of the direct fallacy of accident: to infer that a magistrate is justified in using his power to advance his own religious views, because every man has a right to teach his own opinions. Clearly a magistrate, as a man, has the same rights as other men. But in his capacity as a magistrate he is set apart from other men, and he must not claim for his special powers what is true only of his rights as a man. For another instance, take this: "Anyone who thrusts a knife into another person should be punished; a surgeon does this when he operates; therefore he should be punished." The fallacy here is absurdly obvious, but it is not so obvious how we should classify the error. We might say that, as a general rule, whoever stabs or cuts another person is to be punished, unless it can be shown that it was done under exceptional circumstances, as by a properly qualified surgeon acting for the patient's good. Read that way, the example belongs to the direct fallacy of accident. Read another way, the first premise might mean only the special case of thrusting a knife into someone out of malice. To argue from that case to the case of a surgeon would then be to infer from one special case to another special case.

It is certainly true that giving to beggars encourages begging and does harm. But if we take this to mean that help should never be given to anyone who asks for it, we fall into the converse fallacy of accident: we infer about everyone who asks for charity what is true only of those who beg for a living. In the same way, it is a very good rule to avoid lawsuits and quarrels, but only as a general rule, because circumstances often arise in which going to law is a plain duty. Nearly all the difficulties we meet in matters of law and moral duty come from this: we can never be sure exactly which cases a legal or moral rule covers and which it does not. That is why there are endless differences of opinion, even among the judges of the land.

The Third Material Fallacy is the Irrelevant Conclusion, technically called the Ignoratio Elenchi, which means literally Ignorance of the Refutation. It consists in arguing to the wrong point: proving one thing in such a way that people suppose something else has been proved. Here again it is hard to give short examples, because this fallacy usually turns up in the course of long speeches, where the flood of words and figures leaves room for muddled thinking and forgetfulness. In fact, this fallacy is the great standby of anyone who has to defend a weak case. It is not unknown in the legal profession. The story goes that a solicitor for the defendant in a lawsuit once handed the barrister (the lawyer who would argue the case in court) his brief marked: "No case; abuse the plaintiff's attorney." Anyone who argues like this is using what is called the argumentum ad hominem, an argument that rests not on the merits of the case but on the character or position of the people involved in it, and he commits this fallacy. If a man is accused of a crime, it is no answer to say that the prosecutor is just as bad. If a great change in the law is proposed in Parliament, it is an Irrelevant Conclusion to argue that the person proposing it is not the right man to bring it forward. Everyone who gives advice lays himself open to the retort that he should practice what he preaches, or that people who live in glass houses should not throw stones. Even so, there is no necessary connection between the character of the person giving advice and the quality of the advice.

The argumentum ad populum is another form of Irrelevant Conclusion. It consists in addressing to a crowd arguments designed to stir up their feelings and keep them from judging the matter in hand calmly. It is the great weapon of orators and demagogues.

Petitio Principii is a familiar name, and the fallacy it names is exactly described by the phrase begging the question. Another good name for it is circulus in probando, "a circle in the proof." It consists in using the conclusion itself as one of the premises of the argument. Of course, the conclusion of a syllogism must always be contained in, or implied by, the premises. But it is contained in them only when the premises are put together, and the premises must be assertions clearly different from the conclusion. Take this syllogism:

	B is C,
	A is B,
	therefore A is C.

Here the conclusion is proved by being deduced from two propositions, neither of which is the same as the conclusion. But suppose the truth of one of these premises itself depends on the following syllogism:

	C is B,
	A is C,
	therefore A is B.

Then plainly we are trying to prove a proposition by means of itself, which makes as much sense as trying to hold a body up by itself. This kind of fallacy is not easy to illustrate with examples, because it usually happens in long arguments, and especially in wordy metaphysical writing. We are, however, very likely to fall into it when we mix plain Anglo-Saxon words with Latin or Greek ones, so that we seem to prove one proposition by another which is really the same proposition in different words. For example: "Consciousness must be immediate cognition of an object; for I cannot be said really to know a thing unless my mind has been affected by the thing itself."

This fallacy is especially likely to happen when we use the disjunctive syllogism. If we list only the alternatives that favor one view and forget the others, it is easy to prove anything at all. There is an instance of this in the famous sophism by which some of the ancient Greek philosophers proved that motion was impossible. A moving body, they said, must move either in the place where it is or in the place where it is not. Now it is absurd to say that a body can be where it is not; and if it moves, it cannot be in the place where it is. Therefore it cannot move at all. The error lies in assuming a premise that begs the question. The fact, of course, is that the body moves between the place where it is at one moment and the place where it is at the next.

Jeremy Bentham pointed out, however, that even a single name can carry a Petitio Principii with it. Suppose a church assembly, or synod, is discussing whether a certain doctrine ought to be condemned. It would be a Petitio Principii to argue that the doctrine is heresy and therefore ought to be condemned. To assert that it is heresy is to beg the question, because everyone understands by heresy a doctrine that is to be condemned. In the same way, in Parliament a bill is often opposed on the ground that it is unconstitutional and therefore ought to be rejected. But since no one can give a precise definition of what is or is not constitutional, this means little more than that the opponent dislikes the measure. Bentham gave names used in this fallacious way a good title: Question-begging Epithets. We beg the question in just the same way when we oppose a change by saying that it is un-English.

The Fallacy of the Consequent is better known by the familiar phrase non sequitur ("it does not follow"). We may give this name to any argument so loose and disconnected that no one can find any force in it at all. It amounts to little more than asserting a conclusion that has no connection with the premises. Professor De Morgan gives this example (episcopacy means the government of a church by bishops): "Episcopacy is of Scripture origin; the Church of England is the only episcopal Church in England; ergo, the Church established is the Church that should be supported."

By the Fallacy of the False Cause I mean the fallacy usually referred to by the Latin phrase non causa pro causâ (the non-cause taken for the cause). In this fallacy we assume, without sufficient grounds, that one thing is the cause of another. People still blame a change in the weather on the new moon or full moon that came shortly before it, although it has been shown over and over again that the moon can have no such effect. In earlier centuries, any plague or other public disaster that followed the appearance of a comet or an eclipse was thought to be its result. The Latin phrase post hoc ergo propter hoc ("after this, therefore because of this") describes exactly what is wrong with conclusions like these. We no longer fear signs and omens, but we still commit the fallacy often enough. We do it when we assume that all the prosperity of England comes from the national character, forgetting that the country's plentiful coal and its position on the sea have contributed to our material wealth. It is, no doubt, just as fallacious to give national character no importance at all; or to argue that because England has misgoverned Ireland in past centuries, all the present evils of Ireland are due to that misgovernment.

Last comes the rather trivial Fallacy of Many Questions. It is committed by anyone who combines two or three questions into one in such a way that no true answer can be given. I cannot think of a better example than the crude joke of asking, "Have you stopped beating your mother?" Questions just as unfair are constantly asked by barristers examining witnesses in court, and no one can fairly be required to answer Yes or No to every question that may be put to him. As Aristotle says, "Several questions put as one should be at once decomposed into their several parts. Only a single question admits of a single answer: so that neither several predicates of one subject, nor one predicate of several subjects, but only one predicate of one subject, ought to be affirmed or denied in a single answer."

Read Professor De Morgan's excellent and amusing Chapter on Fallacies, Formal Logic, Ch. xiii.

Whately's remarks on Fallacies, in his Elements of Logic, Book III, are often very original and sharp.

Questions on Fallacies

1. Classify the fallacies.

2. Explain the following expressions:

A dicto secundum quid ad dictum simpliciter; ignoratio elenchi; argumentum ad hominem; argumentum ad populum; petitio principii; circulus in probando; non sequitur; post hoc ergo propter hoc.

3. What is arguing in a circle, and what is a question-begging epithet?

4. What different meanings can be given to the following sentence by changing which word is stressed?

"Newton's discovery of gravitation is not generally believed to have been at all anticipated by several philosophers in England and Holland."

5. Point out the ways in which each of the following sentences could be misunderstood.

(1) He went to London and then to Brighton by the express train.

(2) Did you make a long speech at the meeting?

(3) How much is five times seven and nine?

Miscellaneous Examples

(Continued from the questions to Lesson 18.)

The following examples are partly sound arguments and partly false ones. The reader is asked to deal with them as follows:

1. If the example is not in a simple and complete logical form, put it into whatever complete form seems most fitting.

2. Decide whether it is a valid argument or a fallacy.

3. Give the exact name of the argument, or of the fallacy, as the case may be.

4. If it is a categorical syllogism, reduce it to the first figure.

5. If it is a hypothetical syllogism, state it in categorical form.

21. Elementary substances alone are metals. Iron is a metal; therefore it is an elementary substance.

22. No Athenians could have been Helots (the serfs of Sparta); for all the Helots were slaves, and all Athenians were free men.

23. Aristotle must have been a man of extraordinary industry; for only such a man could have produced his works.

24. Nothing is better than wisdom; dry bread is better than nothing; therefore dry bread is better than wisdom.

25. Pitt (William Pitt the Younger, the Prime Minister) was not a great and useful minister; for although he would have been one if he had carried out Adam Smith's doctrines of Free Trade, he did not carry out those doctrines.

26. Only the virtuous are truly noble; some who are called noble are not virtuous; therefore some who are called noble are not truly noble.

27. Ireland is idle and therefore starves; she starves, and therefore rebels.

28. No designing person ought to be trusted; engravers are designers by profession; therefore they ought not to be trusted.

29. Logic as the schoolmen (the philosophers of the medieval universities) practiced it proved a fruitless study; therefore logic as it is practiced today must be a fruitless study too.

30. Is a stone a body? Yes. Then is not an animal a body? Yes. Are you an animal? I think so. So you are a stone, since you are a body. (Lucian.)

31. "If ye were Abraham's children, ye would do the works of Abraham." (John 8:39.)

32. "He that is of God heareth God's words: ye therefore hear them not, because ye are not of God." (John 8:47.)

33. Muhammad was a wise lawgiver; for he studied the character of his people.

34. Everyone desires virtue, because everyone desires happiness.

35. His weakness of character might have been inferred from his fondness for favorites; for all weak princes have this failing. (De Morgan.)

36. He is brave who conquers his passions; he who resists temptation conquers his passions; so he who resists temptation is brave.

37. Suicide is not always to be condemned; for it is only voluntary death, and many of the greatest heroes of the ancient world gladly embraced voluntary death.

38. Since all metals are elements, the rarest of all the metals must be the rarest of all the elements.

39. The express train alone does not stop at this station; and since the last train did not stop, it must have been the express train.

40. Peel's remission of taxes was beneficial; the taxes Peel remitted were indirect taxes; therefore the remission of indirect taxes is beneficial. (Sir Robert Peel, as Prime Minister in the 1840s, abolished or cut many duties on goods.)

41. Books are a source both of instruction and of amusement; a table of logarithms is a book; therefore it is a source both of instruction and of amusement.

42. All desires are not blameworthy; all desires are liable to excess; therefore some things liable to excess are not blameworthy.

43. Whoever intentionally kills another person should suffer death; so a soldier who kills his enemy should suffer death.

44. Schemers are unfit to be trusted; this man has formed a scheme; therefore he is unfit to be trusted.

45. Few towns in the United Kingdom have more than 300,000 inhabitants; and since all such towns ought to be represented by three members in Parliament, it is clear that few towns ought to have three representatives.

46. All the works of Shakespeare cannot be read in a day; therefore the play of Hamlet, being one of the works of Shakespeare, cannot be read in a day.

47. In moral matters we cannot stand still; therefore whoever does not go forward is sure to fall behind.

48. The people of the country are suffering from famine; and since you are one of the people of the country, you must be suffering from famine.

49. Substances that are lighter than water can float on it; the metals that can float on it are potassium, sodium, lithium, and so on; therefore potassium, sodium, lithium, and so on, are lighter than water.
