50. The laws of nature must be discovered by Deduction, Traduction or Induction; but the first two are not enough for the purpose; therefore the laws of nature must be discovered by Induction.

51. A successful author must be either very hard-working or very talented; Gibbon was very hard-working; therefore he was not very talented.

52. You are not what I am; I am a man; therefore you are not a man.

53. The holder of some shares in a lottery is sure to win a prize; and since I am the holder of some shares in a lottery, I am sure to win a prize.

54. Gold and silver are wealth; and therefore a decrease of the gold and silver in the country by exporting it is a decrease of the wealth of the country.

55. Overly credulous people ought never to be believed; and since the Ancient Historians were in many instances overly credulous, they ought never to be believed.

56. Some mineral compounds are not decomposed by heat; all organic substances are decomposed by heat; therefore no organic substances are mineral compounds.

57. Whatever schools exclude religion are irreligious; non-sectarian schools do not allow the teaching of religious creeds; therefore they are irreligious.

58. Night must be the cause of day; for it always comes before it.

59. The ancient Greeks produced the greatest masterpieces of eloquence and philosophy; the Lacedaemonians (the Spartans) were ancient Greeks; therefore they produced the greatest masterpieces of eloquence and philosophy.

60. All presuming men are contemptible; this man, therefore, is contemptible; for he presumes to believe that his opinions are correct.

61. If a substance is solid it has elasticity, and so it does if it is liquid or gaseous; but all substances are either solid, liquid or gaseous; therefore all substances have elasticity.

62. If Parr's Life Pills (a popular patent medicine) are of any value, those who take them will improve in health; now my friend, who has been taking them, has improved in health; therefore they are of value.

63. He who calls you a man speaks truly; he who calls you a fool calls you a man; therefore he who calls you a fool speaks truly.

64. Whoever is most hungry eats most; whoever eats least is most hungry; therefore whoever eats least eats most.

65. Whatever produces drunkenness should be prohibited; the use of spirits (strong liquor) causes drunkenness; therefore the use of spirits should be prohibited.

66. What we eat grew in the fields; loaves of bread are what we eat; therefore loaves of bread grew in the fields.

67. If light consisted of material particles, it would have momentum; so it cannot consist of material particles, for it does not have momentum.

68. Everything that is morally right is allowed by law; indulgence in pleasures is allowed by law; therefore indulgence in pleasures is morally right.

69. All the trees in the park make a thick shade; this is one of them; therefore this tree makes a thick shade.

70. All visible bodies shine either by their own light or by reflected light. The moon does not shine by its own, therefore it shines by reflected light; but the sun shines by its own light, therefore it cannot shine by reflected light.

71. Honesty deserves reward, and a Black man is a fellow human being; therefore an honest Black man is a fellow human being who deserves reward.

72. Nearly all the satellites revolve around their planets from west to east; the moon is a satellite; therefore it revolves around its planet from west to east.

73. Italy is a Catholic country and is full of beggars; France is also a Catholic country, and therefore is full of beggars.

74. Every law is either useless or causes harm to someone; now a law that is useless ought to be abolished; and so ought every law that causes harm; therefore every law ought to be abolished.

75. The end of a thing is its perfection; death is the end of life; therefore death is the perfection of life.

76. When we hear that all righteous people are happy, it is hard not to exclaim: What! Are all the unhappy people we see to be thought unrighteous?

77. I am offered a sum of money to help this person get the office he wants; to help a person is to do him good, and no rule of morality forbids doing good; therefore no rule of morality forbids me to take the money for helping the person.

78. Ruminant animals (the cud-chewers) are those which have cloven hoofs, and they usually have horns; the extinct animal that left this footprint had a cloven hoof; therefore it was a ruminant animal and had horns. And again, since no beasts of prey are ruminant animals, it cannot have been a beast of prey.

79. We must either indulge our vicious tendencies or resist them; the first course will involve us in sin and misery; the second requires self-denial; therefore we must either fall into sin and misery or practice self-denial.

80. The stonemasons benefit from the masons' union; the bricklayers from the bricklayers' union; the hatmakers from the hatmakers' union; in short, every trade from its own union; so it is clear that if all workmen had unions, all workmen would benefit from them.

81. Every moral aim requires the rational means of reaching it; these means are the establishment of laws; and since happiness is the moral aim of human beings, it follows that reaching happiness requires the establishment of laws.

82. Whoever can swim need not despair of flying; for to swim is to fly in a coarser fluid, and to fly is to swim in a finer one.

83. If the Helvetii went through the country of the Sequani, they were sure to meet various difficulties; and if they went through the Roman province, they faced the danger of opposition from Caesar; but they had to go one way or the other; therefore either they were sure to meet various difficulties, or they faced the danger of opposition from Caesar. (Caesar, De Bello Gallico, Book I, 6.)

84. "Riches are for spending, and spending for honour and good actions; therefore extraordinary expense must be limited by the worth of the occasion." (Bacon.)

85. If light is not refracted near the surface of the moon, there cannot be any twilight there; but if the moon has no atmosphere, light is not refracted near its surface; therefore if the moon has no atmosphere, there cannot be any twilight.

86. The preservation of society requires exchange; whatever requires exchange requires a fair valuation of property; this requires the adoption of a common measure; so the preservation of society requires the adoption of a common measure.

87. The fact that the various species of brute animals were created to prey on one another proves that the human species was intended to prey on them.

88. The more correct the logic, the more certainly the conclusion will be wrong if the premises are false. Therefore, where the premises are wholly uncertain, the best logician is the least safe guide.

89. If our rulers could be trusted always to look to the best interests of their subjects, monarchy would be the best form of government; but they cannot be trusted; therefore monarchy is not the best form of government.

90. If people were prudent, they would act morally for their own good; if they were benevolent, they would do so for the good of others. But many people will not act morally, either for their own good or for that of others; such people, therefore, are not prudent or benevolent.

91. Whoever bears arms at the command of the magistrate does what is lawful for a Christian; the Swiss in the French service, and the British in the American service, bore arms at the command of the magistrate; therefore they did what was lawful for a Christian. (Whately.)

92. "A man that hath no virtue in himself ever envieth virtue in others; for men's minds will either feed upon their own good or upon others' evil; and who wanteth [lacks] the one will prey upon the other." (Bacon.)

93. The object of war is lasting peace; therefore soldiers are the best peacemakers.

94. Confidence in promises is essential to the dealings of human life; for without it most of our conduct would be left to chance. But there could be no confidence in promises if people were not obliged to keep them; therefore the obligation to keep promises is essential to the same ends, and in the same degree.

95. If the majority of those who use pubs (public houses) are prepared to close them, legislation is unnecessary; but if they are not prepared for such a measure, then to force it on them by outside pressure is both dangerous and unjust.

96. Whoever believes himself always to be in the right in his opinion lays claim to infallibility; you always believe yourself to be in the right in your opinion; therefore you lay claim to infallibility. (Whately.)

97. If we never find skins except as the coverings of animals, we may safely conclude that animals cannot exist without skins. If color cannot exist by itself, it follows that nothing colored can exist without color either. In the same way, if language without thought is unreal, thought without language must be unreal too.

98. No soldiers should be brought into the field who are not well qualified to do their part; none but veterans are well qualified to do their part; therefore none but veterans should be brought into the field. (Whately.)

99. The minimum visibile is the smallest size that can be seen; no part of it alone is visible, and yet every part of it must affect the mind in order for it to be visible; therefore every part of it must affect the mind without being visible.

100. The scarlet poppy belongs to the genus Papaver, of the natural order Papaveraceae; this order is part of the subclass Thalamiflorae, which belongs to the great class of Dicotyledons. So the scarlet poppy is one of the Dicotyledons.

101. Improbable events happen almost every day; but what happens almost every day is a very probable event; therefore improbable events are very probable events. (Whately.)

Recent Logical Views

Lesson 22: The Quantification of the Predicate

In the last three lessons the syllogism has been explained almost exactly as it has been taught for more than two thousand years. Geometry has been taught in the way and the order first laid down by the ancient Greek writer Euclid, and in just the same way logic has been taught very nearly as Aristotle taught it, around the year 335 B.C.

But within the last few years, teachers in England have at last come to the conclusion that Euclid's ideas of geometry are not as perfect as they might be. And over the last thirty or forty years it has gradually become clear that Aristotle's syllogism is not an absolutely perfect system of logical deduction either. In fact, certain eminent writers, especially Sir William Hamilton, Professor De Morgan, Archbishop Thomson and George Boole, have shown that we need to make improvements from the very foundations of the science.

This reform in logic goes by the rather mysterious name of the quantification of the predicate. But a reader who has found no impossible difficulty in the lessons so far need not fear one here. To quantify the predicate simply means to state whether the whole of the predicate, or only part of it, agrees with or differs from the subject. Take this proposition:

	"All metals are elements."

Here the subject is quantified, but the predicate is not. We know that all metals are elements, but the proposition does not say plainly whether the metals make up the whole of the elements or not. Now take the quantified proposition

	"All metals are some elements."

The little word "some" says clearly that the metals really form only a part of the elements. Aristotle avoided using any mark of quantity on the predicate. As we have seen, he assumed that every affirmative proposition has a particular predicate, like the example just given, and that only negative propositions have a distributed, or universal, predicate. The fact is, however, that he was entirely wrong about this. As a result he shut out of his system an infinite number of affirmative propositions that are universal in both terms. It is true that "All equilateral triangles are all equiangular triangles," but this proposition could have appeared in his system only in the mutilated form

	"All equilateral triangles are equiangular."

A proposition such as

	"London is the capital of England,"

or "Iron is the cheapest metal," had no proper place at all in his syllogism. In each of these, both terms are singular and identical with each other, and so both are universal.

As soon as we allow the quantity of the predicate to be stated, the forms of reasoning become much simpler. Let us first look at the process of conversion. In our lesson on that subject we had to distinguish between conversion by limitation and simple conversion. Now a single process, simple conversion, is enough for every kind of proposition. So the quantified proposition of the form A,

	"All metals are some elements,"

is converted simply into

	"Some elements are all metals."

The particular affirmative proposition

	"Some metals are some brittle substances"

becomes, just by swapping the terms around,

	"Some brittle substances are some metals."

The particular negative proposition

	"Some men are not (any) trustworthy persons"

is also converted simply, into

	"Not any trustworthy persons are some men,"

though the result may seem less satisfactory in this form than in the affirmative form that follows:

	"Some men are some not-trustworthy persons,"

converted simply into

	"Some not-trustworthy persons are some men."

The universal negative proposition E is converted simply, as before. And finally we have a new affirmative proposition, universal in both subject and predicate, as in "All equilateral triangles are all equiangular triangles." This can obviously be converted simply into

	"All equiangular triangles are all equilateral triangles."

This doubly universal affirmative proposition is extremely common: every definition and every singular proposition is one. I may give as examples "Honesty is the best policy," "The greatest truths are the simplest truths," "Virtue alone is happiness below," and "Self-exaltation is the fool's paradise."

When affirmative propositions are put in quantified form, every immediate inference can easily be drawn from them by one rule: whatever we do to one term, we should do to the other. So from the doubly universal proposition "Honesty is the best policy" we infer that "what is not the best policy is not honesty," and also that "what is not honesty is not the best policy." In fact we can draw two contrapositives from this proposition. But the reader should carefully remember that from the ordinary, unquantified proposition A we can draw only one contrapositive (see Lesson 10). So if "metals are elements," we must not say that "what are not metals are not elements." But if we quantify the predicate, as in "All metals are some elements," we may infer that "what are not metals are not some elements." Immediate inference by added determinant and by complex conception can also be applied in either direction to quantified propositions, without any fear of the errors pointed out in Lesson 10.

Once we admit the mark of quantity in front of the predicate, it is clear that we double the number of propositions the syllogism must take in, because the predicate of each of the four propositions A, E, I, O may be either universal or particular. So we arrive at a list of eight conceivable kinds of proposition, set out in the following table.

UAll X is all Y.Affirmative.
ISome X is some Y.Affirmative.
AAll X is some Y.Affirmative.
YSome X is all Y.Affirmative.
ENo X is (any) Y.Negative.
ωSome X is not some Y.Negative.
ηNo X is some Y.Negative.
OSome X is no Y.Negative.

The letters X and Y stand for any subject and any predicate, and by putting in various terms the reader can easily make up propositions of each kind. The symbolic letters on the left were proposed by Archbishop Thomson as a convenient way of referring to each of the eight propositions, and they are very well chosen. The doubly universal affirmative proposition is called U. The simple converse of A is called Y. The Greek letter η (Eta, ē) is given to the proposition we get by changing the universal predicate of E into a particular predicate, and the Greek ω (Omega, ō) to the proposition we get in the same way from O. Sir William Hamilton uses all eight of these propositions, but Archbishop Thomson considers that two of them, η and ω, are never really used. It is a remarkable fact that a complete table of these eight propositions was given by Mr. George Bentham in a work called Outline of a New System of Logic, published in 1827, several years before the earliest of Hamilton's writings on logic. But Mr. Bentham considered that some of the propositions can hardly be told apart from others: Y from A, of which it is the simple converse, for instance, or η from O.

Even using only the two extra propositions U and Y brought in by Thomson extends the list of possible syllogisms a great deal, to 62 in all, without counting the fourth figure, which Hamilton and Thomson do not use. When all eight propositions are admitted, we have to extend the list of possible syllogisms to 12 affirmative and 24 negative moods in each of the first three figures. All of these moods are conveniently set out in the table below, which Archbishop Thomson gives at p. 188 of his Laws of Thought.

	Table of Moods of the Syllogism.
First Figure, Affirm.First Figure, Neg.Second Fig., Affirm.Second Fig., Neg.Third Figure, Affirm.Third Figure, Neg.
iUUUEUE / UEEUUUEUE / UEEUUUEUE / UEE
iiAYIηYω / AOωYYIOYω / YOωAAIηAω / Aηω
iiiAAAηAη / AηηYAAOAη / YηηAYAηYη / AOη
ivYYYOYO / YOOAYYηYO / AOOYAYOAO / YηO
vAIIηIω / AωωYIIOIω / YωωAIIηIω / Aωω
viIYIωYω / IOωIYIωYω / IOωIAIωAω / Iηω
viiUYYEYO / UOOUYYEYO / UOOUAYEAO / UηO
viiiAUAηUη / AEηYUAOUη / YEηAUAηUη / AEη
ixUAAEAE / UηηUAAEAE / UηηUYAEYE / UOη
xYUYOUO / YEEAUYηUO / AEEYUYOUO / YEE
xiUIIEIO / UωωUIIEIO / UωωUIIEIO / Uωω
xiiIUIωUω / IEηIUIωUω / IEηIUIωUω / IEη

Sir William Hamilton also invented a curious system of notation for showing all the moods of the syllogism clearly. He always used the letter M for the middle term of the syllogism, and the two letters C and Γ (the Greek capital letter Gamma) for the two terms that appear in the conclusion. He showed the copula of the proposition by a line thickened toward the subject, so that C ——— M means "C is M." To show the quantity of the terms, Hamilton put a colon (:) between the term and the copula when the quantity is universal, and a comma (,) when it is particular. In this way we can easily express the following affirmative propositions:

and so on. Any affirmative proposition can be turned into the corresponding negative proposition by drawing a stroke through the line that stands for the copula, as in the

Any syllogism can be shown by putting M, the middle term, in the center and joining it on each side to the other terms. The copula representing the conclusion can then be put underneath. Barbara is expressed like this:

The negative mood Celarent looks like this:

Cesare, in the second figure, is shown like this:

Sir William Hamilton also proposed a new law, or supreme canon, of the syllogism, by which the validity of every form of the syllogism could be tested. He stated it in these words: "What worse relation of subject and predicate subsists between either of two terms and a common third term, with which both are related, and one at least positively so—that relation subsists between these two terms themselves."

By a "worse" relation, Sir William means that a negative relation is worse than an affirmative one, and a particular relation worse than a universal one. So this canon expresses the rules that if there is a negative premise the conclusion must be negative, and if there is a particular premise the conclusion must be particular. Special canons were also worked out for each of the three figures. But in making the system so complicated, the advantages of the quantified form of proposition seem to be lost.

Professor De Morgan also discovered the advantages of the quantified predicate, and invented a system very different from Hamilton's. It is fully explained in his Formal Logic, in The Syllabus of a new System of Logic, and in various important papers on the syllogism in the Transactions of the Cambridge Philosophical Society. These works also give a complete explanation of the "Numerically Definite Syllogism." De Morgan pointed out that two particular premises can often give a valid conclusion, provided that the actual quantities of the two terms are stated and that, added together, they exceed the quantity of the middle term. Suppose a majority of a public meeting vote for the first resolution, and a majority also vote for the second. Then it follows necessarily that some who voted for the first also voted for the second. The two majorities added together are more than the whole number of people at the meeting, so they cannot be made up of entirely different people. They may, indeed, be made up of exactly the same people. But all we can deduce from the premises is that the excess of the two majorities added together over the number at the meeting must have voted for both resolutions. Hamilton said that this kind of inference depends on ultra-total distribution, and the name Plurative Propositions has been proposed for all those propositions that give a definite idea of what fraction or number of the subject the assertion is about.

T. Spencer Baynes, Essay on the new Analytic of Logical Forms; Edinburgh, 1850.

Professor Bowen's Treatise on Logic or the Laws of Pure Thought, Cambridge, U.S., 1866 (Trübner and Co.), gives a full and excellent account of Hamilton's logic.

Questions and Exercises

1. What does the quantification of the predicate mean?

2. Give each of the following propositions its proper symbol, and give the symbol of its converse:

(1) Knowledge is power.

(2) Some rectangles are all squares.

(3) Only the honest ultimately prosper.

(4) Princes have only their titles for their glories.

(5) In man there is nothing great but mind.

(6) The end of philosophy is the detection of unity.

3. Draw all the contrapositive propositions and immediate inferences you can from the following propositions:

(1) London is a great city.

(2) London is the capital of England.

(3) All ruminant animals are all cloven-footed animals.

(4) Some members of parliament are all the ministers.

4. Write out the moods Baroko, Darapti, Felapton and Bokardo in Hamilton's notation.

Lesson 23: Boole's System of Logic

In an elementary book like this one, it would be quite impossible to give any real idea of the system of indirect inference first discovered by the late George Boole, Professor of Mathematics at Queen's College, Cork. As I mentioned in the last lesson, his system was founded on the Quantification of the Predicate. But Boole regarded Logic as a branch of Mathematics, and he believed he could reach every possible inference by the principles of algebra. The process he actually used is very obscure and difficult, and so far hardly anyone has tried to bring it into elementary textbooks of Logic.

I have been able to reach exactly the same results as Boole without using any mathematics at all. The very simple process I am going to describe can hardly be called Boole's logic in the strict sense, but it is very similar to it, and it can prove everything Boole proved. This Method of Indirect Inference is founded on the three primary Laws of Thought stated in Lesson 14. Any reader who thought those laws were useless truisms may be surprised to find how wide and elegant a system of deduction can be built on them.

The law of excluded middle lets us assert that anything must either have a given quality or not have it. If the thing is iron, say, and the quality is combustibility, anyone can see that

	"Iron is either combustible or incombustible."

We can repeat this division into alternatives as often as we like. Let Book be the class of things to be divided, and let English and Scientific be two qualities. Then any book must be either English or not English. An English book, in turn, must be either Scientific or not Scientific, and the same goes for books that are not English. So we can divide books straight away into four classes:

	Books, English and Scientific.
	Books, English and not-Scientific.
	Books, not-English and Scientific.
	Books, not-English and not-Scientific.

We may call this an exhaustive division of the class Books. There is no possible book that does not fall into one or other of these four divisions, for the simple reason that if it does not fall into any of the first three, it must fall into the last. The process can go on without end, as long as anyone can suggest a new circumstance to divide by. We might divide each class again according to whether the books are octavo (a standard mid-sized format) or not octavo, bound or unbound, published in London or elsewhere, and so on. This process of twofold division is really the process of Dichotomy described in Lesson 12. We shall call it the development of a term, because it always lets us develop the greatest number of alternatives that need to be considered.

As a general rule, it is unlikely that all the alternatives unfolded, or developed, in this way can actually exist. The next step is to find out how many do or may exist. The Law of Contradiction asserts that nothing can combine contradictory attributes or qualities, so if we meet any term that contradicts itself in this way, we are entitled to strike it out of the list at once. Now take our old example of a syllogism:

	Iron is a metal;
	All metals are elements;
	Therefore iron is an element.

We can easily prove this conclusion by the indirect method. If we develop the term iron, we get four alternatives:

	Iron, metal, element.
	Iron, metal, not-element.
	Iron, not-metal, element.
	Iron, not-metal, not-element.

Now compare each of these alternatives with the premises of the syllogism, and it becomes clear that several of them cannot exist. We are told that iron is a metal, so no class of things "iron, not-metal" can exist. The first premise therefore lets us strike out both of the last two alternatives, since they combine iron with not-metal. The second alternative combines metal with not-element. But the second premise tells us that "all metals are elements," so that one must be struck out too. Only one alternative remains that can exist if the premises are true. And since there cannot possibly be any alternatives beyond the ones we have considered, it follows by strict demonstration that iron occurs only together with the qualities of metal and element; in short, that iron is an element.

But we can prove more than the ordinary syllogistic conclusion. We can prove any other conclusion that can be drawn from the same premises. In fact, the syllogistic conclusion is usually only one of many that can be got from a given set of premises. Suppose, for instance, that we want to know what the term or class not-element is like, as far as the premises we have just used can tell us. We can develop the alternatives of this term just as we did those of iron, and we get the following:

	Not-element, iron, metal.
	Not-element, iron, not-metal.
	Not-element, not-iron, metal.
	Not-element, not-iron, not-metal.

Compare these combinations with the premises, as before. The first plainly cannot exist, because all metals are elements, and the third cannot exist for the same reason. The second is excluded as well, because iron is a metal and cannot exist together with the qualities of not-metal. So only one combination remains to represent the class we wanted, namely

	Not-element, not-iron, not-metal.

So the premises teach us that every not-element is not a metal and is not iron.

For another example of this kind of deduction, I will take a case of the Disjunctive Syllogism in the negative mood:

	A fungus is either plant or animal,
	A fungus is not an animal;
	Therefore it is a plant.

If we develop all the possible ways in which fungus, plant and animal can be combined, we get, for the term fungus:

	(1) Fungus, plant, animal.
	(2) Fungus, plant, not-animal.
	(3) Fungus, not-plant, animal.
	(4) Fungus, not-plant, not-animal.

Of these, the 4th cannot exist, because according to the premise a fungus must be a plant, or, if not a plant, an animal. The 1st and 3rd cannot exist either, because the minor premise tells us that a fungus is not an animal. That leaves only the second combination,

	Fungus, plant, not-animal,

and from it we learn the syllogistic conclusion that "a fungus is a plant."

The chief strength of this way of deducing is that it is not tied to a fixed series of forms, as the syllogism is. Without any extra rules, it applies to every kind of proposition or problem that can be conceived and stated. There may be any number of premises, and they may contain any number of terms. To get any possible inference, all we have to do is develop the term we want into all its alternatives and then see how many of them agree with the premises. Whatever is left after this examination is, necessarily, the description of the term. The method's only drawback is that the number of alternatives to examine grows very fast as the number of terms grows, and writing them all out soon becomes tedious. We can shorten the work by putting single letters in place of the terms, somewhat as in algebra. We may take A, B, C, D, and so on, to stand for the affirmative terms, and a, b, c, d, and so on, for the corresponding negative ones. For a first example, take the premises:

	Organic substance is either vegetable or animal.
	Vegetable substance consists mainly of carbon, hydrogen, and nitrogen.
	Animal substance consists mainly of carbon, hydrogen, and nitrogen.

It would take a long time to write out all the combinations of the four terms that occur here. But if we put letters in place of them, like this,

	A = organic substance,
	B = vegetable substance,
	C = animal substance,
	D = consisting mainly of carbon, hydrogen, and nitrogen,

we can easily set out all the combinations that can belong to the term A:

(1)ABCDAbCD(5)
(2)ABCdAbCd(6)
(3)ABcDAbcD(7)
(4)ABcdAbcd(8)

Now the premises amount to the statements that

	A must be either B or C,
	B must be D,
	C must be D.

Combinations (7) and (8) are inconsistent with the first premise; combinations (2) and (4) with the second premise; and (6) is inconsistent with the third premise. That leaves only

	ABCD
	ABcD
	AbCD.

From this we learn at once that "organic substance (A) always consists mainly of carbon, hydrogen and nitrogen," because it always occurs together with D. The reader may notice that the combination ABCD implies that an organic substance may be both vegetable (B) and animal (C). If the first premise is taken to mean that this is impossible, then of course this combination should be struck out as well. Whether the alternatives of a disjunctive proposition can exist together or not is an unsettled question (see Lesson 19), but I much prefer the view that they can. And as a matter of fact, it is quite likely that there are very simple kinds of living things that cannot be said for certain to be only vegetable or only animal, but share the nature of both.

To show the power of this system on a harder problem, let us take the premises Boole treated in his Laws of Thought, p. 125:

"Similar figures consist of all whose corresponding angles are equal, and whose corresponding sides are proportional.

Triangles whose corresponding angles are equal have their corresponding sides proportional; and vice versa.

Triangles whose corresponding sides are proportional have their corresponding angles equal."

Now suppose we choose our letters as follows:

	A = similar figure,
	B = triangle,
	C = having corresponding angles equal,
	D = having corresponding sides proportional.

Then the premises turn out to amount to the statements that

	A is identical with CD,

and that

	BC is identical with BD.

In other words, all A's must be CD's, and CD's must be A's; all BC's must be BD's, and all BD's must be BC's.

The letters can be combined in 16 possible ways, shown in the following table:

ABCDaBCD
ABCdaBCd
ABcDaBcD
ABcdaBcd
AbCDabCD
AbCdabCd
AbcDabcD
Abcdabcd

Comparing each of these combinations with the premises, we see that ABCd, ABcD, ABcd and others must be struck out, because every A has to be CD as well. The combinations aBCD and abCD are struck out because every CD must also be A. Again, aBCd is inconsistent with the condition that every BC must also be BD. If the reader carefully carries the same examination through to the end, only six combinations will be left that agree with all the premises:

ABCDaBcd
AbCDabCd
abcD
abcd

From these combinations we can draw any description we like of the classes of things that agree with the premises. The class A, or similar figures, is represented by only two combinations, or alternatives. The negative class a, or dissimilar figures, is represented by four. So we may draw the following conclusion: "Dissimilar figures consist of all triangles that do not have their corresponding angles equal and their sides proportional (aBcd), and of all figures that are not triangles and that have either their angles equal but their sides not proportional (abCd), or their corresponding sides proportional but their angles not equal (abcD), or neither their corresponding angles equal nor their corresponding sides proportional (abcd)."

Anyone who works this method of inference soon sees that it runs in a very simple, mechanical way, and that its only drawback is the large number of alternatives, or combinations, to be examined. So I have invented several ways of cutting down the labor. The simplest is to engrave the series of 16 combinations in the table above, which turn up again and again in problems, along with larger and smaller sets, on an ordinary writing slate. Then the excluded combinations can easily be struck out with an ordinary slate pencil, and the slate can still be used again for any later logical question. A second device, which I have called the "Logical abacus," is made by printing the letters on slips of wood fitted with pins, arranged so that any part or class of the combinations can be picked out mechanically with very little trouble. A logical problem is then solved by the hand rather than by the head. More recently, though, I have reduced the system to a completely mechanical form, and so built the whole indirect process of inference into what may be called a Logical Machine. On the front of the machine are some movable wooden rods carrying the set of 16 combinations of letters shown in the table above. At the foot are 21 keys like those of a piano. Eight keys toward the left are marked with the letters A, a, B, b, C, c, D, d, and they stand for these terms when they occur in the subject of a proposition. Eight more keys toward the right stand for the same letters, or terms, when they occur in the predicate. The copula of a proposition is represented by a key in the middle of the row, and the full stop by a key at the far right. Two other keys serve for the disjunctive conjunction or, one for when it occurs in the subject and one for when it occurs in the predicate. Now let the letters stand for the terms of a syllogism, or of any other logical argument, and press the keys of the instrument in exactly the order of the words of the premises. The 16 combinations will be sorted and arranged by this so that at the end only the possible combinations remain in view. Any question can then be put to the machine, and the combinations that remain will give an infallible answer. So the internal workings of the machine actually perform the work of inference that, in Boole's system, was done by a very complicated mathematical calculation. I should add that there is one last key at the far left, which wipes out all the previous operations and puts all the combinations back in their original places, so that the machine is ready for a new problem.

An account of this logical machine can be found in the Proceedings of the Royal Society for January 20, 1870; on that day the machine was shown working to the Fellows of the Society. The principles of the method of inference described here are set out more fully in The Substitution of Similars and in the Principles of Science, which I published in 1869 and 1874. I may add that each of these works contains certain views about the real nature of the process of inference that I do not think it wise to bring into an elementary book like this one, because they are speculative. The process of inference I have taken from Boole's system, on the other hand, is so self-evident, and so clearly proved true by being reduced to a mechanical form, that I do not hesitate to bring it to the reader's attention.

Footnote: The Substitution of Similars, the true Principle of Reasoning, derived from a modification of Aristotle's Dictum. Macmillan and Co. 1869.

Footnote: The Principles of Science: a Treatise on Logic and Scientific Method. 2 vols. Macmillan and Co.

George Boole, Mathematical Analysis of Logic, 1847.

An Investigation of the Laws of Thought. London, Walton and Maberly, 1854.

Questions and Exercises

1. Apply this system of inference to prove the syllogisms in Cesare and Camestres given in Lesson 16.

2. Show that if no A's are B's, then no B's are A's; and that if all A's are all B's, then all not-A's are all not-B's.

3. Develop the term substance with respect to the terms vegetable, animal, organic. Then pick out the combinations that agree with these premises: "What is vegetable is not animal but is organic; what is animal is organic."

4. Test whether this argument is valid: "Good always triumphs, and vice always fails; therefore the victor cannot be wrong, nor the vanquished right."

5. It is known of a certain class of things that:

(1) Where the quality A is, B is not.

(2) Where B is, and only where B is, C and D are. What can we infer from these premises about the class of things in which A is not present but C is present?

6. If all A's are B's, all B's are C's, and all C's are D's, show that all A's are D's, and that all not-D's are not A's.

Method

Lesson 24: On Method, Analysis and Synthesis

Many writers on logic have held that logical doctrine has a fourth part, in addition to the three parts that deal in turn with Terms, Propositions and Syllogisms. That fourth part is method. The doctrine of Judgment considers how terms are arranged and combined into propositions. The doctrine of the Syllogism considers how propositions are arranged so that they form arguments. In the same way, they said, there should be a fourth part, called Method, to govern how syllogisms are arranged and combined into a complete discourse. So method is defined as arranging the parts of a discourse so that the whole is as easy as possible to understand.

The famous Peter Ramus, who was killed in the Massacre of St. Bartholomew's Day (Paris, 1572), was the first to propose making method a part of the science of logic in this way. But we may well doubt whether any definite set of rules or principles can be given to guide us in arranging arguments. Every discourse has to be made of arguments arranged to suit the particular nature of its subject. Things vary without end in the way they need to be treated, and no general rules can be given for treating them. So the supposed general rules of method are no better than truisms: they tell us nothing we must not be supposed to know already. For example, we are told that in composing any discourse we should take care that:

1. Nothing should be missing or superfluous.

2. The separate parts should agree with each other.

3. Nothing should be treated unless it suits the subject or purpose.

4. The separate parts should be connected by suitable transitions.

But obviously the whole difficulty lies in deciding what is missing or superfluous, what is suitable or consistent. Rules of this kind simply tell us to do what we ought to do, without saying what that is.

All the same, there are certain general ways of treating any subject that can be clearly told apart, and the student of logic should understand them well. Logic cannot teach him exactly how and when to use each kind of method. But it can teach him what the methods are and what they can do, so that he is more likely to use them rightly. We must distinguish:

	1. The method of discovery,
	2. The method of instruction.

The method of discovery is the one we use in acquiring knowledge. It really consists of the processes of inference and induction by which we find out general truths from collecting and examining particular facts. This method will be the subject of most of our remaining lessons. The second method applies only when knowledge has already been acquired and expressed in the form of general laws, rules, principles or truths. Then all we have to do is get to know these, and learn the proper way to apply them to particular cases, and we will have a complete knowledge of the subject.

Take a student learning Latin, Greek, French, German, or any other well-known language. He is given a complete grammar and syntax that set out all the principles, rules and nature of the language. He receives these instructions and takes them as true on the authority of his teacher, or of the writer of the book. Once he has made them familiar, he has nothing left to do but combine and apply the rules in reading or writing the language. In short, he follows the method of instruction. But the scholar who has been handed some writings in an unknown language, and is trying to work out its alphabet, words, grammar and syntax, has to go about it in a completely different, opposite way. He doesn't have the laws of the grammar. He has words and sentences that obey those laws, and he has to detect the laws, if he can, by watching their effects on the written language. In short, he follows the method of discovery. It consists of a tedious comparison of letters, words and phrases, carried out so as to reveal the forms and combinations in which they most often occur. The process would be a strictly inductive one, of the kind I will partly illustrate in the lessons on induction. But it is far harder than the method of instruction, and it depends a great deal on making good use of conjecture and hypothesis, which takes a certain skill and inventiveness.

Exactly the same is true of investigating natural things and events. The principles of mechanics, such as the lever, the inclined plane and the other Mechanical Powers, or the Laws of Motion, seem fairly simple and obvious when they are explained to us in textbooks. But the early philosophers had no such books. They had only the Book of Nature, which sets out not the laws but the results of the laws. It took the most patient and skillful investigation, and hundreds of mistakes, before those laws were found out. Today it is very easy to understand the Copernican system of astronomy, which has the planets revolving around the sun in orbits of different sizes. Once we know the theory, we can easily see why the planets move in such different ways and appear in such different positions, and why they sometimes seem to stand still. It is easy to see, too, why on top of their own proper motions they all seem to go around the earth every day, because the earth turns on its axis daily. But all these changes were extremely puzzling to the ancients, who believed the earth stood still.

So the method of discovery begins with facts that are apparent to the senses, and it has the hard task of detecting the universal laws or general principles that only the intellect can grasp. It has been well said that the method of discovery goes from the things that are better known to us, that is, to our senses (nobis notiora), to the things that are simpler, or better known in nature (notiora naturæ). The method of instruction goes in the opposite direction. It begins with the things notiora naturæ and goes on to show or explain the things nobis notiora. The difference is almost like the difference between hiding and seeking. Whoever hid a thing knows where to find it. A discoverer is not in that position. He has no clue except what he may come across in his own careful and shrewd search.

Closely matching the distinction between the methods of discovery and instruction is the distinction between the methods of analysis and synthesis. It is very important that the reader understand clearly what these terms mean in their various uses. Analysis is the process of separating a whole into its parts, and synthesis is the combining of parts into a whole. An analytical chemist who is given a piece of mineral to examine may be able to separate completely the chemical elements it is made of, and find out what they are and in what proportions they are present. This is chemical analysis. At other times the chemist mixes carefully weighed amounts of certain simple substances and combines them into a new compound substance. This is chemical synthesis. Logical analysis and synthesis must not be confused with these physical actions. Even so, they are actions of the mind of a similar kind.

In logical synthesis we begin with the simplest possible notions or ideas and combine them. The best possible example is elementary geometry. In Euclid we begin with certain simple notions: points, straight lines, angles, right angles, circles and so on. Put three straight lines together and we make a triangle. Add to this the notion of a right angle, and we form the notion of a right-angled triangle. Join four equal lines at right angles to each other and we get the idea of a square. Now suppose we imagine such a square built on each side of a right-angled triangle, and reason from the necessary qualities of these figures. We discover that the two squares on the sides that contain the right angle must together be exactly equal to the square on the third side, as is shown in the 47th Proposition of Euclid's first book. This is a perfect example of combining simple ideas into more complex ones.

Often, though, in geometry we have to take the opposite course, analysis. We may be given a complicated geometrical figure, and in order to prove the properties it has, we may have to break it up into its separate parts and consider the properties of each part separately from the others.

A similar distinction between the analytic and the synthetic methods can be traced all through the natural sciences. By keeping exact records of how the weather looks and changes, we can easily build up an immense collection of facts. Each recorded fact involves a crowd of different circumstances occurring together. In any storm or shower of rain, for instance, we have to consider the direction and strength of the wind; the temperature and dampness of the air; the height and shapes of the clouds; the amount of rain that falls, or the lightning and thunder that come with it. If we tried to explain the changes of the weather by analysis alone, we would have to try breaking each storm or change of weather down into its separate circumstances, and comparing each with every other, to find out which circumstances usually go together. No doubt we could find out in this way, with considerable certainty, what kinds of clouds and what changes of wind, temperature, moisture and so on usually come before each kind of storm. In time we might even give some imperfect explanation of what goes on in the atmosphere.

But we could also make good use of the synthetic method. Earlier chemical investigations tell us that the atmosphere consists mainly of two permanent gases, oxygen and nitrogen, together with water vapor, whose amount varies a great deal. We can test by experiment what happens when portions of such air, at various degrees of dampness, are compressed or allowed to expand, or are mixed together, as often happens in the atmosphere. In this way we discover that whenever moist air is allowed to expand, cloud is produced, and perhaps drops of rain. Dr. Hutton, too, found that whenever cold moist air is mixed with warm moist air, cloud is again produced. We can safely argue from small experiments like these to what happens in the atmosphere. By putting together synthetically everything that the sciences of chemistry, mechanics and electricity tell us about air, wind, cloud and lightning, we can explain what happens in a thunderstorm far more completely than we could by simply watching what happens in the storm. Here, however, we are getting ahead of ourselves, into the methods of inductive investigation, which we must consider in the following lessons. It will turn out that induction is equivalent to analysis, and that the deductive kinds of reasoning we have dealt with in the earlier lessons are synthetic in character.

It has been said that the synthetic method usually corresponds to the method of instruction, and the analytic method to the method of discovery. But it is possible to discover new truths by synthesis and to teach old ones by analysis. Sir John Herschel, in his well-known Outlines of Astronomy, partly adopts the analytic method. He imagines a spectator who first surveys the appearances of the heavenly bodies and of the earth's surface and looks for an explanation. Then he leads him through a series of arguments showing that these appearances really point to the roundness of the earth, its turning on its own axis and its revolution around the sun, and its humble position as one of the smaller planets of the solar system. Mr. Norman Lockyer's Elementary Lessons in Astronomy is a clear example of the synthetic method of instruction. He starts by describing the sun, the center of the system, and then adds the planets and the other members of the system one after another, until at last we have the complete picture. The reader, who has for the time being taken everything on the writer's authority, then sees that the description matches the truth. Each method, we must admit, has its own advantages.

We must take careful note that the meaning of analysis, and so the meaning of synthesis, changes depending on whether we are looking at terms in intension or in extension. To divide, or analyze, a class of things in extension, I must add a quality, or difference. For example, I divide the class organism when I add the quality vegetable, and separate vegetable organism from what is not vegetable. So analysis in extension is the same process as synthesis in intension. And the other way around: whenever I separate, or analyze, a group of qualities, each part belongs to a larger class of things in extension. When I analyze the notion vegetable organism, and consider the notion organism apart from vegetable, I am obviously adding the whole class of animal organisms to the class I am considering. So analysis in intension is synthesis in extension. A reader who has thought carefully about Lessons 5 and 12 will probably see that this connection between the two processes is just a restatement of the law (see Lesson 5) that "as the intension of a term is increased, the extension is decreased."

To express the difference between knowledge gained deductively and knowledge gained inductively, the Latin phrases a priori and a posteriori are often used. By a priori reasoning we mean argument based on truths already known. A posteriori reasoning, on the other hand, works from the consequences of a general truth to infer what that general truth is. Many philosophers believe that the mind naturally possesses certain laws or truths that it must recognize in every act of thought. All such truths, if there are any, would be a priori truths. No one can doubt, for example, that in thinking we must always recognize the three Primary Laws of Thought discussed in Lesson 14. There we have a priori knowledge that "matter cannot both have weight and be without weight," or that "everything must be either self-luminous or not self-luminous." But no law of thought can force us to think that matter has weight while the luminous ether (the medium that was then believed to carry light) has no weight, or that Jupiter and Venus are not self-luminous but comets are to some extent self-luminous. No doubt these facts are necessary consequences of the laws of nature and the general make-up of the world. But since we are not born knowing all the secrets of creation, we have to learn them by observation, that is, by the a posteriori method.

Nowadays, however, the name a priori is not usually restricted to truths obtained entirely without the help of observation. Knowledge may start out a posteriori, and yet, once it has been held for a long time and has become as certain as anything can be, it may serve as the ground of deductions. It may then be said to give a priori knowledge. For example, all scientists now believe that force (in today's terms, energy) cannot be created or destroyed by any of the processes of nature. If this is true, the force that disappears when a bullet hits a target must be turned into something else, and on a priori grounds we can say that the result will be heat. It's true that we could easily learn the same thing a posteriori, by picking up pieces of a bullet that has just hit a target and noticing that they are warm. But a priori knowledge has a great advantage: we can often apply it where experiment or observation would be difficult. If I lift a stone and drop it, the most delicate instruments could hardly show that the stone was heated by hitting the ground. Yet on a priori grounds I know that it must have been, and I can easily calculate how much heat was produced. In the same way we know, without the trouble of observing it, that Niagara Falls and every other waterfall produce heat. This is a fair example of a priori knowledge, because no one, as far as I have heard, has tested the fact or proved it a posteriori. All the same, the knowledge is founded in the first place on the experiments of Mr. Joule, who measured, in certain well-chosen cases, how much force is equivalent to a given amount of heat. The reader should take care, though, not to confuse the meaning of a priori explained here with the meaning given to the words by the philosophers who hold that the mind possesses knowledge independently of all observation.

It is not hard to see that the a priori method is equivalent to the synthetic method (see earlier in this lesson) considered in intension, and the a posteriori method, of course, is equivalent to the analytic method. But the same difference is really expressed by the words deductive and inductive, and we will often need to consider it in the following lessons.

For general remarks on method, see the Port Royal Logic, Part IV.

Questions and Exercises

1. What place did earlier writers on logic give to method, and what are the rules of method?

2. Explain the expressions nobis notiora and notiora naturæ.

3. What kind of method is the usual method of instruction?

4. Prove that analysis in extension is synthesis in intension, using some of the series of terms in question 6 of Lesson 5 as illustrations.

5. Explain the exact meanings of the expressions a priori knowledge and a posteriori knowledge.

6. To which kind does our knowledge of the following facts belong?

(1) The light of the stars takes a long time to reach us.

(2) Vaccination protects against smallpox.

(3) A meteor becomes heated as it passes through the air.

(4) There must be either some inhabitants or no inhabitants on Jupiter.

Induction

Lesson 25: Perfect Induction and the Inductive Syllogism

In the earlier lessons we have been looking at deductive reasoning. Deduction combines two or more propositions synthetically and arrives at a conclusion that is less general than the premises: a proposition or truth that applies to fewer individual instances than the separate premises it was inferred from. Take the general truth that "metals are good conductors of heat," and combine it with the truth that "aluminum is a metal." A syllogism in the mood Barbara then lets me infer that "aluminum is a good conductor of heat." This proposition is about one metal only, so it is plainly less general than the premise, which was about every metal there is. Induction goes the other way. In induction we move from less general facts, or even from individual facts, to more general propositions or truths, or, as we will often call them, Laws of Nature. Once we know that Mercury moves around the sun in an elliptical orbit, and that Venus, the Earth, Mars, Jupiter and the rest do too, we can arrive at the simple, general truth that "all the planets move in elliptical orbits around the sun." That is an example of inductive reasoning.

It is true that we can also reason without making our conclusion either more or less general than the premises. For example:

	Snowdon is the highest mountain in England or Wales.
	Snowdon is not so high as Ben Nevis.
	Therefore the highest mountain in England or Wales is not so high as Ben Nevis.

Or again:

	Lithium is the lightest metal known.
	Lithium is the metal indicated by one bright red line in the spectrum.
	Therefore the lightest metal known is the metal indicated by a spectrum of one bright red line.

Footnote: Roscoe's Lessons in Elementary Chemistry.

In these examples every proposition is a singular proposition. Each one simply asserts that two singular terms are identical, so the generality never changes. Each conclusion applies to exactly the same sort of object as each premise does. This kind of reasoning has been given the apt name of traduction.

Induction is a much harder and much more important kind of reasoning than traduction, or even deduction. Its work is to discover the general laws or uniformities, the relations of cause and effect, in short all the general truths we can assert about the countless and very varied events that happen in the natural world around us. Most of our knowledge, and perhaps all of it, as some philosophers think, comes in the end from inductive reasoning. The mind, people plausibly say, does not come supplied with knowledge already made up into general propositions and stamped on it. Instead it is given powers of observation, comparison and reasoning. When these powers are well trained and exercised, they are enough to get us knowledge of the world outside us and of the world inside the human mind. Even when we argue synthetically and deductively from simple ideas and truths that seem to be ready-made in the mind, as we do in geometry, we may in fact have gathered those simple ideas and truths earlier, by observation or by a kind of induction we were hardly aware of. This is a disputed point, and I will not take a firm position on it here. But if it is true, then induction is the way all the materials of knowledge are brought into the mind and analyzed. Deduction will then be the almost equally important process by which we put that knowledge to use, and by which, as we will see, new and more complicated inductions become possible.

An induction, that is, one act of inductive reasoning, is called perfect when all the possible cases or instances the conclusion can refer to have been examined and listed in the premises. Usually it is impossible to examine every case, since some may occur in the future, or in distant parts of the earth, or in other regions of the universe. Then the induction is called imperfect. The statement that every month of the year is shorter than thirty-two days comes from perfect induction, and it is a certain conclusion. The calendar is a human institution, so we know beyond doubt how many months there are, and we can easily check that each of them is shorter than thirty-two days. But the statement that all the planets move around the sun in one direction, from west to east, comes from imperfect induction. There may be planets farther out than Neptune, the most distant planet known in the 1870s, and the statement would of course apply to such a planet too.

So there is obviously a great difference between perfect and imperfect induction. Imperfect induction includes some process that lets us make statements about things we have never seen or examined, or even known to exist. But we must also be careful to remember that no imperfect induction can give a certain conclusion. It may be highly probable, or nearly certain, that the unexamined cases will be like the ones we have examined, but it can never be certain. It is quite possible, for instance, that a newly found planet might go around the sun in the opposite direction from the others. Among the satellites of the planets, more than one exception of this kind has already been discovered, and science has made mistakes again and again by expecting every new case to be exactly like the old ones. Imperfect induction, then, gives only a certain degree of probability, or likelihood, that all the instances will agree with the ones examined. Perfect induction, on the other hand, gives a necessary and certain conclusion, but it asserts nothing beyond what the premises already asserted.

Mr. J. S. Mill, in fact, disagrees with almost every other logician here. He holds that perfect induction is wrongly called induction at all, because it does not lead to any new knowledge. He defines induction as inference from the known to the unknown, and he counts the unexamined cases that seem to be brought into our knowledge as the only thing the reasoning gains. So perfect induction seems to him to have no scientific value whatever, because its conclusion merely says the premises over again in a shorter form, a mere summing up. I would point out, though, that even if perfect induction were nothing more than a way of abbreviating, it would still be very important, and we need it constantly in science and in everyday life. Without it we could never make a comprehensive statement at all. We would have to list every particular case. Suppose we examined the books in a library and found that they were all English books. Without perfect induction we could not sum up our results in the single proposition "all the books in this library are English books." We would have to go through the list of books every time we wanted to tell anyone what the library held. The truth is that the power to express a great number of particular facts in a very small space is essential to the progress of science. The whole art of arithmetic is nothing but a set of processes for shortening addition and subtraction, so that we can handle a great number of units in a very short time. In just the same way, perfect induction is absolutely necessary if we are to handle a great number of particular facts in a very small space.

Perfect induction is usually set out in the form of an inductive syllogism, as in this example:

	Mercury, Venus, the Earth, and so on, all move around the sun from west to east.
	Mercury, Venus, the Earth, and so on, are all the known planets.
	Therefore all the known planets move around the sun from west to east.

This argument is a true perfect induction. The conclusion makes a statement only about all the known planets, which shuts out any reference to planets that might be discovered later, and we may suppose that all the known planets have been listed in the premises. The argument looks like a syllogism in the third figure, namely Darapti, with the group of known planets as the middle term. In reality, though, it is not an ordinary syllogism. The minor premise does not say that Mercury, Venus, the Earth, Neptune and so on are contained among the known planets. It says that they are those planets, that they are identical with them. So this premise is a doubly universal proposition, of a kind (see Lesson 22) that the Aristotelian syllogism does not recognize. That is why the conclusion here is a universal proposition, something not allowed in the third figure of the syllogism.

As another example of perfect induction, we can take this:

	January, February, ... December, each contain less than 32 days.
	January ... December are all the months of the year.
	Therefore all the months of the year contain less than 32 days.

Sir William Hamilton rejected the idea completely, but it seems worth asking whether the inductive syllogism is not really a disjunctive syllogism. I would be inclined to set out the last example in this form:

A month of the year is either January, or February, or March, ... or December. But January has less than 32 days; and February has less than 32 days; and so on until we come to December, which has less than 32 days.

It clearly follows that a month, whichever it is, must have less than 32 days. There are only twelve possible cases, and in each one this is asserted. The fact is that the major premise of the syllogism above is a compound sentence with twelve subjects, so it is equivalent to twelve separate logical propositions. The minor premise is either a disjunctive proposition, as I have set it out, or something quite different from anything we have met elsewhere.

From perfect induction we will have to go on to imperfect induction. But logicians do not agree about what entitles us to take only some of the instances and conclude that what is true of those is true of all. Suppose we take the example found in many books and say:

	This, that, and the other magnet attract iron;
	This, that, and the other magnet are all magnets;
	Therefore all magnets attract iron.

Here we are obviously using a false minor premise, because this, that and the other magnet that we examined cannot possibly be all the magnets in existence. However we put it, there has to be an assumption that the magnets we examined are a fair sample of all magnets, so that what we find in some we may expect in all. Archbishop Whately thinks this assumption ought to be stated in one of the premises, and he sets out induction as a syllogism in Barbara, like this:

	That which belongs to this, that, and the other magnet, belongs to all;
	Attracting iron belongs to this, that, and the other;
	Therefore it belongs to all.

This is no doubt a correct statement of the assumption made in an imperfect induction. But it does not explain in the least what entitles us to make the assumption, or in what circumstances such an assumption is likely to turn out true. Some writers have claimed that there is a principle called the Uniformity of Nature, which lets us affirm that what has often been found true of anything will go on being found true of the same sort of thing. We must notice, though, that if there is such a principle, it has exceptions. Many facts that held true up to a certain point have later turned out not to be always true. For example, there was a wide and unbroken induction that seemed to show that all the satellites in the planetary system went around their planets in one and the same direction. Yet when the satellites of Uranus were discovered, they were found to move in a retrograde direction, that is, in the opposite direction from every satellite known before. The satellite of Neptune, discovered more recently (the only one known in the 1870s), has the same peculiarity.

We can leave to the next lesson the question of how the degree of certainty that induction gives varies from one branch of knowledge to another.

The advanced student will find it useful to consult the following: Mansel's Aldrich, Appendix, Notes G and H. Hamilton's Lectures on Logic, Lecture XVII, and Appendix VII, On Induction and Example, Vol. II, p. 358. J. S. Mill's System of Logic, Book III, Chap. 2, Of Inductions improperly so-called.

Questions and Exercises

1. Define deduction, induction and traduction, and distinguish them from one another.

2. Find an example of reasoning by traduction.

3. Distinguish perfect and imperfect induction.

4. How does Mr. Mill define induction, and what does he think of perfect induction?

5. What is the use of perfect induction?

6. Construct some examples of the inductive syllogism, and show that they can be put into a disjunctive form.

Lesson 26: Geometrical and Mathematical Induction, Analogy and Example

We now have to look very carefully at what Imperfect Induction rests on. Perfect Induction gives no trouble, because every case that can fall under the general conclusion is listed in the premises. In fact the conclusion holds no information that the premises did not already give. In this respect the Inductive Syllogism fits perfectly with the general principles of deductive reasoning. Those principles require that the information in the conclusion be drawn only from the data: we merely unfold what the premises contain implicitly, and turn it into an explicit statement.

In Imperfect Induction the process seems to be of a completely different kind, because the cases we gain knowledge about may be infinitely more numerous than the cases we gain it from. Let us first consider geometrical reasoning, which closely resembles inductive reasoning. In the fifth proposition of the first book of Euclid we prove that the angles at the base of an isosceles triangle are equal to each other. We do it by taking one particular triangle as an example. We are given a figure, asked to regard it as having two equal sides, and shown conclusively that if the sides really are equal, the angles opposite them must be equal too. But Euclid says nothing about any other isosceles triangle. He treats one single triangle as a sufficient specimen of all isosceles triangles, and we are asked to believe that whatever is true of it is true of any other, whether its sides are so small that only a microscope could show them, or so large that they reach the farthest fixed star. Clearly there can be an infinite number of isosceles triangles just in the length of the equal sides, and each of these can be varied infinitely again by widening or narrowing the angle between those sides. So the number of possible isosceles triangles is infinitely infinite, and yet we are asked to believe of this unimaginable number of objects what we have proved of only one single specimen. This might look like the most extreme Imperfect Induction possible, and yet everyone agrees that it gives us truly certain knowledge. We know, with as much certainty as any knowledge can have, that if we imagine lines drawn from the earth to two stars at equal distances from it, those lines will make equal angles with the line joining the two stars. And we can never have tried the experiment.

The generality of this geometrical reasoning plainly depends on how certainly we know that all isosceles triangles resemble each other exactly. The proposition we proved does not in fact apply to a triangle unless that triangle agrees with our specimen in every quality the proof depends on. The actual length of any side, and the actual size of the angle between any two sides, were not things the proof depended on. They were purely accidental circumstances. So we are perfectly free to apply to every new case of an isosceles triangle what we learn from one case. The whole vast body of certain knowledge in the mathematical sciences rests on similar ground: not only all the truths of geometry, but all the general truths of algebra. We showed in Lesson 7, for instance, that if a and b are two quantities and we multiply their sum by their difference, we get the difference of the squares of a and b. However often we try this, we will find it true. If a = 10 and b = 7, the product of the sum and difference is 17 × 3 = 51; the squares of the quantities are 10 × 10, or 100, and 7 × 7, or 49, and their difference is also 51. But however often we tried the rule, we would not add any certainty to it. When it was proved algebraically, there was no condition limiting the result to particular numbers, so a and b could be any numbers whatever. This generality of algebraic reasoning, which proves a property of infinitely many numbers at once, is one of the chief advantages of algebra over arithmetic. Algebra also has a process called Mathematical Induction, or Demonstrative Induction, which shows off the power of reasoning in a very striking way. Here is a good example. Take the first two odd numbers in a row, 1 and 3, and add them: the sum is 4, exactly two times two. Take three of them, 1 + 3 + 5, and the sum is 9, exactly three times three. Take four, 1 + 3 + 5 + 7, and the sum is 16, exactly four times four. In general, if we take any number of terms of the series 1 + 3 + 5 + 7 + ..., the sum equals the number of terms multiplied by itself. Anyone who knows a very little algebra can prove that this remarkable law is true universally, like this. Let n be the number of terms, and suppose for a moment that the law holds up to n terms, so that—

1+3+5+7+…+(2n−1)=n2. Now add 2n+1, the next odd number in the series, to each side of the equation. It follows that—

1+3+5+7+…+(2n−1)+(2n+1)=n2+2n+1. But the last quantity n2+2n+1 is exactly equal to (n+1)2. So if the law is true for n terms, it is also true for n + 1 terms. This lets us argue from each single case of the law to the next one. And we have already shown that it is true for the first few cases, so it must be true for all of them. No amount of labor could let a person find out by trial what the first billion odd numbers add up to (a billion here meaning, as the British counted it, a million million). Yet by symbols, that is, by general reasoning, we know for certain that they would add up to a billion billion, not one more or one less. This process of Mathematical Induction is not quite the same as Geometrical Induction, because each case depends on the one before it. But the proof rests on an equally narrow basis of experience, and it produces knowledge that is just as certain and just as general.

Mathematical truths like these depend on observing a few cases, but they get their certainty from our perception that one case is exactly similar to another, so that we believe without any doubt that what is true of one case is true of another. It is very instructive to set beside these cases some others where we have the same kind of ground in observation, but not the same tie of similarity. It was once believed that if you took any whole number, multiplied it by itself, added the number itself, and then added 41, the result would be a prime number, that is, a number that cannot be divided by any whole number except itself and one. In symbols, x2+x+41=prime number.

This was believed purely on the strength of trial and experience, and it certainly holds for a great many values of x. When x is given in turn each of the numbers in the first line below, the expression x2+x+41 gives the values in the second line, and they are all prime numbers:

012345678910
4143475361718397113131151

But no one could give any reason why it should always be true, and in fact the rule does not always hold. It fails when x = 40. Then we get 40 × 40 + 40 + 41 = 1681, and this is plainly equal to 41 × 40 + 41, or 41 × 41, which is not a prime number.

In the branch of mathematics that deals with the special properties and kinds of numbers, other propositions resting only on observation have been claimed to be always true. Fermat, for example, believed that 22x+1 always gives a prime number, but he could give no reason for the claim. It does in fact hold until the result reaches the large number 4294967297, which was found to be divisible by 641, and that disproved the general statement.

So we find that in some cases a single instance proves a general and certain rule, while in others a very great number of instances is not enough to give any certainty at all. Everything depends on our perception of similarity, or identity, between one case and another. We can see no similarity among all prime numbers that assures us that because one is given by a certain formula, another will be too. But we do find that kind of similarity among sums of odd numbers, and among isosceles triangles.

Exactly the same considerations apply to inductions in physical science. A chemist analyzes a few grains of water and finds that every 9 parts of water contain exactly 8 parts of oxygen and 1 of hydrogen. He feels entitled to assert that the same is true of all pure water, wherever it came from and whatever part of the world it was taken from. But if he analyzes a piece of granite, or a sample of seawater from one part of the world, he has no confidence that it will exactly resemble a piece of granite or a sample of seawater from another part of the earth. So he does not venture to assert of all granite, or all seawater, what he finds true of one sample. Wider experience shows that granite varies a great deal in composition, but that seawater is kept fairly uniform by the constant mixing of currents. Only experience could tell us, in these cases, how far we can safely assert of one sample what we have found out about another. But we have reason to believe that chemical compounds are by nature fixed and unchanging in composition, according to Dalton's laws of combining proportions. No a priori reasoning from the principles of thought could have told us this. We learn it only through wide experiment. But once we have shown it to be true of certain substances, we don't need to repeat the trial with every other substance, because we have every reason to believe that this is a natural law in which all chemical substances resemble each other. A single accurate analysis of a given fixed compound is all we need, then, to tell us the composition of every other portion of the same substance.

We must be careful to notice, however, that all inductions in physical science are only probable, or, if they are certain, the certainty they have is only hypothetical. Can I be absolutely certain that all water contains one part of hydrogen in nine? I am certain only on two conditions:—

1. That this really was the composition of the sample I tested.

2. That any other substance I call water exactly resembles that sample. But even if the first condition is beyond doubt, I cannot be certain of the second. After all, how do I know what water is, except by its being a transparent liquid that freezes into a solid and evaporates into steam, that has a high specific heat, and that has a number of other distinct properties? And can I be absolutely certain that every liquid with all these properties is water? In practice I can be certain, but in theory I cannot. Two substances might have been created so like each other that we have never yet discovered the difference, and then we might constantly be misled by assuming of one what is true only of the other. That this should ever happen with substances that have qualities as distinctive as water's is extremely improbable. But in other cases it is so far from impossible, or even improbable, that it has often happened. Most of the new elements discovered in recent years were, without doubt, mistaken for other elements before. Caesium and Rubidium had long been mistaken for each other, and for Potassium, until Bunsen and Kirchhoff told them apart by means of the spectroscope. Since they are now known to be widely distributed, though in small quantities, it is certain that what was taken for Potassium in many thousands of analyses was partly made up of different substances.
