Selenium had probably been confused with Sulfur, and there are certain metals that have only recently been told apart: for instance Rhodium, Ruthenium, Iridium, Osmium, and Beryllium; Yttrium, Erbium, Cerium, Lanthanum, and Didymium (which was itself later found to be a mixture of two elements); and Cadmium and Indium. The progress of science will no doubt show that we are mistaken in many of our identifications, and the various difficulties that arise from them will eventually be explained.

Now take a very different case of induction. Are we certain that the sun will rise again tomorrow morning, as it has risen for many thousands of years, and probably for some hundred million years? We are certain only on one condition, or hypothesis: that the planetary system goes on tomorrow as it has gone on for so long. Many causes might exist that could upset all our calculations at any moment. Our sun is believed to be a variable star, and for all we know it might at any moment suddenly explode or flare up, as certain other stars have been seen to do, and then we would all be turned into thin glowing vapor in an instant. It is not at all impossible that a collision once happened in the planetary system, and that the tiny planets, or asteroids, are the result. Even if there is no large meteor, comet or other body able to break up the earth by collision, the sun is probably moving through space at nearly 300 miles a minute, and if some other star should come toward us at a similar speed, the consequences would be terrible beyond imagining. It is very unlikely, however, that such an event would happen even in the course of a million years.

The reader will now see that no mere Imperfect Induction can give certain knowledge. All inference goes forward on the assumption that new instances will exactly resemble old ones in every material circumstance. But with natural phenomena this is purely hypothetical, and we may constantly find ourselves wrong. In Mathematical Induction the certainty came from the cases being hypothetical by their very nature, or being constructed so as to match the conditions exactly. We cannot assert that any triangle existing in nature has two equal sides or two equal angles; in practice it is even impossible for any two lines or angles to be absolutely equal. But it is true all the same that if the sides are equal, the angles are equal. So all certainty of inference is relative and hypothetical. Even in the syllogism, the certainty of the conclusion rests only on the hypothesis that the premises are certain. In fact, it is probable that all reasoning comes down to a single type: what is true of one thing will be true of another thing, on condition that the two exactly resemble each other in every material circumstance.

The reader will now easily understand the nature of reasoning by analogy. Strictly speaking, an analogy is not an identity of one thing with another, but an identity of relations. Take numbers: 7 is not identical with 10, nor 14 with 20, but the ratio of 7 to 10 is identical with the ratio of 14 to 20, so there is an analogy between these numbers. Multiplying two by two is not the same thing as building a square on a line two units long. But there is this analogy between them: the square will contain just as many units of area as there are units in the product of two times two. This analogy is so obvious that we confidently assert that a square mile contains 1760 × 1760 square yards, without testing it at all. In ordinary language, though, analogy has come to mean any resemblance between things that lets us believe of one what we know of the other.

The planet Mars, for example, has an atmosphere with clouds and mists much like our own. It has seas, which can be told from the land by their greenish color, and polar regions covered with snow. (So astronomers of the 1870s read what their telescopes showed them.) The red color of the planet seems to come from its atmosphere, like the red color of our sunrises and sunsets. So much about the surface of Mars is like the surface of the Earth that we readily argue there must be inhabitants there, as there are here. But all we can say for certain is that if the circumstances really are similar, and similar seeds of life have been created there as here, there must be inhabitants. The fact that many circumstances are similar increases the probability. Between the Earth and the Sun, however, the analogy is far fainter. We do speak of the sun's atmosphere as subject to storms and full of clouds, but those clouds are probably heated beyond the temperature of our hottest furnaces. If they produce rain, it must be like a shower of melted iron. And sunspots are disturbances of such tremendous size and nature that the earth, along with half a dozen of the other planets, could easily be swallowed up in one of them. So there is plainly little or no analogy between the Sun and the Earth, and that is why we find it hard to form any idea of what goes on in a sun or star.

Footnote: Lockyer's Elementary Lessons in Astronomy, § 108.

Argument from analogy can be defined as direct inductive inference from one instance to any similar instance. As Mr. J. S. Mill says, it can be reduced to the following formula:—

"Two things resemble each other in one or more respects; a certain proposition is true of the one; therefore it is true of the other." This is no doubt the type of all reasoning, and the certainty of the process depends entirely on how far the cases resemble, or are identical with, each other. In geometry the cases are, by hypothesis, absolutely identical in every material point, and no doubt attaches to the inference. In physical science the identity is a matter of probability, and the conclusion is probable to the same degree. I should add that Mr. Mill holds that Geometrical and Mathematical Induction ought not to be called Induction at all, for reasons whose force entirely escapes me; but the reader will find his views in the 2nd chapter of the 3rd book of his System of Logic.

One form of analogical, or inductive, argument is the constant use of examples and instances. The best way to describe the nature of a class of things is to present one of the things itself, and to point out which of its properties belong to the class, as distinct from those peculiar to that one thing. Throughout these Lessons, as in every book on logic, instances of propositions, of compound or complex sentences, of syllogisms and so on are used again and again, and the reader is asked to apply to all similar cases what he observes in the examples given. It is assumed that the writer chooses examples that truly display the properties in question.

All inductive and analogical inferences rest on the same principles, but their sources of probability differ widely. In analogy we have two cases that resemble each other in a great many properties, and we infer that some further property found in one will probably be found in the other. The very narrow basis of experience is made up for by the high degree of similarity. In the processes more usually treated under the name of Induction, the things generally resemble each other in only two or three properties, and we need more instances to assure us that what is true of these is probably true of all similar instances. In short, the smaller the intension of the resemblance, the greater the extension of our inquiries must be.

In the following Lessons we go on to the ordinary processes of Induction.

Mr. Mill's System of Logic, Book III. Chap. XX. Of Analogy. Mansel's Aldrich, App. Note H. On Example and Analogy.

Questions and Exercises

1. What gives reasoning in geometry its certainty and generality?

2. Find other instances of certain and general reasoning about the properties of numbers.

3. Why are inductive conclusions about prime numbers uncertain and not general?

4. Why is a single instance sometimes enough to justify a universal conclusion, while in other cases the greatest possible number of agreeing instances, without a single exception, is not enough to justify one?

5. What are the strict and the ordinary meanings of the word analogy?

6. Explain the use of examples.

7. Explain exactly the difference between analogical argument and ordinary induction.

Lesson 27: Observation and Experiment

It is safe to say that all knowledge must ultimately rest on experience. "Experience" is simply a general name for all the different feelings and impressions the mind receives at any time in its existence. The mind never creates completely new knowledge independent of experience. All our reasoning powers can do is work out the full meaning of the facts we already have. In earlier centuries, people of the very highest ability believed that the mind, by its own power alone and by thinking hard enough, could discover what the things outside us ought to be, and would turn out to be when examined. They thought we could anticipate Nature, working out from the human mind an idea of what the Creator would have made. The famous philosopher Descartes, for example, held that whatever the mind can clearly conceive may be taken as true. But we can conceive of mountains of gold or oceans of fresh water, and in fact they do not exist. Anything we can clearly conceive must agree with the laws of thought, and so, as far as our intellect is concerned, its existence is not impossible. But the shapes and sizes and ways in which it has pleased the Creator to make things, in this or any other part of the universe, cannot possibly be anticipated by the very limited wisdom of the human mind. They can only be learned by actually examining the things that exist.

In the second half of the 13th century the great Roger Bacon taught clearly, in England, that experience is supremely important as the basis of knowledge. By a curious coincidence, the same doctrine was taken up again in the 17th century by the great Lord Chancellor Francis Bacon, and it has been called the Baconian Philosophy after him. I believe that Roger Bacon was an even greater man than Francis, though Francis is the better known. But the words in which Francis Bacon proclaimed the importance of experience and experiment deserve to be remembered forever. At the beginning of his great work, the Novum Organum, or New Instrument, he points out our proper position as learners in the world of Nature:

"Man, the Servant and Interpreter of Nature, can do and understand as much as he has observed concerning the order of nature in outward things or in the mind; beyond this he can neither know nor do."

That is the first of the aphorisms, or short numbered paragraphs, with which the Novum Organum begins. In the second aphorism he says that the mind on its own can do little and is liable to go wrong. It needs help, in the form of a definite logical method, and supplying that method was the purpose of his New Instrument. The 3rd and 4th aphorisms must be given in full. They are:

"Human science and human power coincide, because not knowing a cause deprives us of the effect. For Nature is not conquered except by obeying her; and what we discover as a cause when we contemplate becomes a rule when we act."

"Man can do nothing for himself except move natural bodies toward or away from each other; Nature working within does the rest."

It would be impossible to express more clearly and completely how we discover science by interpreting the changes we observe in Nature, and then turn that knowledge to useful purposes in the arts and manufacturing. We cannot create or destroy a single particle of matter. It is now known that we cannot even create or destroy force (in today's terms, energy). Nor can we really alter the inner nature of any substance we deal with. All we can do is observe carefully how one substance acts on another by its natural powers, and then, by bringing them together at the right time, accomplish what we want. As Bacon says, "Nature working within does the rest." If it had not been the nature of heat, applied to water, to produce steam with elastic power (the power to push back when compressed), the steam engine obviously could never have been made. The invention of the steam engine came from noticing how useful the force of steam was, and putting it to work accordingly. It is in this sense that the Roman poet Virgil called that person happy who knows the causes of things:

	Felix qui potuit rerum cognoscere causas,

and that Bacon said, "Knowledge is Power." To the extent that we have observed how things happen in Nature, and on what occasions particular effects come about, we can avoid those effects or make use of them as we wish. We do this not by altering the natures of things, but by letting them show their own proper powers at suitable times and in suitable circumstances. In this way, as Tennyson has put it so well, we

	"Rule by obeying Nature's Powers."

Inductive logic deals with the methods of reasoning by which we can successfully interpret Nature and learn the natural laws that different substances obey in different circumstances. In this lesson we consider the first requirement of induction: the experience, or examination of Nature, that supplies us with facts. We get this experience either by observation or by experiment. To observe is simply to notice events and changes produced in the ordinary course of Nature, without being able to control or vary them, or at least without trying to. The early astronomers, for example, observed the motions of the sun, moon and planets among the fixed stars, and gradually detected many of the laws, or regular returns, of those bodies. In the same way the meteorologist observes the ever-changing weather. He notes the height of the barometer, the temperature and humidity of the air, the direction and force of the wind, and the height and character of the clouds, without being able to control any of these things in the least. The geologist, again, is generally a simple observer when he investigates the nature and position of rocks. The zoologist, the botanist and the mineralogist usually rely on mere observation when they examine animals, plants and minerals in the natural condition in which they find them.

In experiment, by contrast, we vary the combinations of things and circumstances as we choose, and then observe the result. This is how the chemist discovers the composition of water: he uses an electric current to separate it into its two constituents, oxygen and hydrogen. The mineralogist may use experiment when he melts two or three substances together to find out how a particular mineral might have been formed. Even the botanist and the zoologist are not limited to passive observation. By moving animals or plants to different climates and different soils, and by what is called domestication, they can test how far the natural forms and species can be altered.

Clearly, where experiment can be used at all, it is the most powerful and direct way of getting facts. We might have to wait years or centuries to come across, by accident, facts that we can easily produce at any moment in a laboratory. Most of the chemical substances now known, and many extremely useful products, would probably never have been discovered at all if we had waited for Nature to present them to us of her own accord. Many forces and changes, too, may be going on in Nature all the time, but so faintly that they escape our senses, and we need some experimental means to detect them. Electricity is no doubt at work in every particle of matter, perhaps at every moment. Even the ancients could not help noticing its action in the lodestone (a natural magnet), in lightning, in the Aurora Borealis (the northern lights), and in a piece of rubbed amber (in Greek, electrum, from which "electricity" takes its name). But in lightning, electricity was too intense and dangerous to study; in the other cases it was too weak to be properly understood. The science of electricity and magnetism could only advance once we had regular supplies of electricity from the ordinary electric machine (a friction generator) or the galvanic battery, and could make powerful electromagnets. Most, if not all, of the effects electricity produces must go on in Nature too, but far too faintly to be observed.

Experiment is also indispensable for another reason. On the surface of the earth we usually meet substances under certain uniform conditions, so observation alone could never teach us what those substances would be like under other conditions. Carbonic acid (carbon dioxide), for example, is met with only as a gas, given off when carbon burns. But when it is exposed to extreme pressure and cold, it condenses into a liquid, and can even be turned into a solid, like snow. Many other gases have been liquefied or solidified in the same way, and there is reason to believe that every substance can take all three forms, solid, liquid and gas, if only the conditions of temperature and pressure can be varied enough. Mere observation of Nature would have led us to the opposite belief: that nearly all substances were fixed in a single state, and could not be turned from solid into liquid, or from liquid into gas.

We must not suppose, however, that we can draw a precise line between observation and experiment and say where one ends and the other begins. The difference is one of degree rather than of kind. All we can say is that the more we vary the conditions artificially, the more we are using experiment. I have said that meteorology is a science of almost pure observation. But if we deliberately climb mountains to observe how the air becomes thinner and colder with height, or go up in balloons for the same purpose, as Gay-Lussac and Glaisher did, we vary our way of observing so much that it becomes almost experimental. Astronomers, again, can almost be said to experiment rather than merely observe when they set up instruments at the same moment as far north and as far south on the earth's surface as possible, to observe how far the position of Venus appears to shift as it crosses the face of the sun in a transit. In this way they can compare the distances of Venus and the sun with the size of the earth.

Sir John Herschel has described this difference excellently in his Discourse on the Study of Natural Philosophy. "Essentially they are much alike, and differ rather in degree than in kind; so that perhaps the terms passive and active observation might better express their distinction; but it is, nevertheless, highly important to mark the different states of mind in inquiries carried on by their respective aids, as well as their different effects in promoting the progress of science. In the former, we sit still and listen to a tale, told us, perhaps obscurely, piecemeal, and at long intervals of time, with our attention more or less awake. It is only by after rumination that we gather its full import; and often, when the opportunity is gone by, we have to regret that our attention was not more particularly directed to some point which, at the time, appeared of little moment, but of which we at length appreciate the importance. In the latter, on the other hand, we cross-examine our witness, and by comparing one part of his evidence with the other, while he is yet before us, and reasoning upon it in his presence, are enabled to put pointed and searching questions, the answer to which may at once enable us to make up our minds. Accordingly it has been found invariably, that in those departments of physics where the phenomena are beyond our control, or into which experimental enquiry, from other causes, has not been carried, the progress of knowledge has been slow, uncertain and irregular; while in such as admit of experiment, and in which mankind have agreed to its adoption, it has been rapid, sure, and steady."

Footnote: Herschel's Discourse, p. 77.

Often, though, Nature has, so to speak, made experiments on a scale and over a length of time that we cannot possibly match. We do not need to test which soil and situation suit a given plant best, for example. We only have to look around and notice the habitat, or place, where it grows naturally in the most flourishing condition, and we can be sure that this shows the result of ages of natural experiment. We would probably never have known the distances of the fixed stars if the earth, by traveling in an orbit 182,000,000 miles across, did not give us a kind of experimental base line for observing them. Because of it we see the stars in very slightly different positions, and so can judge their distances compared with the size of the earth's orbit. Eclipses, transits, occultations (when the moon or a planet passes in front of a star) and remarkable conjunctions of the planets are also kinds of natural experiment. They were often recorded in early times, and so give us data of the greatest value.

Footnote: See Lockyer's Elementary Lessons in Astronomy, Nos. XLVI, XLVII.

Logic can do little or nothing to make someone a sharp or accurate observer. No definite rules can be laid down on the subject. Observing well is an art that can only be gained by practice and training, and one of the greatest advantages of studying the Natural Sciences is that it develops the ability to observe clearly and steadily. Logic can, however, give us one caution, which Mr. J. S. Mill has pointed out well: we must distinguish accurately between what we really observe and what we only infer from the facts we observe. As long as we only record and describe what our senses have actually witnessed, we cannot make a mistake. The moment we assume or infer anything, we can go wrong. For example, suppose we examine the sun's surface through a telescope. We observe that it is intensely bright except where there are small breaks, or round openings, in the surface with a dark interior. We are irresistibly led to conclude that the inside of the sun is colder and darker than the outside, and we record as a fact that we saw the dark interior of the sun through certain openings in its luminous atmosphere. But that record would contain a mistaken inference. We saw nothing but dark spots, and as observers we should have done no more than record the shape, size, appearance and changes of those spots. Whether they are dark clouds above the luminous surface, or glimpses of the dark interior, or, as is now (in the 1870s) almost certainly inferred, something entirely different from either, can only be proved by comparing many unprejudiced observations.

The reader cannot keep in mind too often this caution against confusing the facts observed with the inferences drawn from them. It is not too much to say that nine-tenths of what we seem to see and hear is inferred, not actually felt. Every sense has what are called acquired perceptions: the power, built up by long experience, of judging unconsciously many things that cannot be perceived directly. The eye cannot see distance. Yet we constantly imagine, and say, that we see things at such and such a distance, without realizing that this is the result of judgment. As Mill remarks, it is saying too much to say "I saw my brother." All I positively know is that I saw someone who closely resembled my brother, as far as I could observe. It is only by judgment that I can say he was my brother, and that judgment might be wrong.

Nothing is more important in observation and experiment than not letting any prejudice or theory influence us, so that we record the facts observed correctly and give them their proper weight. Someone who fails to do this will almost always be able to find facts that support an opinion, however mistaken. For example, most uneducated people still believe firmly that the moon has a great influence on the weather. The changes of the moon, full, new and half moon, happen four times every month, and it is supposed that any change can influence the weather at least on the day before or the day after it. So there will be twelve days out of every 28 on which any change in the weather would be put down to the moon, and over a year many changes will probably be recorded in this way as supporting the opinion. The uneducated observer is struck by these cases and remembers them carefully. But he fails to notice, or at least to remember, that changes of weather also often happen when there is no change of the moon at all. The question could only be settled by a long course of careful and unbiased observation, in which all the facts, favorable and unfavorable, were recorded equally. All the observations that have been published go against the idea that the moon has any such influence as the popular mind gives it.

At the same time, it would be a mistake to think that the best observer or experimenter is the one who holds no previous opinions or theories about the subject he is investigating. On the contrary, the great experimenter is the one who always has a theory, or even a whole crowd of theories or ideas, in his mind, but is always putting them to the test of experience and throwing out the ones that are false. The number of things that could be observed and experimented on is infinite. If we simply set to work recording facts without any definite purpose, our records will be worthless. We need some opinion or theory to guide our choice of experiments, and we are more likely to hit on the truth that way than by working at random. But the great requirement of the true scientist is that he be completely unbiased, and give up any opinion as soon as facts are observed that are inconsistent with it.

The famous Turgot (the French economist and statesman) put it well: "the first thing is to invent a system; the second thing is to be disgusted with it." That is, we ought to have some idea of the truth we are looking for, but we should immediately put it to a severe trial, as if we were inclined to distrust and dislike it, rather than be biased in its favor. Probably few people have entertained more false theories than Kepler and Faraday; few have discovered or established truths of greater certainty and importance. Faraday himself has said:

"The world little knows how many of the thoughts and theories which have passed through the mind of a scientific investigator, have been crushed in silence and secrecy by his own severe criticism and adverse examination; that in the most successful instances not a tenth of the suggestions, the hopes, the wishes, the preliminary conclusions have been realized."

Footnote: Modern Culture edited by Youmans, p. 222. [Macmillan and Co.]

The student is strongly advised to read Sir John Herschel's Preliminary Discourse on the Study of Natural Philosophy (Lardner's Cabinet Cyclopædia), especially Part II, Chapters 4 to 7, on Observation, Experiment, and the Inductive Processes generally.

Questions and Exercises

1. What is the false method of science that Bacon protested against?

2. Explain exactly what Bacon meant when he said that man is the Servant and Interpreter of Nature, and that Knowledge is Power.

3. How does experiment differ from observation?

4. Classify the sciences according to whether they use passive observation, experiment, or both.

5. Name the chief ways in which experiment is better than mere observation.

6. What is the chief precaution needed in observation?

7. Explain how it is possible to anticipate Nature and yet base all our conclusions on the results of experience.

Lesson 28: Methods of Induction

We now have to look at the methods that can be laid down to guide us when we search for general truths, or laws of nature, among the facts we have gathered by observation and experiment. Induction means inferring from particulars to generals: spotting a general truth among the particular cases in which it shows up. In physical science, though, the truths to be discovered are usually about the connection of cause and effect, and we generally call them laws of causation or natural laws. By the Cause of an event we mean the circumstances that must have come before it in order for the event to happen. And it is usually impossible to say that an event has one single cause and no more. Producing an effect normally takes many different things, conditions or circumstances, and all of them must be counted as causes, or as necessary parts of the cause. Take the loud bang of a gun going off. Its cause is not simply the pulling of the trigger, which is only the last visible cause, or the occasion, of the explosion. The qualities of the powder, the right shape of the barrel, a charge in the barrel to resist the blast, the proper arrangement of the percussion cap and the powder, and the air surrounding the gun are all among the circumstances needed for that loud report. If any one of them had been missing, it would not have happened.

Again, the cause of water boiling is not just heating it up to a certain temperature. The vapor must also be able to escape once it reaches a certain pressure. In the same way, the freezing of water does not depend merely on drawing off heat until the temperature falls below 0° Centigrade (Celsius). So the work of Induction is to detect the circumstances that will always produce a given effect. As soon as we know them, we have a law, or uniformity of nature, of greater or less generality.

In this lesson and the ones that follow I will often need, besides cause and effect, the words antecedent and consequent, and the reader should notice what they mean. An antecedent is any thing, condition or circumstance that exists before an event or phenomenon, or possibly at the same time as it. A consequent is any thing, circumstance, event or phenomenon that is different from all the antecedents and follows after they have come together. An antecedent is not necessarily a cause, because the effect might have happened without it. The sunlight, for example, may be an antecedent of a house burning down, but it is not the cause, because the house would burn just as well at night. A necessary, or indispensable, antecedent, however, is the same thing as a cause: it is that without which the effect would not happen.

I will also often use the word phenomenon. It simply means anything that appears, and so is observed by the senses. The word comes from the Greek φαινόμενον (phainomenon), "that which appears," and this origin matches its use in logic exactly.

The first method of Induction is the one Mr. J. S. Mill has aptly called the Method of Agreement. It rests on this rule: "If two or more instances of the phenomenon under investigation have only one circumstance in common, the circumstance in which alone all the instances agree, is the cause (or effect) of the given phenomenon." I think the meaning of this First Canon of inductive inquiry could be put more briefly: the sole invariable antecedent of a phenomenon is probably its cause.

To apply this method, we collect as many instances of the phenomenon as we can and compare their antecedents. The causes will be somewhere among them. But if we notice that certain antecedents are sometimes present and sometimes absent without seeming to affect the result, we conclude that they cannot be necessary antecedents. So it is the one antecedent, or group of antecedents, that is always present when the effect follows that we take to be the cause. Here is an example. Bright rainbow colors appear on bubbles, on films of tar floating on water, on thin sheets of mica, on cracks in glass, and between two pieces of glass pressed together. When we examine all these cases, they seem to agree in nothing except the presence of a very thin layer or plate, and it seems to make no noticeable difference what kind of matter the plate is made of, whether solid, liquid or gas. So we conclude that these colors are caused simply by the thinness of the plates, and the theory of the interference of light proves this conclusion true. Sir David Brewster gave a beautiful proof, along the same lines, that the colors seen on mother-of-pearl are caused not by the nature of the substance but by the shape of its surface. He took impressions of mother-of-pearl in wax and found that, although the substance was now entirely different, the colors were exactly the same. It was later found that a metal plate whose surface was marked with very fine, close-set grooves would show iridescent colors like those of mother-of-pearl. So it is clear that the shape of the surface, the only invariable antecedent or condition needed to produce the colors, must be their cause.

The Method of Agreement runs into a serious difficulty, which Mill calls the Plurality of Causes: the same effect may be due to different causes in different instances. If we look carefully into the cause of heat, for example, we find it is produced by friction, by burning or combustion, by electricity, by pressure and so on. So even if one and the same thing happened to be present in every case we examined, it would not follow that this thing was the cause. The second method of induction, which we will look at now, is free from this difficulty. It is known as the Method of Difference, and Mill's Second Canon states it like this:

"If an instance in which the phenomenon under investigation occurs, and an instance in which it does not occur, have every circumstance in common save one, that one occurring only in the former; the circumstance in which alone the two instances differ, is the effect, or the cause, or an indispensable part of the cause, of the phenomenon."

In other words: the antecedent that is always present when the phenomenon follows, and always absent when the phenomenon is absent, with the other circumstances staying the same, is the cause of the phenomenon in those circumstances.

This lets us prove clearly that friction is one cause of heat. When two sticks are rubbed together they get hot; when they are not rubbed, they do not. Sir Humphry Davy showed that even two pieces of ice rubbed together in a vacuum produce heat, as their melting shows, and so he completely demonstrated that the friction is the source and cause of the heat. We prove that air is what carries sound to our ears by striking a bell inside the receiver (the glass jar) of an air pump, as Hawksbee first did in 1705. When the receiver is full of air we hear the bell; when it holds little or no air we do not. We learn that sodium, or any of its compounds, produces a spectrum with a bright yellow double line by noticing two things: there is no such line in the spectrum of a light when no sodium is present, but if the smallest quantity of sodium is thrown into the flame or other source of light, the bright yellow line appears instantly. Oxygen is the cause of breathing and life, because if an animal is put into a jar of ordinary air from which the oxygen has been removed, it soon suffocates.

This is essentially the great method of experiment. How useful it is depends mainly on one precaution: varying only one circumstance at a time and keeping all the others exactly as they were. Thomson and Tait put this into one of the rules for conducting experiments in their great treatise on Natural Philosophy, Vol. I. p. 307:

"In all cases when a particular agent or cause is to be studied, experiments should be arranged in such a way as to lead if possible to results depending on it alone; or, if this cannot be done, they should be arranged so as to increase the effects due to the cause to be studied till these so far exceed the unavoidable concomitants, that the latter may be considered as only disturbing, not essentially modifying the effects of the principal agent."

Here is what an imperfect and unsatisfactory experiment would look like. Suppose we took air whose oxygen had been turned into carbonic acid (carbon dioxide) by burning carbon in it, and argued that, because an animal dies in that air, oxygen is the cause of breathing. Instead of simply removing the oxygen, we have brought in a new substance, carbonic acid, which is perfectly capable of killing the animal by its own poisonous properties. In fact the animal would suffocate even if a good deal of oxygen were left. So the presence of the carbonic acid is a disturbing circumstance that confuses the experiment and spoils it.

We can prove that the force of gravity exists, and even measure how strong it is, by delicately hanging up a small ball about the size of a marble and then suddenly bringing a very heavy lead ball, weighing a ton or more, close to it. The small ball will be attracted and set moving. But the experiment would be worthless unless it were done with the greatest care. Obviously, moving the big lead ball suddenly would disturb the air, shake the room, set up air currents by being colder or warmer than the air around it, and even cause electrical attractions or repulsions. All of these would probably disturb the small ball far more than the force of gravitation does.

As Sir John Herschel has pointed out, beautiful examples of experiment by this method can be found in the research by which Dr. Wells discovered the cause of dew. On a clear, calm night, stretch a sheet or some other covering a foot or two above the ground, so that it screens the ground beneath it from the open sky. Dew will form on the grass around the screen but not underneath it. Since the temperature, the moisture of the air and the other circumstances are exactly the same, the open sky must be an indispensable antecedent of dew. Nature in fact tries the same experiment for us. If we observe the dew on two nights that differ in nothing except that one is cloudless and the other cloudy, we will find that a clear open sky is needed for dew to form.

Often we cannot apply the Method of Difference perfectly, by varying only one circumstance at a time. For example, we generally cannot test the qualities of the same substance as a solid and as a liquid without any other change in the circumstances, because to melt it or freeze it we have to change its temperature. The temperature might then be the cause of what we put down to the substance being liquid or solid. In cases like this we have to fall back on what Mill calls the Joint Method of Agreement and Difference. It consists of applying the Method of Agreement twice: first to a number of instances where an effect is produced, and second to a number of quite different instances where the effect is not produced. It must be clearly understood, though, that the negative instances differ from the positive ones in several circumstances. If they differed in only one, we could use the simple Method of Difference. Iceland spar, for instance, has the curious power of making things seen through it look double. This phenomenon, called double refraction, belongs to many other crystals as well. We could prove at once that it is due to crystalline structure if we could get some transparent substance in both a crystallized and an uncrystallized state, with no other change at all. We can't, but we do have a fairly satisfactory proof. We observe that uniform, transparent, uncrystallized substances agree in not having double refraction, and that crystalline substances, on the other hand, agree in having that power, apart from certain exceptions that are easy to explain. The principle of the Joint Method can be stated in the following rule, which is Mill's Third Canon:

"If two or more instances in which the phenomenon occurs have only one circumstance in common, while two or more instances in which it does not occur have nothing in common save the absence of that circumstance; the circumstance in which alone the two sets of instances (always or invariably) differ, is the effect, or the cause, or an indispensable part of the cause, of the phenomenon."

I have put in the words in parentheses myself, because without them the canon seems to me to say exactly the opposite of what Mill means.

It may help in understanding these inductive methods exactly if I give a symbolic picture of them, in the way Mill does. Let A, B, C, D, E and so on be antecedents that can be combined in various ways, and let a, b, c, d, e and so on be the effects that follow from them. Suppose we can collect the following sets of antecedents and effects:

Antecedents.Consequents.
ABCabc
ADEade
AFGafg
AHKahk
............
............

Then we can apply the Method of Agreement, and little doubt will remain that A, the sole invariable antecedent, is the cause of a.

The Method of Difference is represented well enough by this:

Antecedents.Consequents.
ABCabc
BCbc

Here B and C stay completely unchanged, and we find that the presence or absence of A brings about the presence or absence of a. So A is the cause of a when B and C are present. But the reader should be warned against thinking that this proves A is the cause of a under every circumstance whatever.

The Joint Method of Agreement and Difference is represented in the same way:

Antecedents.Consequents.
ABCabc
ADEade
AFGafg
AHKahk
............
PQpq
RSrs
TVtv
XYxy
............

Here the presence of A is followed by a, as in the simple Method of Agreement, and the absence of A, in circumstances different from the earlier ones, is followed by the absence of a. So it is very highly probable that A is the cause of a. But it is easy to see that A is not the only circumstance in which the two sets of instances differ; if it were, we could apply the simple Method of Difference to any pair of them. What we can say is that the presence of A is a circumstance in which one set invariably, or uniformly, or always, differs from the other set. So the Joint Method stands in for the simpler Method of Difference in cases where that method cannot properly be put to work.

Herschel's Discourse, part II. chap. 6, p. 144.

Mill's System of Logic, book III. chaps. 8 and 9.

Lesson 29: Methods of Quantitative Induction

The methods of Induction described in the last Lesson dealt only with whether the event whose cause we were looking for happened or did not happen. That is how we learned that friction is one cause of heat. We saw that two solid bodies rubbed together, even two pieces of ice, produced heat, and that when they were not rubbed, no heat was produced. But this is a very elementary sort of experiment. As an investigation moves forward, we always need to measure the exact quantity of an effect, if it is the kind of thing that can be more or less, and to connect it with the quantity of the cause. In fact there is a natural course of progress that we follow in every inquiry of this kind, and it can be laid out as a series of questions:

1. Does the antecedent invariably produce an effect?

2. In what direction is that effect?

3. How much is that effect in proportion to the cause?

4. Is it uniformly in that proportion?

5. If not, according to what law does it vary?

Take, for instance, the way heat changes the size of bodies. The first question is whether heating a solid body, say a bar of iron, changes its length. The simple method of difference lets us answer that it does. The next inquiry shows that almost all substances get longer, or larger in size, when heated, but that a very few, such as India rubber, and water below 4.08° Centigrade (Celsius), get smaller. Next we work out the proportion of the change to each degree of temperature, which is called the coefficient of expansion. Iron, for example, expands by 0.0000122 of its own length for every 1° Centigrade between 0° and 100°.

Still more careful inquiry shows, however, that the expansion is not uniformly proportional to the temperature. Most metals expand faster and faster the hotter they get. But we don't need to go into the details of the subject here.

Or take the fixed stars. They have come up often in these Lessons, but the reader probably knows that they are not really fixed. For any particular star, the astronomer actually has to answer the five questions below.

Firstly. Does the star move?

2ndly. In what direction does it move?

3rdly. How much does it move in a year or a century?

4thly. Does it move uniformly?

5thly. If not, according to what law does the motion vary in direction and speed?

Every science, and every question in a science, starts out as a matter of fact only. Then it becomes a matter of quantity, and little by little it becomes more and more precisely quantitative. Thirty years ago most of the phenomena of electricity and electromagnetism were known only as facts. Now most of them can be exactly measured and calculated.

As soon as phenomena can be measured like this, we can use a further Method of Induction, and a very important one. It is really the Method of Difference again, but used under far more favorable conditions, where every degree and quantity of a phenomenon gives us a new experiment and a new proof of the connection between cause and effect. It may be called the Method of Concomitant Variations, and Mr. J. S. Mill states it like this, in what he calls the Fifth Canon of Induction:

"Whatever phenomenon varies in any manner whenever another phenomenon varies in some particular manner, is either a cause or an effect of that phenomenon, or is connected with it through some fact of causation."

Sir John Herschel states the same method as follows: "Increase or diminution of the effect, with the increased or diminished intensity of the cause, in cases which admit of increase and diminution," and he adds, "Reversal of the effect with that of the cause."

The examples of this method are countless. Mr. Joule, of Manchester, proved beyond doubt that friction is a cause of heat. He spent exact quantities of force (in today's terms, energy) rubbing one substance against another, and showed that the heat produced was greater or less in exact proportion as the force was greater or less. We can also apply the method to many cases that had earlier been handled by the simple method of difference. Instead of striking a bell in a complete vacuum, for instance, we can strike it with a very little air left in the receiver (the glass jar) of the air pump. We then hear a very faint sound, which grows louder or softer every time we increase or decrease the density of the air. This experiment fully satisfies anyone that air is the cause of the transmission of sound.

This method often lets us detect the material connection that exists between two bodies. For a long time it was doubtful whether the red flames seen during total eclipses of the sun belonged to the sun or to the moon. But during the last eclipse of the sun, it was noticed that the flames moved with the sun, and that the moon gradually covered and uncovered them at successive moments of the eclipse. From then on, no one could doubt that they belonged to the sun.

Again, whenever phenomena go through Periodic Changes, increasing and decreasing by turns, we should look for other phenomena that go through changes in exactly the same periods, and there will probably be a connection of cause and effect between them. This is how the tides are proved to be caused by the attraction of the moon and the sun. The periods of high and low tides, and of spring and neap tides, follow one another at intervals that match the apparent revolutions of those bodies around the earth. The moon turns on its own axis in exactly the same period in which it goes around the earth, so that for unknown ages the same side of the moon has always faced the earth. This is a perfect case of concomitant variations, and it proves beyond doubt that the earth's attraction governs the moon's turning on its own axis.

The most extraordinary case of variations, however, is the connection that has been shown in recent years between the Aurora Borealis, magnetic storms, and the spots on the sun. Only in the last 30 or 40 years has it become known that the magnetic compass needle is subject from time to time to very slight but curious movements. At the same moments there are usually natural currents of electricity running in telegraph wires, strong enough to interfere with sending messages. These disturbances are known as magnetic storms, and they are often observed when a fine display of the Northern or Southern Lights is taking place somewhere on the earth. Observations over many years have shown that these storms reach their worst at the end of every eleven years (the maximum falling around the present year, 1870), and then weaken until the next period of eleven years has passed. Close observation of the sun over 30 or 40 years has shown that the size and number of the dark spots, which are gigantic storms going on at the sun's surface, increase and decrease at exactly the same periods as the magnetic storms at the earth's surface. No one can doubt, then, that these strange phenomena are connected, although how they are connected is quite unknown. It is now believed that the planets Jupiter, Saturn, Venus and Mars are the real causes of the disturbances, because Balfour Stewart and Warren de la Rue have shown that the motions of these planets correspond exactly to the periods of the sunspots. This is a most remarkable and far-reaching case of concomitant variations.

Next we have to consider a method of Induction that must be used when several causes act at once and their effects all blend together, producing a joint effect of the same kind as the separate effects. Suppose that in one experiment friction, combustion, compression and electric action are all going on at the same time. Each of these causes will produce a quantity of heat, the quantities will add together, and it will be hard or impossible to say how much is due to each cause separately. We may call this a case of the homogeneous intermixture of effects. The name means that the joint effect is of the same kind as the separate effects. Mr. Mill distinguishes it from cases of the heterogeneous, or, as he calls it, the heteropathic intermixture of effects, where the joint effect is totally different in kind from the separate effects. If we bend a bow too far, for example, it breaks instead of bending further. If we warm ice, it soon stops rising in temperature and melts. If we warm water, it rises in temperature homogeneously for a while, but then it suddenly stops, and an effect of a totally different kind follows: the production of vapor, or possibly an explosion.

Now, when the joint effect is of a heterogeneous kind, the method of difference is enough to find the cause of its occurrence. Whether a bow or a spring will break under a given weight is easy to test, and whether water will boil at a given temperature, at any given reading of the barometer, is just as easy to find out. But in the homogeneous intermixture of effects our task is more complicated. There are several causes, each producing part of the effect, and we want to know how much is due to each one. In this case we must use a further Inductive Method, which Mr. Mill calls the Method of Residues and states in his Fourth Canon:

"Subduct from any phenomenon such part as is known by previous inductions to be the effect of certain antecedents, and the residue of the phenomenon is the effect of the remaining antecedents."

If we know that the joint effect a, b, c is due to the causes A, B, and C, and we can prove that a is due to A and b to B, it follows that c must be due to C. There can be no simpler case of this than finding the exact weight of a load of goods in a cart. We weigh the cart and load together, and then subtract the tare, the weight of the cart alone, which we found earlier. In the same way we can find how much of the spring tides is due to the attraction of the sun, provided we have already worked out the height of the tide due to the moon, which will be about the average height of the tides over the whole lunar month. Subtract the moon's tide, and what remains is the sun's tide.

Newton used this method in a beautiful experiment to measure the elasticity of different substances. He made balls of the substances, let them swing against each other, and then observed how far they bounced back compared with how far they had originally fallen. But the loss of motion is due partly to imperfect elasticity and partly to the resistance of the air. He measured the second effect in the simplest possible way: he let the balls swing without striking each other, and observed how much smaller each swing was than the one before. This made it easy for him to calculate the amount that had to be subtracted for the resistance of the air.

This is the method we use when we make allowance for errors, or apply the necessary corrections, in observations. Few thermometers are quite accurate. But if we put a thermometer into melting snow, which has a temperature of exactly 0° Centigrade, or 32° Fahrenheit, we can see exactly how far below or above the true point the mercury stands. That tells us how much we should add to or subtract from the thermometer's readings to make them correct. The height of the barometer is affected by several causes besides changes in the pressure of the air. It is lowered by the capillary repulsion between the glass tube and the mercury. It is raised by the expansion of the mercury with heat, if the temperature is above 32° Fahrenheit. And it may be raised or lowered by any error in the length of the scale used to measure the height. In an accurate observation, all these effects are calculated and allowed for in the final result.

In chemical analysis this method is used constantly to find the proportional weights of substances that combine with each other. The composition of water, for example, is found like this. We take a known weight of copper oxide, pass hydrogen over it in a heated tube, and condense the water produced in a tube containing sulfuric acid. Subtracting the original weight of the condensing tube from its final weight tells us how much water was produced. The quantity of oxygen in that water is found by subtracting the final weight of the copper oxide from its original weight. If we then subtract the weight of the oxygen from the weight of the water, we learn the weight of the hydrogen that we have combined with the oxygen. When the experiment is done very carefully, as described in Dr. Roscoe's Lessons in Elementary Chemistry (p. 38), we find that 88.89 parts by weight of oxygen unite with 11.11 parts of hydrogen to form 100 parts of water.

This method is used in every science that allows quantities to be measured, but above all in astronomy, the most exact of all the sciences. Almost all the causes and effects in astronomy have been discovered as residual phenomena. That is, astronomers calculate the effects of all the known attractions on a planet or a satellite, and then observe how far it is from the place those calculations predicted. When this was done very carefully for Uranus, the planet was still found to be sometimes ahead of and sometimes behind its true place. This residual effect pointed to some cause of attraction that was not yet known, and as a result that cause was soon discovered in the shape of the planet Neptune. The motions of several comets have been calculated in the same way, but it is observed that they fail to return at the expected time. There is a discrepancy that points to some obstructing power in the space they pass through, whose nature is not yet understood.

Mill's System of Logic, Book III. Chap. 10, Of the Plurality of Causes; and of the Intermixture of Effects.

Lesson 30: Empirical and Deductive Methods

So far we have treated deduction and induction as if they were two entirely separate and independent methods. In reality they are often blended together, or used by turns, in the search for truth. In fact, all the more important and far-reaching investigations in science can be said to rely on one just as much as on the other. Probably the greatest merit of Mr. J. S. Mill's writings on logic is that he shows how completely inadequate the so-called Baconian Method is for detecting the more obscure and difficult laws of nature. Bacon advised that we should always begin by collecting facts, sort them according to how they agree and differ, and gradually gather from them laws of greater and greater generality. He protested against "anticipating nature" altogether, that is, against forming our own hypotheses and theories about what the laws of nature probably are. He seems to have thought that arranging facts systematically would take the place of every other method. The reader will soon see that the progress of science has not borne out his opinions.

When a law of nature has been found purely by induction from certain observations or experiments, and has nothing else to guarantee its truth, it is called an empirical law. As Mill puts it, "Scientific inquirers give the name of Empirical Laws to uniformities which observation or experiment has shown to exist, but on which they hesitate to rely in cases varying much from those which have been actually observed, for want of seeing any reason why such a law should exist." The name comes from the Greek word ἐμπειρία (empeiria), meaning experience or trial. Examples of such laws are plentiful. We learn empirically that a certain strong yellow color at sunset, or an unusual clearness in the air, warns of rain; that a quick pulse is a sign of fever; that horned animals are always ruminants (animals that chew the cud); that quinine does good to the nervous system and to the health of the body in general; and that strychnine has a terrible effect of the opposite kind. We know all of these are true from repeated observation, but we can give no other reason for their truth. That is, we cannot bring them into harmony with any other scientific facts, and we could never have deduced them or predicted them from what we knew before. The connection between sunspots, magnetic storms, auroras and the motions of the planets, mentioned in the last lesson, is perhaps the most remarkable empirical induction known in the 1870s, for no one has yet given any hint of how these magnetic influences could act across the vast distances of the planetary system. The properties of the various alloys of metals are also good examples of empirical knowledge. When two or three metals are mixed in a given proportion for the first time, no one can tell in advance what the mixture will be like. No one could have predicted that brass would be both harder and more ductile than either of the metals it is made from, copper and zinc; that copper, mixed with the very soft metal tin, would make hard, ringing bell-metal; or that a certain mixture of lead, bismuth, tin and cadmium would melt at a temperature (65° centigrade, or 149° Fahrenheit) far below that of boiling water.

Footnote: Roscoe's Lessons in Elementary Chemistry.

However useful empirical knowledge may be, it matters very little compared with the well-connected and fully explained body of knowledge that makes up an advanced, deductive science. In fact, a science becomes perfect exactly in proportion as it becomes deductive and lets us bring more and more facts that seem unconnected under the same law. Whoever knows exactly why a thing happens will also know exactly in which cases it will happen, and what difference in the circumstances will stop it from happening. Take the simple fact that hot water cracks glass. Most people learn this empirically. They have a vague idea that hot water has a natural and unavoidable tendency to break glass, and that thin glass, being more fragile than other glass, will be broken more easily. Physical science, however, gives a very clear reason for the effect. It shows that this is just one case of the general tendency of heat to make substances expand. The crack happens when the heated glass manages to expand in spite of the colder glass it is joined to. But then we see at once that the same will not be true of thin glass vessels. The heat passes through them so quickly that the whole of the glass is heated almost equally. And so chemists regularly use thin, even glass vessels to hold or boil hot liquids, without any fear of the cracks that would be sure to happen in thick glass vessels or bottles.

The history of science would show beyond question that deduction was the key to all the greatest discoveries. Newton, who after Galileo was the chief founder of experimental science, had without doubt the greatest power of deductive thought any human being has ever had. It is striking to compare his results in optics with his results in chemistry, or rather alchemy. It is not widely known that Newton really was an alchemist. He spent days and nights experimenting in his laboratory, trying to discover the secret of turning metals into gold. But in this research everything was purely empirical, and he had no clue to guide him toward successful experiments. A few lucky guesses in his famous Queries (the questions he added at the end of his Opticks) are all that came of this labor. In optics it was quite different. There he grasped general laws, and every experiment led him to design several more and to predict their results, each more beautiful than the last. In this way he was able to lay, beyond all doubt, the foundations of the science of the spectrum, which is now producing such wonderful results. Some people may suppose that Newton, living not long after Bacon, adopted the Baconian method. But I believe there is no mention of Bacon anywhere in Newton's works, and it is certain that he did not use Bacon's method. The Principia, though it appeals to experiment and observation all the way through, is nonetheless the product of a constant, sustained effort of deductive mathematical reasoning.

What Mill has called the Deductive Method, but which I think might more fittingly be called the Combined or Complete Method, consists of using induction and deduction by turns. It can be said to have three steps:

1. Direct Induction.

2. Deduction, or, as Mill calls it, Ratiocination.

3. Verification.

The first step is a rough and simple appeal to experience, enough to give us a glimpse of the laws at work but not enough to prove them true. Taking these laws as true for the time being, we then reason out what their effects would be in other cases. A further appeal to experience then either verifies the laws we assumed or shows them to be false. In short, there are two appeals to experience, joined by reasoning in between. Newton, for example, passed a ray of sunlight through a glass prism and found that it spread out into a band of colors like those of the rainbow. He adopted the theory that white light is really a mixture of lights of different colors, which get separated as they pass through the prism. He saw that if this were true, then if he passed a single ray from the spectrum, say the yellow ray, through a second prism, it ought not to break up into different colors again. It should stay yellow, whatever was done with it afterward. When he tried it, he found that this was so. He then devised a whole series of similar experiments confirming it, which verified his theory beyond all possible doubt.

It was no mere accident that led Pascal to have a barometer carried to the top of the mountain Puy de Dôme in France. Galileo, it is true, learned by accident that water will not rise more than 33 feet in an ordinary pump, and this led him to claim that it was the limited weight of the atmosphere that made the water rise. Torricelli reasoned from this theory. Mercury is fourteen times as heavy as water, so, he saw, it ought not to rise more than one-fourteenth of that height, or about 29 or 30 inches. The experiment was tried, and it verified the theory. But it took the genius of Pascal to see that the experiment needed to be varied in another way, by carrying the mercury barometer to the top of a mountain. If the weight of the atmosphere really was what held the mercury up, the mercury ought to stand lower on the mountain than at the bottom, because only the upper part of the atmosphere would be pressing down on the mountaintop. The experiment succeeded, and completely verified the original hypothesis. The progress of the experimental sciences depends mainly on this way in which one experiment leads to others and brings to light new facts. In all probability we would never have noticed those facts if we had kept to the purely Baconian method of collecting the facts first and doing the induction afterward.

The greatest achievement of the deductive method is nothing less than the theory of gravitation, which is a perfect example of how the method works. Here the first induction was, we may suppose, the famous fall of the apple, which happened while Newton was sitting in an orchard, during the time he had left London because of the Great Plague. The fall of the apple, so we are told, led Newton to reflect that there must be some power tending to pull bodies toward the earth, and he asked himself why, in that case, the moon did not fall onto the earth. The Lancashire astronomer Horrocks had brought another fact to his mind: when a stone tied to a string is whirled around, it pulls on the string with a force often called centrifugal force. Horrocks had remarked that the planets, as they revolve around the sun, must in the same way tend to fly off from the center. Newton knew Horrocks's views, and so he may have been led to suppose that the earth's attractive force might exactly balance the moon's centrifugal tendency, and so keep the moon going around and around.

But it so happened that the world already had certain empirical laws about the motions of the planets, and without them Newton could hardly have gone any further. Kepler had spent a lifetime observing the heavenly bodies and making up hypotheses to explain their motions. Most of his ideas were wild and baseless, but a lifetime of work was rewarded when he established the three laws that bear his name. They describe the shape of the orbits the planets travel in, and the relation between the size of a planet's orbit and the time the planet takes to go around it. Newton was able to show by geometrical reasoning that if one body revolved around another, attracted toward it by a force that decreases as the square of the distance increases, it would necessarily travel in an orbit for which Kepler's laws hold true, and which would therefore be exactly like the orbits of the planets. This was a partial verification of his theory by an appeal to the results of experience. But several other thinkers had already got that far in the subject. Newton's chief claim to honor is that he kept on with his deductions and verifications until he reached complete proof. To do this, he first had to show that the moon really does fall toward the earth just as fast as a stone would if it were in the same position. Using the best figures then available for the distance of the moon, Newton calculated that the moon falls through 13 feet in one minute, but that a stone raised to the same height would fall through 15 feet. Most people would have taken this near agreement as proof of the theory. But Newton's love of certain truth set him apart even from most scientists, and the difference made him lay "aside at that time any further thoughts of this matter."

It was not until many years later (probably fifteen or sixteen) that Newton heard of more exact data for calculating the distance of the moon, and was able to explain the difference. His theory of gravitation was then verified as far as the moon was concerned. But for him this was only the start of a long series of deductive calculations, each ending in a verification. If the earth and the moon attract each other, and so do the sun and the earth, there is no reason why the sun and the moon should not attract each other too. Newton worked out the consequences of this inference, and showed that the moon would not move as if it were attracted by the earth alone, but would go sometimes faster and sometimes slower. Comparison with Flamsteed's observations of the moon showed that this was the case. Newton argued further that since the waters of the ocean are not rigidly fixed to the earth, they might attract the moon, and be attracted by it in return, independently of the rest of the earth. That would cause certain daily motions of the water exactly like the tides, and there were the tides to verify it. What makes Newton stand above every other scientist is the almost superhuman power with which he traced out the consequences of his theory geometrically and tested them again and again against experience.

What he began has been going on ever since. The positions of the moon and planets are calculated for every day on the assumption that Newton's law of gravitation is absolutely true. Every night their positions are observed, as far as possible, at Greenwich or some other observatory. When the observed position is compared with the predicted one, there is always some degree of error, and if the two ever coincided exactly it would only be by accident. The theory is never proved completely true, and never can be. But the more accurately its results are calculated, and the more perfect the astronomer's instruments become, the closer the agreement gets. So the rough observations of Kepler, and the few small facts that worked on Newton's mind, became the foundation of a theory that gave us endless ways of predicting new facts. By constant verification, as far as human accuracy can reach, it has been placed beyond all reasonable doubt.

If there were room, it could be shown that all the other great theories have followed much the same course. The wave theory (undulatory theory) of sound was in fact very nearly verified by Newton himself, although when he calculated the speed of sound from it there was again a difference, which only later research could explain. This theory no doubt suggested the matching theory of light. When Young, Fresnel and others worked that theory out, its results always turned out in the end to agree with observation. It even let mathematicians predict results that the most eager imagination could hardly have guessed, and that experimenting at random might never have revealed. Dalton's laws of equivalent proportions in chemistry, if not his atomic theory, were founded on experiments made with the simplest and roughest apparatus, yet results deduced from them are verified every day in the most delicate processes of modern chemical analysis. The still more recent theory of the Conservation of Energy had been vaguely anticipated by Bacon, Rumford, Montgolfier, Seguin, Mayer and possibly others. Mr. Joule brought it to the test of experimental verification in some of the most beautiful and decisive experiments on record. It will be a long time before scientists have traced out all the consequences of this grand principle, but the way it agrees with the facts already puts it far beyond doubt.

I think it will now be clear that, although observation and induction must always be the ground of all certain knowledge of nature, on their own they could never have produced the results of modern science. A person who merely collects and digests facts will seldom come to understand their laws. A person who builds a theory and is satisfied with his own deductions from it, as Descartes did, will only astonish the world with his misused genius. The best student of science is the one who, with a plentiful store of theories and fancies, has the greatest power of foreseeing their consequences, the greatest care in comparing them with facts beyond doubt, and the greatest honesty in admitting the ninety-nine mistakes he has made on the way to the one true law of nature.

Questions and Exercises

1. Define an empirical law, and find a few more examples of such laws.

2. What are the three steps of the Deductive Method?

3. Trace some of the successive steps in the progress of the theory of gravitation, showing that it was established by this method.

Lesson 31: Explanation, Tendency, Hypothesis, Theory, and Fact

In the last few lessons I have used several expressions without ever defining what they mean. Now is a good time to show how these terms are used, with examples, and to get as clear an understanding as we can of their proper meanings.

Explanation literally means making something plain, or level, so that there is nothing uneven or obscure to block our view. Scientific explanation consists in harmonizing fact with fact, or fact with law, or law with law, so that we can see both to be cases of one uniform law of causation. Suppose we hear of a great earthquake in some part of the world, and later hear that a nearby volcano has erupted. We say that the earthquake is now partly explained. The eruption shows that great forces were at work beneath the earth's surface, and the earthquake is obviously an effect of causes like these. Or take the scratches that can be plainly seen on the surface of rocks in certain parts of Wales and Cumberland (in the English Lake District). They are explained by the fact that glaciers once existed in those mountains: the scratches match exactly the effects of the glaciers that still exist in Switzerland, Greenland and elsewhere. We may call these explanations of fact by fact.

A fact may also be explained by a general law of nature. That is, we can point out the cause and the way the fact was produced, and show that it is the same cause that operates in many cases that look quite different. So the cracking of glass by heat was explained (in Lesson 30) as one result of the universal law that heat increases the dimensions of solid bodies. The trade-winds are explained as one case of the general tendency of warm air to rise and be pushed aside by cold, dense air. The very same simple laws of heat and mechanics that make a draft flow up a chimney when there is a fire below also make the winds blow from each hemisphere toward the equator. At the same time, the simplest laws of motion explain why the winds come from the east. The earth rotates from west to east, and its surface moves much faster at the equator than nearer the poles. The air coming from the north or south tends to keep its slower rate of motion, so as the earth near the equator moves on beneath it, the wind seems to blow from east to west.

According to Mr. J. S. Mill, there are three distinct ways in which one law may be explained by other laws, or brought into harmony with them.

The first is the case where two or more separate causes are really at work, and their results are combined, or added together, homogeneously. As was explained before (in Lesson 29), the homogeneous intermixture of effects means that the joint effect is simply the sum of the separate effects, and is of the same kind as they are. Our last example, the trade-winds, really comes under this case. We find one law, or tendency, that makes winds blow from the arctic regions toward the equator, and a second tendency that makes them blow from east to west. These tendencies combine, and make the trade-winds blow from the northeast in the northern hemisphere and from the southeast in the southern hemisphere. Another example is the law that governs the temperature of the air in any part of the earth. It is a very complicated one. It depends partly on the law that governs the sun's heating power, and partly on the earth's power to radiate the heat away into space, but perhaps even more on the way currents of air or water bring warmth in or carry it away. The path of a cannon-ball, or any other projectile, is likewise fixed by several laws acting together. First comes the simple law of motion, by which any moving body tends to keep moving onward at a uniform rate in a straight line. Second comes the law of gravity, which keeps bending the body's path toward the earth's surface. Third comes the resistance of the air, which tends to reduce its velocity.

The reader may have noticed how often I use the word tendency, and that I have repeatedly spoken of a cause as tending to produce its effect. If the joint, homogeneous action of causes has been made clear, it will now be clear what a tendency is. A tendency means a cause that will produce an effect unless there are opposite causes which, combined with it, counteract and disguise that effect. When we throw a stone into the air, for instance, the attractive power of the earth tends to make it fall, but the upward motion we have given it disguises the result for a certain time. The endless circling motion of the moon around the earth is the result of two balanced tendencies: one toward the earth, and one to go straight on in a line. The laws of motion and gravity are such that this balance must always be kept. If anything brought the moon nearer to the earth, its tendency to fly off would increase, and would outweigh the effect of gravity until the moon had got back to its proper distance. A tendency, then, is a cause that may or may not be counteracted.

In the second case of explanation, an effect is shown to be due, not directly to the supposed cause, but to an intermediate effect of that cause. Instead of A being the cause of C, we find that A is the cause of B, and B the cause of C, so that B forms an intermediate link. This kind of explanation may seem to make the matter more complicated, but it really simplifies it. The connection of A with B may be a case of some familiar and simple law, and so may the connection of B with C, while the law that A produces C may be purely empirical and seem out of harmony with everything else. In lightning, for example, it looks as if electricity had the power of creating a loud explosion. In reality electricity only produces heat, and it is the heat that makes the sound, by suddenly expanding the air. So thunder comes into harmony with the sound of artillery, which is also caused by the sudden expansion of the heated gases given off by the gunpowder. Again, when chlorine was discovered, it was soon found to have a strong power of bleaching, and today almost all bleaching is done with chlorine instead of by the sun, as it used to be. Inquiry showed, however, that it was not really the chlorine that destroyed the color. Oxygen is the intermediate and active agent. Chlorine breaks down water, takes the hydrogen, and leaves the oxygen in a state of great activity, ready to destroy the organic coloring matter. In this way a number of facts are harmonized. We learn why dry chlorine does not bleach, and why several other substances resemble chlorine in their bleaching power: for instance ozone, hydrogen peroxide, sulfurous acid, and a peculiar oxide of vanadium recently discovered by Dr. Roscoe. We could not understand the effect at all unless we knew that in all these cases it is probably due to active oxygen, or ozone, even in the old method of bleaching by laying things out in the sun.

Footnote: Watts's Dictionary of Chemistry, Vol. i. p. 601.

The third case of explanation, and much the most important, is where one law is shown to be a case of a more general law. As was explained in Lesson 24, we naturally discover the less general laws first, and only gradually make our way to the simpler but deeper secrets of nature. It has often happened that scientists possessed several well-known laws without seeing the bond that connected them. People had long known, for instance, that all heavy bodies tend to fall toward the earth, and before Newton's time Hooke, Huygens and others knew that some force probably connected the earth with the sun and moon. It was Newton, however, who clearly brought these and many other facts under one general law, so that each fact, or less general law, throws light on every other.
