The science of Electricity now harmonizes a vast series of partial laws and facts, among which it was truly hard to find any resemblance. The chief properties of the magnet had been fairly well known since the time of Gilbert, the physician of Queen Elizabeth. Ordinary frictional electricity was carefully studied by Otto von Guericke, Aepinus, Coulomb and others. Galvanism (electricity produced by chemical action, as in a battery) was investigated in great detail almost as soon as Galvani and Volta discovered that the chemical action of one substance on another can produce electricity. In the early part of this century there were three distinct sciences, Magnetism, Electricity and Galvanism; now there is only one. Oersted of Copenhagen supplied the first link between them in 1819, when he pointed out that an electric current can make a compass-needle move. Ampère and Faraday worked out the complicated relations among the three sciences, and in the end took them all into a wider science. We may call it Electro-magnetism, or perhaps, more conveniently, we may widen the name Electricity to take in all the phenomena connected with it.

A number of minor laws and separate facts are taken in and explained by the theory now generally accepted: that heat, electricity, light, and in fact all the phenomena of nature, are only different forms in which one and the same kind of energy shows itself. The total amount of energy in the universe is held to be fixed and unchangeable, like the quantity of matter. Sometimes it is disguised, because it affects only the molecules too small to perceive. At other times we see it produce visible mechanical effects, as in the fall of a stone or the expansion of steam. Now it had been known ever since the time of the Greeks that a simple lever greatly alters the character of a force, making its action slower or faster, but does not alter its amount: the more intense the force, the slower and more limited its action. In modern times a similar truth was proved of every kind of machine, and it was recognized that, apart from friction, no machine of any kind either creates or destroys energy. Quite separately, it had been recognized that the electricity produced in a galvanic battery is exactly proportional to the amount of chemical action, and that almost any one of the forces just named can be converted into any of the others. All facts like these are now taken in under one general theory. Its details are gradually being made more certain and accurate, but its main principle is that a certain amount of mechanical energy is equal to a certain amount of heat, or of electricity, or of chemical action, or even of muscular effort.

The word hypothesis is used a great deal in connection with the subject we are discussing, and we must consider what it means. It comes from the Greek words ὑπό (hupo), under, and θέσις (thesis), placing, so it means exactly the same as the Latin word suppositio, a placing under, which gives us our everyday word supposition. In science it seems to mean imagining some thing, force or cause that lies underneath the phenomena we are examining and is the agent that produces them, but that cannot be directly observed. When we make a hypothesis, we assert that a cause exists on the strength of the effects we observe. How probable it is that the cause exists depends on how many different facts or partial laws it lets us explain, or bring into harmony. To be of any value at all, a hypothesis must harmonize at least two different facts. Suppose we account for the effects of opium by saying, with Molière, that it possesses a dormitive power (a power of putting people to sleep), or say that a magnet attracts because it has a magnetic power. Everyone can see that we gain nothing. We know neither more nor less about the dormitive power or the magnetic power than we did about the opium or the magnet. But suppose we say that a magnet attracts because currents of electricity are circulating inside it. The hypothesis may seem very improbable, but it is valid, because it draws a certain analogy between a magnet and a coil of wire carrying electricity. Such a coil of wire attracts other coils in exactly the way one magnet attracts another, so this hypothesis lets us harmonize several different facts.

Take another example: the existence of intense heat in the interior of the earth. It is hypothetical in the sense that we cannot actually see the heat and measure it directly. But it harmonizes so many facts drawn from different sources that we can hardly doubt it is there. Hot springs and volcanoes are facts in its favor, though they might be explained on other grounds. The empirical law that the heat increases as we sink mines, in any part of the earth's surface, is stronger evidence. The intensely heated condition of the sun and the other stars strongly confirms it, because it shows that other bodies really do exist in the condition we suppose the earth's interior to be in. The cool state of the earth's surface is perfectly consistent with the earth's comparatively small size and with the known facts and laws about how heat is conducted and radiated. And the more we learn about the way the sun's heat is kept up by the fall of meteoric matter into it (the explanation most favored in the 1870s), the more probable it becomes that the earth, too, was once intensely heated like the sun, although it has been slowly cooling for an immense length of time. A supposition that agrees with so many facts, laws and other probable hypotheses almost stops being hypothetical, and its high probability makes us regard it as a known fact.

As long as it is consistent with the laws of thought, there is nothing we may not have to accept as a probable hypothesis, however hard it may be to imagine and understand. The force of gravity is hypothetical in the sense that we know it only by its effects on the motions of bodies. The way it weakens with distance does indeed match exactly the way that light, sound, electric or magnetic attraction, and in fact every influence that spreads out from a point through space, grows weaker. So it is probable that the law of the inverse square is absolutely true. But in other respects gravity goes strongly against all our ideas. If sound could travel to the sun as fast as it travels in the earth's atmosphere, it would take nearly fourteen years to get there. If the sun and the earth were joined by a solid, continuous bar of iron, a strong pull at one end would not be felt at the other for nearly three years. Light, it is true, comes from the sun in a little more than eight minutes. But what are we to think of the force of gravity, which seems to reach the sun in an instant, so short that no calculation has yet been able to detect any interval at all? In fact there seems to be some reason to suppose that gravity is felt instantaneously throughout the immeasurable regions of space.

The undulatory hypothesis of light (the hypothesis that light is a wave) has features just as extraordinary and inconceivable. That light really does consist of tiny but extremely rapid vibrations of something that fills space is almost certain. This hypothesis brings great harmony into the enormously varied and complicated phenomena of light, and it explains the analogy between light and sound. It is hard, of course, to imagine that anything can vibrate so fast as to strike the retina of the eye 831,479,000,000,000 times in one second, which is what must happen with violet light according to this hypothesis. But that is nothing compared with the difficulty of imagining space filled with a solid ether (the medium that was then believed to carry light), extremely rigid and elastic, which nevertheless offers no noticeable resistance to ordinary matter passing through it, and has no weight of its own. It has indeed been claimed that comets come back later than they should because of friction against this ether, and Mr. Balfour Stewart believes he has produced heat by the friction of a metal disc against the ether in a vacuum. If these claims prove true, we will have new facts in harmony with the theory of light, which will then be less hypothetical than before.

Footnote: See Sir John Herschel's Familiar Lectures p. 315, &c.

Now there is no difficulty in seeing the part that hypothesis plays in the deductive method of scientific investigation, which we considered in the last lesson. The preliminary induction is replaced, more or less completely, by imagining agents that we think could produce the known effects in question. Suppose our object is to explain what causes ebbing and flowing wells, which are found in many parts of the world (springs that flow and stop, or rise and fall, at intervals). We cannot possibly begin by exploring the inside of the earth until we find the source of a spring and observe its circumstances. We have to imagine cavities and channels of various shapes, until we think of an arrangement that would, according to the known laws of hydrostatics, make the water flow irregularly in the way we observe. If we can show that cavities of a particular shape will produce that effect, and can think of no other way it could be produced, the hypothesis becomes established as almost a certain fact.

It is the same with any great hypothesis, such as the theory of light. We have no way of directly observing and measuring the qualities of the ether that is the medium of light. All we know about this ether at present comes from the observed phenomena of light. So we are driven to invent something and give it qualities from which we can calculate, by certain principles of mechanics, what effects to expect. Then, finding that these effects can be made to harmonize with the effects actually observed, we rely on this agreement to prove that the ether exists. The truth of a hypothesis therefore depends entirely on its later verification, its agreement with observed facts. To invent hypotheses that cannot be verified in this way, or to invent them and then neglect to verify them, leads either to no result at all or to fallacy. But when the verification is careful and complete, no one can fairly object to the use of hypothesis. It may then be as certain as any other way of investigating, and it is indispensable in any case. In fact there was little truth or reason in Newton's famous protest against the use of hypothesis, "Hypotheses non fingo" (I do not frame hypotheses). The truth is that his theory of gravitation rested on the greatest and most successful of all hypotheses, while his views of the material nature of light, and of the causes of its peculiar phenomena, involved a false hypothesis that has long since been completely disproved.

The word theory has been used constantly in the last few lessons, and it deserves some examination. It comes from the Greek θεωρία (theoria), meaning contemplation, reflection or speculation, but that gives us little clue to how we use it now. In reality the word is highly ambiguous. Sometimes it is used as the equivalent of hypothesis, and at other times as the equivalent of a general law or truth. When people form theories about comets, the sun, the cause of earthquakes and so on, they imagine a great many things that may or may not exist. Theories like these are really complicated hypotheses, and should be called so. In this sense there are two theories of electricity. One supposes that there is a single fluid, which collects in some places and then tends to discharge itself toward places where there is too little of it, just as water always tends to find its level. The other supposes that there are two fluids, which are usually united but, when separated, tend to rush back together again. These so-called theories are really hypotheses, because we have no independent evidence that any such fluid exists, and it is now almost certain that there is no such thing. The atomic theory, again, is really a hypothesis put forward by Dalton to explain the remarkable laws he discovered in the proportions in which chemical elements combine. It is a valid hypothesis insofar as it really does explain why the quantities that combine are fixed. But as to the shapes, properties or absolute sizes of the atoms, it is purely hypothetical, because we have no facts in those respects for it to harmonize, and no apparent way of getting any.

In another, and more proper, sense, theory is opposed to practice, just as the general is opposed to the particular. The theory of gravitation means all the more general laws of motion and attraction on which Newton founded his system of the universe. We may know what those laws are without being able to work out the position of a planet or put them to any practical use. Skilled astronomers must calculate the particular results before navigators, travelers or anyone else can use them in practice to find latitude or longitude. When we speak of the mathematical theory of sound, the lunar theory or the theory of the tides, the word has no special reference to hypothesis. It simply means general knowledge, or science. It implies that we possess a complete series of general and accurate laws, but does nothing to set them apart from accurate knowledge in general. When a word like theory really is used in an equivocal way, it is not wise to try to give it an exact definition, which would be imaginary and artificial.

The word fact is used very often in this book, as in most books, and calls for a few remarks. It comes from factum, the past participle of facere, to do, and so would mean something done, an act or deed. But its meaning has obviously been greatly extended by analogy. We usually set fact and theory against each other. But just as theory seems to have two meanings that get confused, I believe that fact is ambiguous too. Sometimes it means what is certain and known by the evidence of the senses, as opposed to what is known only probably, by hypothesis and inference. At other times it is contrasted with a general law, and means a particular instance or case. A law of great generality may often be just as certain and true as the particular facts that come under it, especially in mathematics, so in this case the contrast must be between the general and the particular. We also often use the word in everyday life simply to mean a truth. We might say, for example, "It is a fact that the primary laws of thought are the foundation of reasoning." In short, just as theory means, ambiguously, what is hypothetical, general, abstract or uncertain, so fact is equally ambiguous, and means, in a confused way, what is intuitively known, particular, concrete or certain.

See Mill's System of Logic, Book III, Chapters 12, 13 and 14, Of Explanation, and Hypothesis.

Questions and Exercises

1. What do you mean by the explanation of a fact?

2. State the three ways in which a law of nature may be explained, and suggest some more examples of each case.

3. Define tendency. Do all causes consist only of tendencies, or can you find examples to the contrary?

4. Give a definition of hypothesis. How can a valid hypothesis be told apart from an invalid one?

5. What place does hypothesis hold in the Deductive Method?

6. Explain the ambiguities of the words theory and fact.

Subsidiaries of Induction

Lesson 32: Classification, and Abstraction

In Lesson 12, on the Predicables, we looked at the doctrine of classification as logicians treated it many centuries ago. Over the last two centuries, though, the progress of science has drawn a great deal of attention to the true principles by which we can put a huge number of different objects in order. So we now have to ask what makes a system of classification natural and perfect.

In fact, you could say that our subject here covers the whole science of logic. All thought and all reasoning, as far as it deals with general names or general notions, can be said to consist in classification. Every common, or general, name is the name of a class, and every name of a class is a common name. "Metal" is the name of one class of substances, the one we have used so often in our syllogisms; "element" is the name of another class, of which the first is a part. Reasoning has been described, quite plausibly, as affirming of the parts of a class whatever can be affirmed of the whole. Every law of nature we arrive at lets us class a number of facts together. It would hardly be going too far to define logic as the theory of classification.

Here, however, we are dealing with the more deliberate and clear-cut arrangement of objects or notions that is used above all in the natural sciences, such as botany, zoology, mineralogy and paleontology.

The history of the word class is rather curious. In ancient Rome the whole people was summoned together at certain times, and this ceremony was called a classis, from the Greek κλάσις (klasis) or κλῆσις (klēsis), which comes from καλέω (kaleō), to call together. Servius Tullius is said to have divided the people into six orders according to how much tax they could pay, and these orders were, naturally enough, called the classes of the people. From there the name gradually came to be used for any organized body of people, such as an army. Then it was carried over to a fleet of ships drawn up in a fixed order, and finally, by analogy, it was stretched to cover any collection of objects carefully arranged. It is curious that when we speak today of the lower or higher classes of the people, we are bringing the word back very close to its original meaning.

Classification is perhaps best defined as the arrangement of things, or of our notions of them, according to their resemblances or identities. Every class should be made up of objects that resemble each other exactly in certain definite qualities, and those qualities are stated in the definition of the class. The more numerous and wide-reaching the resemblances that a system of classes points out, the more perfect and useful we must consider that system.

Mr. J. S. Mill describes his view of what classification means this way: "Classification is a contrivance for the best possible ordering of the ideas of objects in our minds; for causing the ideas to accompany or succeed one another in such a way as shall give us the greatest command over our knowledge already acquired, and lead most directly to the acquisition of more. The general problem of classification, in reference to these purposes, may be stated as follows: To provide that things shall be thought of in such groups, and those groups in such an order, as will best conduce to the remembrance, and to the ascertainment of their laws."

A collection of objects can usually be classified in an unlimited number of ways. Any quality that some of them have and others lack can be taken as the first difference, and the groups it marks off can then be subdivided, one after another, by any other qualities we like. A library of books, for example, could be arranged (1) by size, (2) by the language they are written in, (3) by the alphabetical order of their authors' names, (4) by subject, and in various other ways. Large libraries and catalogues use these arrangements and combine them in different ways. Each arrangement has its own particular convenience, and we must choose the one that best serves the special purpose of the library or catalogue. The population of a country, again, can be classified in an almost endless number of ways, for different purposes or different sciences. The people of the United Kingdom can be divided by place of birth, as English, Welsh, Scottish, Irish, colonial-born, and foreigners. The ethnographer would divide them into Anglo-Saxons, Cymri (the Welsh Britons), Gaels, Picts, Scandinavians, and so on. The statistician arranges them by age; by marital condition, as married, unmarried, widowed, and so on; and by state of body, as able-bodied, incapacitated, blind, or intellectually disabled. The economist looks at the countless trades people carry on and classifies them in a complicated way. The lawyer, again, treats everyone as a minor, an adult, a feme sole (an unmarried woman, who held property in her own name), a feme couverte (a married woman, whose legal identity the law then merged with her husband's), a guardian, ward, trustee, felon, and so on.

In the natural world, too, we can make various classifications. Plants can be arranged by the country they come from; by the kind of place, or habitat, they thrive in; by how long they live, as annual, biennial, perennial; by size, as herbs, shrubs, trees; or by their uses, as food plants, drugs, or poisons. All of these are different from the classifications that botanists devise to represent the natural affinities, or relationships, of plants. So it is clear that when we make a classification there is no single fixed method that can be found by rule. Usually an unlimited number of choices are open to us. In cases like this logic cannot do much; it is really the job of the special sciences to work out what kind of classification is needed. All logic can do is point out certain general requirements and principles.

The first requirement of a good classification is that it suits the purpose in hand. That is, the points of resemblance chosen to form the leading classes should be the ones that matter for the practical use of the classification. Everything that needs to be treated alike must be grouped together, and things that need to be treated differently must be separated. A lawyer, for instance, has no need to classify people by the English county they were born in, because the law is the same whatever the county. But a Scot, a Manxman, or a foreigner lives under laws different from those of a man born in England, and to that extent we will have to classify them separately. A gardener is quite right to classify plants as annuals, biennials, perennials; as herbs, shrubs, trees; as evergreen and deciduous; or by the soil, temperature and other conditions that affect them. These are exactly the points that must guide him in treating some plants differently from others.

A second requirement, and from a scientific point of view the most important one, is that a good classification lets us make the greatest possible number of general assertions. This is the criterion, as Dr. Whewell stated it, that distinguishes a natural system of classification from an artificial one, and we need to look carefully at what it means. Clearly, a good classification is more than just an orderly arrangement. It involves a process of induction that brings to light all the more general relations among the things being classified. An arrangement of books will usually be artificial: the octavo volumes have nothing in common except being octavo size. An alphabetical arrangement of names, again, is extremely convenient and suitable for many purposes, but it is artificial, because it allows few or no general assertions. We cannot make any general assertion at all about people just because their names happen to begin with an A or a B, a P or a W. Even the people who share the name Smith or Taylor or Robinson could be put through the inductive method of agreement without our discovering any common circumstance that could be stated as a general proposition, or law. It is true that if we looked into the backgrounds of the Evanses and the Joneses, we would find nearly all of them to be Welsh, and the Campbells to be Scottish, and people with a very unusual name would often turn out to descend from common ancestors. To that extent even an alphabetical arrangement has something natural in it, and lets us make general assertions. In fact, hardly any arrangement can be made that does not show some traces of important relations and resemblances. But what we want is a system that will reveal all the most important general truths.

For this purpose we must choose, as the basis for grouping things together, the characters that carry the most other characters with them. In Lesson 12 we described a property (proprium) as a quality that belongs to the whole of a class without being part of the class's definition. Now, we should frame the definition of a class so that it contains as few characters as possible, while as many other characters, properties, or propria as possible can be attributed to the things in the class. Everyone can see, for instance, that animals form one great group of living beings with many characters in common, and that plants form another. Animals have sensation and voluntary motion; they consume carbon-containing food and give off carbonic acid (carbon dioxide); they have a stomach; and they produce fat. Plants lack sensation and voluntary motion; they build carbon-containing tissue, absorb carbonic acid, and give off oxygen; they have no stomach; and they produce starch. At one time it might have been thought that almost any one of these characters was enough to tell which group a living being belonged to. Whatever had a stomach was an animal, and whatever did not was a plant. Whatever produced starch or gave off oxygen was called a plant; whatever absorbed oxygen or produced fat was an animal. These statements are still generally true today, so we can make assertions in the form of the proposition U (the doubly universal affirmative of Lesson 22): "all animals are all beings that give off carbonic acid, and all plants are all beings that absorb carbonic acid." But in reality there are many exceptions, and the more research we do, the clearer it becomes that no definite line can be drawn between animal and vegetable life. This, of course, is not a failure of logical science. It is a highly significant fact about the things themselves.

When we classify plants, we again meet very deep and natural distinctions, this time between the great classes called Exogens, Endogens, and Acrogens (roughly, the groups botanists now call dicots, monocots, and the spore-bearing plants such as ferns and mosses). The Acrogens have no true flowers with sexes and no seeds. They are made almost entirely of cellular tissue, and their outer skin, or epidermis, has no pores. The other two classes have much in common: they have true flowers, woody tissue and pores in the skin, and so they can be united into one wider class, the Vasculares. But exogens and endogens are also very sharply distinguished from each other. Exogens have a stem or trunk made of distinct bark, pith, and wood in concentric layers; leaves with net-like veins; and seeds with two seed-leaves and a naked radicle (the root of the embryo). Generally, too, the parts of the flower come in some multiple of two or five. Endogens, on the other hand, have no distinct bark, pith, and wood, and no concentric layers; their leaves have parallel veins; their seeds have one seed-leaf and a radicle that is not naked; and the parts of the flower generally come in multiples of three.

These are the very widest classes in what is called the natural system of botanical arrangement, but the same principles hold in all its smaller classes. Botanists are constantly working to bring the great multitudes of plants together into species, genera, orders, classes, and various groups in between, so that the members of each group have the greatest number of points of resemblance to each other and the fewest points of resemblance to members of other groups. This is how the great purpose of classification is best fulfilled. Classification reduces multiplicity to unity, and it lets us infer of all the other members of a class what we know of any one member, provided we distinguish properly between the qualities that are likely, or known, to belong to the class and those that are peculiar to the individual. As Professor Huxley has remarked, it is a necessary condition of correct classification that the definition of a group should hold exactly true of all the members of the group, and not of the members of any other group. In the natural sciences, though, this condition is very hard to carry out, because kinds of plants or animals keep being discovered that sit halfway between classes that would otherwise be well distinguished. Ferns, for example, are a real embarrassment to the fundamental division of plants. They have no true flowers, and in this and other ways they agree with the other acrogens; yet they have plenty of woody fiber, which would entitle them to rank with the vasculares, the larger group that exogens and endogens are subdivisions of.

We might add that the progress of chemistry is quickly turning it into a science of classification. In fact the whole theory of chemical combination now depends on grouping the elements and compounds correctly. Dr. Roscoe, in his Lessons in Elementary Chemistry, lists no fewer than eleven classes of metals, each class having a number of properties in common. The metals of the alkalies, for instance, namely potassium, sodium, cesium, rubidium and lithium, form a remarkably natural class. They are all soft, melt easily, and turn to vapor at high temperatures. They combine very violently with oxygen and decompose water at any temperature, forming oxides that dissolve very readily in water and become powerfully caustic and alkaline substances, from which heat cannot drive the water out. Their carbonates dissolve in water, and each metal forms only one compound with chlorine.

The metals of the alkaline earths, calcium, strontium and barium, also form a very natural class. What distinguishes them is that their carbonates do not dissolve in pure water but do dissolve in water that has carbonic acid dissolved in it. The gold class contains the rare or valuable metals gold, platinum, palladium, rhodium, ruthenium, iridium and osmium. Nitric acid does not act on them, and they can only be dissolved by chlorine or by the mixture of acids called aqua regia. Their oxides can be reduced, or have their oxygen removed, simply by heating them.

Natural classifications give us the deepest resemblances and relations, and they may lead us in the end to know how the different varieties of things came to be produced. So they are essential to a true science; you could almost say they make up the framework of the science. It does not follow, though, that they suit every purpose. When all we want is to recognize which chemical element, plant or animal we have in front of us, its character as defined in a natural system will give us little or no help. A chemist does not detect potassium by getting it into the state of a metal and testing whether it decomposes water. He simply looks for whichever compounds of potassium have the most clearly marked and unusual characters. A compound of potassium, platinum and chlorine, for example, is the most distinctive, or characteristic, of the metal, and it is generally used as a way of recognizing it. The fine violet color that potash (a potassium compound) gives to the flame of a lamp was also used as a sign of potassium's presence long before the spectroscope was brought in to analyze such colors. An artificial classification of the elements is therefore necessary for detecting substances. Accordingly, any book on chemical analysis will contain arrangements of the elements by characters of very minor importance, which are chosen because they can be observed easily and reliably.

In botany, again, the natural system of classification is far from well suited to finding out the name of a plant. Its classes are often defined by the shape of tiny parts of the seed, the arrangement of the seed-vessel, and other parts that are usually hard, and sometimes impossible, to examine. So botanists usually arrange their genera and species in the order of the natural system, but devise a sort of key, or artificial arrangement, that uses the simplest and most obvious characters, often called characteristics, to tell the plants apart. The best arrangement of this kind for British plants is found in Bentham's British Flora. In fact, the famous Linnaean arrangement of plants was intended by its author to serve exactly this purpose. Linnaeus was far too deep a thinker to suppose that the numbers of stamens and pistils generally expressed the real relationships of plants. Many of his classes were genuinely natural classes, but he chose the stamens and pistils as the general guide to the classes and orders because they were very plain and obvious marks.

Closely connected with classification is the process of abstraction. To abstract is to separate the qualities common to all the individuals of a group from the peculiarities of each individual. The notion "triangle" is the result of abstraction, to the extent that we can reason about triangles without paying any attention to the particular size or shape of any one triangle. All classification involves abstraction, because in forming and defining a class I must separate the common qualities from the peculiarities. When I abstract, I also form a general conception, that is, one that usually takes in many objects. But if the quality I abstract is a peculiar property of the class, one that belongs to the whole class and to no other objects, I may not increase the extent of the notion at all. So Mr. Herbert Spencer is perhaps right to hold that we can abstract without generalizing. We often use this word generalization, and the process can be defined as inferring of a whole class what we know only of a part of it. Whenever we regard the qualities of a thing as not confined to that thing alone but as extending to other objects, and in fact whenever we consider a thing only as a member of a class, we are said to generalize. If, after studying the properties of the circle, we go on to those of the ellipse, the parabola and the hyperbola, we soon find that the circle is just one case of a whole class of curves called the conic sections, which correspond to equations of the second degree. I generalize when I regard certain properties of the circle as shared by many other curves.

Dr. Whewell added to the oversupply of terms for the same processes when he introduced the expression Colligation of facts. Whenever two things are found to have similar properties, so that they are placed in the same class, they can be said to be connected together. We connect together the positions of a planet as it moves around the sun when we think of them as points on one and the same ellipse. Whenever we join together facts that were unconnected before, by means of a suitable general notion or hypothesis, we are said to colligate them. Dr. Whewell adds that the general conceptions we use must be (1) clear and (2) appropriate. But it is very doubtful whether there is anything in these processes really different from the general process of natural classification that we have been considering.

Questions and Exercises

1. Define classification, and give the derivation of the word.

2. What do you mean by important characters in classification?

3. State Dr. Whewell's criterion of a good natural arrangement.

4. Distinguish between a natural and an artificial system of classification.

5. What do you mean by a characteristic quality? Is it always an important quality?

6. Define abstraction, generalization, and colligation of facts.

7. What are the characters of a notion that has been properly abstracted?

Lesson 33: Requisites of a Philosophical Language

Among the supporting processes that inductive reasoning needs if it is to succeed, we must count the building of a suitable language. In fact, it is impossible to overstate how much any science depends on a language that is both accurate and rich. Studying things would be almost useless if we had no names to mark those things and to record what we have observed about them.

It is easy to see that language serves three distinct and almost independent purposes:

1. As a means of communication.

2. As a mechanical aid to thought.

3. As an instrument of record and reference.

When language first arose, it was used mainly, if not only, for the first purpose. In the 1870s, many of the world's peoples were thought to build up no store of knowledge at all. We may even say that the lower animals often have some way of communicating by sounds or natural signs, and this amounts to language in the first sense, even though they cannot reason with general notions.

Some philosophers have held that reasoning is impossible without language. The thoroughgoing nominalist went so far as to say that there are no such things as general notions at all, so that general names are the only thing that is general in science and reasoning. That is no doubt false (see Lesson 2). Still, we have to admit that if our general ideas were not fixed and represented by words, we could never manage the kind of sustained thought we now enjoy. For practical purposes, the second use of language is certainly indispensable, and reasoning can almost be thought of as the same thing as the correct use of words. When language is used only to help us reason, there is no need for each word to have a fixed meaning. We could use names the way algebra uses the letters x, y, z, a, b, r and so on, to stand for whatever quantity happens to turn up in a problem. All we need is never to confuse the meaning we give a word in one argument with a different meaning we give it in another. Algebra, in fact, can be described as a very perfect kind of language built for the second purpose alone, one that can lead a person to the solution of a problem in a symbolic, mechanical way.

Language as it comes to us ready-made, grown by habit over the centuries, can serve all three purposes, though by no means perfectly. Because words have a more or less fixed customary meaning, we can not only reason with them but also communicate our thoughts or record them. It is this last use, the recording, that we now have to deal with.

Building a science takes far more facts than anyone could hold in memory with enough accuracy. So one of the indispensable aids to induction is a means of describing and recording our observations. Only in this way can knowledge pile up, so that each observer starts with the advantage of knowing what has already been recorded and proved. We need, then, to look at the way language serves to register facts, and to work out what qualities a philosophical language must have to meet the needs of science.

As an instrument of record, language clearly needs two main things:

	1. Precision or definiteness of meaning.
	2. Completeness.

A name is worse than useless if, when it is used to record a fact, it does not let us find out what kind of fact was recorded. So accuracy and precision matter more in a language than abundance. Lacking the right word will seldom lead to actual error and fallacy. It will only force us to use a roundabout phrase, or else leave the fact unrecorded. But it is obviously convenient that whenever a thing, notion or quality has to be mentioned often, there should be a name set aside for it, and only one name. Let us look in turn at what a precise and complete language must be like.

The reader may not have noticed it before, but it is certainly true: we cannot describe anything without asserting that it resembles some other thing. We can only describe a thing by giving it a name. But how are we to learn what that name means? If we explain the name with other names, we only end up with more names whose meanings we need. In the end we must learn meanings, not from names, but from the things that carry those names. If someone did not know what blue means, there would be no way to tell them except by pointing to something that gave them the sensation of blueness. If they had been blind from birth, they could never get any notion of what blueness is. It is true that many words are so familiar to us from childhood that we cannot say when or how we learned what they mean, though we must have learned it by reference to things. But as soon as we start using names more precisely, we quickly have to refer to physical objects again. So we describe the various kinds of blue as sky-blue, azure-blue, indigo-blue and cobalt-blue, and we likewise tell apart sea-green, olive-green, emerald-green, grass-green and so on. In botany, the shapes of leaves are described with words like ovate, lanceolate, linear, pinnate and peltate, which call to mind, in order, an egg, a lance, a line, a feather and a shield. When we record sizes, we equally cannot avoid comparing them with the sizes of other things. A yard or a foot means nothing unless there is a definite standard yard or foot somewhere that fixes its meaning, and the reader probably knows that once the physical standard of a length is completely lost, it can never be recovered. The word is nothing unless somewhere we have the thing it stands for.

So the first requisite of a philosophical language is plainly that "every general name must have a certain and knowable meaning." It hardly needs saying that singular or proper names, the names of particular objects, must be known too. But such names are just marks stuck on the things they name, so they do not need the same attention. General names are harder, because, as we saw in Lesson 5, they have a double meaning: their denotation, or extension, and their connotation, or intension. Of these two, it is the connotation that must be fixed. The denotation, as a general rule, cannot be limited and pinned down. Suppose the name planet had been restricted to Jupiter, Saturn, Mars, Venus and Mercury, the planets known before the telescope was invented. Then we would have needed a new name for the ones discovered later, and even so we would be making the mistake of giving different names to things that are very much alike. But if by planet we mean any round body that revolves around the sun in a slightly elliptical orbit, the name will take in every such body that is discovered from time to time. In the 1870s more than 100 were already known, counting the small planets we now call asteroids. In the same way, locomotive engine is not just the name of a certain number of engines that exist right now. If it were, we would need a new name every week, as each new engine was built or an old one scrapped. What is fixed in a general name is its connotation, the qualities implied in the things that bear the name. So as far as possible we ought to define every general name we use, not by naming the objects it denotes, but by naming the qualities it connotes. We have already dealt with definition in earlier lessons (Lessons 12 and 13), so here we only need to ask how far it is better to use words already in everyday use rather than newly invented terms.

The advantage of an old term is that it carries meaning for everyone, and to that extent it saves people from having to learn a strange technical expression. Everyone knows what heat is, and the phrase "the science of heat" means something to every person, however unlearned. But old terms have one drawback we must note: they are almost always open to ambiguity. And in fact the scientist uses the word heat differently from everyone else. In science, all things are more or less hot, whereas in everyday life we could never say that ice was hot or contained heat. Ordinarily, heat means a temperature above the usual average, and the idea only makes sense in contrast with cold. We also stretch the word by analogy to sensations of taste, as when we say pepper is hot, and even to things purely in the mind, as in a hot dispute, a hot temper and so on. If we invent a new term, Caloric, to avoid these ambiguities, we can give it as precise a meaning as we like. But we put one more obstacle in the way of studying science, because there is one more technical term to learn.

This difficulty is especially great in the science of political economy (what we now call economics). There we deal with such familiar ideas as wealth, money, value, currency, capital, labor and exchange. It is the very familiarity of these ideas that causes the greatest trouble, because different people attach different meanings to the words, and the result is endless logomachy (from the Greek λόγος (logos), word, and μάχη (machē), battle), that is, disputes that arise over merely verbal questions. Even when a writer carefully defines the sense in which he uses these terms, he cannot make other people keep his definitions in mind. The other option, inventing entirely new terms, is out of the question, since it would certainly make a book unbearable for most readers. The only advice that can be given is this: bring in a new term where it is likely to be readily accepted and to push out an old, ambiguous one; otherwise, try to remove the ambiguity of the old term by always keeping a precise definition of the intended meaning in view.

A complete philosophical language will be made up of two distinct kinds of terms, which form, respectively, the descriptive terminology and the nomenclature of the science.

A descriptive terminology, as Dr. Whewell pointed out, must include all the terms needed to describe exactly what has been observed about any object or phenomenon, so that we have a permanent record of the observation. For every quality, shape, circumstance, degree or quantity there must be a suitable name or way of expressing it. So when we record the discovery of a new mineral, we ought to be able to pin down in words its exact crystal form, its color, its degree of hardness, its specific gravity, its smell and taste if it has any, and many other qualities that may turn out to matter. Modern botany grew out of the efforts of Linnaeus to create a system of terms with which every part and feature of a plant can be described accurately. The language of botany, improved since his time, is the most complete example of a scientific terminology. Geology, in my view, suffers badly from the difficulty of finding accurate terms. Names such as trap, basalt, gneiss, granite, tuff, greenstone, trachyte, porphyry, lava and so on are very vague, and there are no precise descriptive terms to define them and tell them apart. Where a quality does not come in degrees or quantities, it needs only a single name. Otherwise we must find some way of measuring it exactly and expressing the measurement. Inventing an instrument to measure a quality that was never measured before is always an important step in science, and the making of the thermometer by Fahrenheit and of the pendulum clock by Huygens were turning points in science.

On the other hand, each science needs a nomenclature, a collection of names for the distinct objects, or classes of objects, it deals with. In mineralogy the names of the separate minerals, such as haematite, topaz, amphibole, epidote, blende and polybasite, make up the nomenclature. In chemistry we have all the names of the elements, together with a vast apparatus of names for organic and other compounds, such as ethyl, acetyl, cyanogen, naphthalin, benzol and so on. In astronomy the names of the planets, satellites, nebulas, constellations and individual stars form a nomenclature, though by no means a perfect or convenient one. Geology likewise has a nomenclature that is necessarily incomplete, in the names of its successive formations: silurian, devonian, carboniferous, permian, triassic, eocene, miocene, pliocene, post-pliocene and so on.

Clearly a nomenclature must have names at various levels of generality. It must include individual objects, if they need to be recorded separately; the infimæ species, if there are any; and wider classes above them, all the way up to the summa genera, the widest notions the science covers. In astronomy we deal mostly with the names of individual objects, and so far there is little room for classification. In natural sciences such as botany or zoology there is seldom or never any need for names of individuals. That is because, in these sciences, a countless multitude of individuals generally resemble one another very closely in a great many properties, so that they make up what has been called a natural kind. Mr. J. S. Mill uses this term to mean "one of those classes which are distinguished from all others, not by one or a few definite properties, but by an unknown multitude of them; the combination of properties on which the class is grounded being a mere index to an indefinite number of other distinctive attributes."

Going by the way Mr. Mill puts it, he seems to include in a nomenclature only the names of supposed species. For he says: "A nomenclature may be defined, the collection of names of all kinds with which any branch of knowledge is conversant; or more properly, of all the lowest kinds, or infimæ species, those which may be subdivided indeed, but not into kinds, and which generally accord with what in natural history are termed simply species." But the fact is that naturalists have now given up the idea that a species is any fixed form. Many species are already divided into subspecies and varieties, or even varieties of varieties, and on the principles of Darwin's theory the dividing could go on without end. Surely the most reasonable view is that the natural kingdoms of plants and animals are arranged in an endless series of classes and subclasses, and that all the names attached to any of these classes belong to the nomenclature.

Again, Mill does not count as part of the nomenclature those general names that stand for ideas formed artificially in the course of induction and investigation. So, besides a terminology fit for describing the individual facts we observe with precision, there is, in his account, a branch of language containing "a name for every common property of any importance or interest, which we detect by comparing those facts: including (as the concretes corresponding to those abstract terms) names for the classes which we artificially construct in virtue of those properties, or as many of them, at least, as we have frequent occasion to predicate any thing of." As examples of this kind of name he gives Circle, Limit, Momentum, Civilization, Delegation and Representation. While the nomenclature contains the names of natural classes, this third branch of language would apparently contain the names of artificial ideas or classes.

But I find it very hard to give a clear account of Mill's views on this subject. And since my aim in these lessons does not allow me to discuss unsettled questions, I must end by referring any reader who wants to take the subject further to the 4th and 6th chapters of the 4th Book of Mr. Mill's System of Logic, which deal with the Requisites of a Philosophical Language.

See Dr. Whewell's "Aphorisms concerning the Language of Science," at the end of his Philosophy of the Inductive Sciences.

Thomson's Outline of the Laws of Thought contains very interesting remarks on the general nature and use of language, §§ 17–31.

Questions and Exercises

1. What are the three purposes for which we use language?

2. What are the two chief requisites of a philosophical language?

3. What should guide us when we choose between a new scientific term and an old one?

4. Explain the difference between a Descriptive Terminology and a Nomenclature. Then sort the following terms according to which of the two they belong to:

Rose, Rosaceae, Rose-like, Potassium, Alkaloid, Ruminant Animal, Ruminating, Ruby, Ruby-red.

5. What does Mill mean by the expression Natural Kind?

A Vocabulary of Logical Terms

Abscissio Infiniti (the cutting off of the infinite or negative part), the process by which we find the place of an object in a system of classes, by comparing it with one class after another and rejecting each class it does not belong to.

Accidental definition is a definition that gives the properties of a species, or the accidents of an individual; it is more commonly called a Description.

Analogue, a thing that is analogous to some other thing.

Analytics (τὰ Ἀναλυτικά), the title given in the second century to parts of the Organon, the logical treatises of Aristotle; they were divided into the Prior and the Posterior Analytics.

Analytic syllogism, a syllogism in which the conclusion is put first and the premises follow as the reasons for it. See Synthetic syllogism; the distinction is unimportant.

Antinomy (ἀντί, against; νόμος, law), the opposition of one law or rule to another. Kant.

Argument (Latin, argus, from ἀργός, clear, manifest), the process of reasoning: showing or proving what is doubtful by means of what is known. See Inference (Lesson 10). The middle term of a syllogism is sometimes called the argument in a special sense.

Argumentum a fortiori, an argument in which we prove that the case in question is even stronger or more probable than one already admitted to be strong or probable enough.

Argumentum ad judicium, an appeal to the common sense of humankind.

Argumentum ad ignorantiam, an argument that rests on the ignorance of one's opponents.

Argumentum ad verecundiam, an appeal to our respect for some great authority.

Argumentum ex concesso, a proof drawn from a proposition the other side has already granted.

Assertion (ad, to; sero, to join), a statement or proposition, whether affirmative or negative.

Association of ideas (associo, to accompany; socius, a companion), the natural connection in the mind between impressions that have been together before, or that are similar. Any idea tends to bring its associated ideas into the mind, following the two great laws of association: the Law of Contiguity and the Law of Similarity.

Assumption (assumo, to take for granted), any proposition taken as the basis of an argument; in a special sense, the minor premise of a categorical syllogism.

Attribute (attribuo, to give or ascribe to), a quality or circumstance that can be affirmed (or denied) of a thing; the opposite of Substance, which see.

Belief, assent to a proposition. It can have any degree of strength, from the slightest probability to the fullest certainty; see Probability.

Categories, the summa genera, the widest classes into which things can be sorted. There are ten of them: Οὐσία, Substance; Ποσόν, Quantity; Ποιόν, Quality; Πρός τι, Relation; Ποιεῖν, Action; Πάσχειν, Passion, or being acted on; Ποῦ, Place; Πότε, Time; Κεῖσθαι, Position; Ἔχειν, Habit or condition. Everything that can be affirmed must fall under one or another of these highest predicates. Aristotle described them in the first treatise of his Organon, called the Categories.

Cause, Aristotle distinguished four kinds of cause for the existence of a thing: 1. The Material Cause, the substance or matter it is made of; 2. The Formal Cause, the pattern, type or design it is shaped by; 3. The Efficient Cause, the force used in shaping it; 4. The Final Cause, the end, motive or purpose of the work.

Chance, ignorance of the causes that are at work; see Probability.

Cognition (cognosco, to know), knowledge, or the action of the mind in acquiring knowledge.

Colligation of Facts, Dr. Whewell's expression for binding facts together in the mind by some suitable conception.

Comparison (com, together; par, equal or like), the action of the mind by which we judge whether two objects of thought are the same or different in certain respects. See Judgment (Lesson 2).

Compatible terms are terms that are distinct but not contradictory, and so can be affirmed of the same subject, such as "large" and "heavy," or "bright-colored" and "nauseous."

Composition of Causes, the principle shown in every case where the joint effect of several causes is identical with the sum of their separate effects. J. S. Mill.

Concept, that which is conceived, the result of the act of conception; nearly the same as general notion, idea, thought.

Conception (con, together; capio, to take). An ambiguous term. Properly it means the action of the mind in which it takes several things together so as to form a general notion; or else the action in which it forms "a mental image of the several attributes given in any word or combination of words." Mansel.

Conjugate words, words that come from the same root or stock, such as known, knowing, knowingly, knowledge.

Consciousness, the immediate knowledge the mind has of its sensations and thoughts, and, in general, of everything it is doing at present. Reid.

Consectary = Corollary.

Consequence, the connection between antecedent and consequent; but often used, ambiguously, for the consequent itself.

Consilience of Inductions, the agreement of inductions drawn from different and independent series of facts, as when we learn that the earth moves by entirely different kinds of observation and reasoning. Whewell.

Contingent (contingo, to touch), that which may or may not happen; the opposite of both the necessary and the impossible.

Continuity, Law of, the principle that nothing can pass from one extreme to another without passing through all the degrees in between; motion, for instance, cannot be produced or destroyed in an instant.

Corollary, a proposition that follows directly from another that has been proved.

Criterion (κριτήριον, from κρίνω, to judge), any fact, rule, knowledge or means needed for forming a judgment that will settle a doubtful question.

Data (plural of datum, that which is given), the facts or assertions from which an inference is to be drawn.

De facto, what actually or really happens: the opposite of de jure, what ought to happen by law or right.

Demonstration (demonstro, to point out), strictly, pointing out the connection between premises and conclusion. The term is more generally used for any argument or reasoning regarded as proving the conclusion it asserts. A demonstration is either Direct or Indirect. In an indirect demonstration we prove the conclusion by disproving its contradictory, that is, by showing that the conclusion cannot be supposed untrue.

Determination, the marking off of parts of a genus by joining the genus and a difference together again. See Division (Lesson 12).

Dialectic (διαλεκτικὴ τέχνη, the art of discourse, from διαλέγεσθαι, to discourse). The original name of Logic, perhaps invented by Plato. It has also been used to mean the Logic of Probable Matter (Aristotle), the right use of Reason and Language, and the Science of Being, so it is a highly ambiguous term.

Disbelief, the state of mind in which we are fully convinced that some opinion is not true. J. S. Mill. It amounts to belief in the contradictory opinion or assertion, and must not be confused with Doubt, which see.

Doubt (dubito, to go two ways), the state of mind in which we hesitate between two or more inconsistent opinions. See Disbelief.

Drift of a proposition, the different meanings the same sentence can be given depending on where the stress falls. See Fallacy of accent (Lesson 20).

Empiricism (ἐμπειρία, experience), the doctrine of those who hold that all knowledge comes merely from experience.

Essence (essentia, from esse, to be), "the very being of anything, whereby it is what it is." Locke. It is an old scholastic word that cannot really be defined, and it should be dropped from use.

Evidence (e, and videre, to see), literally the seeing of anything. The word now means any facts taken in by the mind and made the grounds of knowledge and belief.

Experimentum crucis, an experiment that decides between two rival theories and shows which one to adopt, just as a signpost (a "finger-post") shows which of two roads to take.

Exposita, a proposition given to be worked on by some logical process.

Extremes of a proposition are its ends or terms, the subject and the predicate.

Fundamentum relationis, the ground of a relation, that is, the series of events or circumstances that sets up a relation between two correlative terms.

Galenian or 4th figure of the syllogism; see Lesson 17.

Homologue, whatever is homologous.

Homology, a special term for the analogy between parts of different plants and animals, such as between the wing of a bird and the foreleg of a four-footed animal, or between the scales of a fish and the feathers of a bird.

Idea (ἰδέα, εἶδος, image), a term used ambiguously, but usually equivalent to thought, notion, concept. Locke defines it as "Phantasm, notion, species, or whatever it is which the mind can be employed about in thinking." To have an idea of a thing is to think of that thing.

Idol (εἴδωλον, εἶδος, image), Bacon's figurative name for the sources of error. He listed four kinds: Idols of the Tribe, which affect all people; Idols of the Cave, which belong to one individual; Idols of the Forum, which arise from people's dealings with one another; and Idols of the Theatre, which come from the systems of philosophers.

Illation (illatum, past participle of infero, to bring in). See Inference (Lesson 10).

Illative, that which can be inferred.

Inconsistent terms imply qualities that cannot exist together in the same thing. See Compatible terms.

Indefinite or infinite term is a negative term, which marks out an object only by shutting it out of a class.

Individual, that which cannot be divided without losing its name and distinctive qualities, although it can usually be physically divided, or partitioned; see Partition (Lesson 12).

Intention, first and second, a distinction between terms, defined by Hobbes like this:—"Of the first intention are the names of things, a man, stone, &c.; of the second are the names of names, and speeches, as universal, particular, genus, species, syllogism, and the like." A term of the second intention expresses the way the mind regards or classifies terms of the first intention.

Lemma (λαμβάνω, to take or assume), a proposition, a premise that is granted; in geometry, a preliminary proposition.

Matter is defined by J. S. Mill as "the external cause to which we ascribe our sensations," or as the Permanent Possibility of Sensation.

Membra dividentia, the parts into which a class is divided; the species that make up a genus.

Metaphysics (τὰ μετὰ τὰ Φυσικά), the works of Aristotle that came after his Physics, or were studied after it. Hence First Philosophy, the so-called science of things in their own nature; ontology, or the science of Being.

Method (μέθοδος, μετά and ὁδός, way), a mode, way or instrument for reaching an end.

Metonymy (μετά, and ὄνομα, name), the grammatical name for transferring the meaning of a word to a closely connected thing, as when we say the church and mean the people in it. See Transfer of meaning (Lesson 4).

Necessity (ne, not; and cesso, to cease), that which always is and cannot help but be.

Notion (nosco, to know), the action of grasping or taking note of the various qualities of an object; or, more commonly, the result of that action. See Idea, Concept.

Objective, that which belongs to the object of thought, the non-ego (the not-self); the opposite of Subjective, which see.

Obversion is the name Professor Alexander Bain uses for the process previously called Immediate Inference by Privative Conception (Lesson 10).

Organon (ὄργανον, Latin Organum, Instrument), a name for Aristotle's logical treatises, first in general use in the 15th century. It implies that they can be regarded as an instrument to help the mind. Bacon adopted the name for his Novum Organum.

Paradox (παρά, δόξα, contrary to opinion), an assertion that goes against common opinion, and which may or may not turn out to be true; often wrongly used to mean something self-contradictory and absurd.

Paralogism (παραλογίζομαι, to reason wrongly), a purely logical fallacy, that is, a breach of the rules of deductive logic.

Parity of reasoning, an expression meaning that once one case has been demonstrated, other similar cases can be demonstrated by the same line of reasoning.

Peripatetic Philosophy (περιπατέω, to walk about), the name usually given to the doctrines of Aristotle and his followers, who are said to have carried on their studies and discussions while walking about the halls and walkways of the Lyceum.

Physical definition gives the parts into which a thing can be separated by partition, or physical division.

Polylemma, an argument with the same form as a dilemma, but with more than two alternatives.

Postulate (postulatum, a thing demanded), a proposition that has to be demanded as a basis for argument; in geometry, the postulates lay down the practical conditions required.

Predicaments (prædicamenta, what can be predicated), see Categories.

Principle (principium, beginning), the first source of anything; sometimes used specially to mean the major premise of a syllogism.

Probability, quantity or degree of belief, or, more truly, quantity of information about an uncertain event. It is measured by the ratio of the number of cases favorable to the event to the total number of possible cases.

Problem (πρόβλημα, that which is thrown down), an assertion put forward to be proved or disproved.

Proof, giving a reason or argument in support of a given proposition.

Ratiocination, a name equivalent to Syllogism or Deduction, adopted by J. S. Mill.

Reason (ratio, from reor, to think), a term with a wide and ambiguous meaning; it has sometimes been used specially for the minor premise of a syllogism.

Reductio ad absurdum or ad impossibile, an indirect demonstration based on the impossibility of a contradictory supposition.

Relation (relatum, past participle of refero, to bear back), any connection, in thought or in fact, between two things.

Scholastic Philosophy, a general name for the systems of philosophy taught in the Middle Ages, from the 9th to the 16th century, at their height chiefly in the 13th and 14th centuries. The subject was mainly the logic of Aristotle, mixed with theology, metaphysics, grammar or rhetoric.

Secundi adjacentis of the second adjacent, an expression in incorrect Latin, applied to a grammatical sentence or proposition that has only two parts, the subject and the verb, with no separate copula.

Significates of a term are the things it denotes or signifies.

Sophism (σόφισμα, from σοφία, wisdom), a false argument; the name often implies that the false argument is being used knowingly, to deceive.

Subjective, that which belongs to the thinking subject, the ego, or the mind engaged in thought; the opposite of Objective, which see.

Substance (sub, under; stans from stare, to stand), that which underlies and supports phenomena or attributes. Strictly speaking it is either mind or matter, but it is more commonly used in the material sense.

Subsumption (sub, under; sumo, to take or put), Sir William Hamilton's name for the minor premise of a syllogism, because it brings, or subsumes, a special case under the rule stated in the major premise, or sumption.

Subsumption of a law is Mr. Mill's expression for the third way of explaining a law: by showing it to be a particular case of a more general law.

Sumption (sumo, to take), Hamilton's name for the major premise of a syllogism.

Synthetic syllogism, a syllogism in which the conclusion comes last; see Analytic syllogism.

System (σύστημα, from συνίστημι, to put together), a connected body of knowledge.

Tertii adjacentis of the third adjacent, an expression in incorrect Latin, applied to a grammatical sentence or proposition in which the subject, copula and predicate are all stated separately.

Theory (θεωρία, contemplation), knowledge of principles, as opposed to practice; used ambiguously.

Thesis (θέσις, from τίθημι, to place), an assertion or proposition put forward to be proved or supported by arguments.

Totum divisum, a class or notion that is divided into parts by a difference.

Trilemma, an argument like a dilemma, but with three alternatives.

Truth, the agreement of our knowledge with the things known.
